Knights lose game, Stone in 4-1 defeat to Sharks

📅 Nov 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

There's a statistic that points out that 77 percent of NHL teams that are in the playoffs on Thanksgiving Day make the postseason come April.Try telling that to the San Jose Sharks.As of Thursday, the Sharks were on the outside looking in. Friday, they looked every bit a playoff team, riding the great play of goaltender Yaroslav Askarov to get a much-needed 4-1 win over the Vegas Golden Knights at the SAP Center. It was the first time the Sharks had beaten the Knights in regulation during the regular season in San Jose. Vegas held a 16-0-3 edge over nine seasons.Of bigger concern to the Knights is the health of their captain, Mark Stone. He left the game late in the second period, returned briefly for the third, taking just one shift before returning to the locker room midway through the final period. His status for Saturday afternoon's game against Montreal at T-Mobile Arena is unknown.Coach Ryan Craig did not have an update on Stone following the game."He's being evaluated," Craig said.Askarov was sensational, making key stops throughout the contest. He denied Victor Olofsson in the first period, stopped Tomas Hertl in close in the second and Olofsson again in the third. Hertl, the former Shark, was playing in his 900th NHL game Friday.Jack Eichel was the lone Vegas player to get the puck past Askarov, scoring on the power play which cut the deficit to 2-1. It was the sixth straight game Eichel had registered a point. It appears his slump of a couple of weeks ago is a thing of the past.But the Knights were unable to draw even. And when Zack Ostapchuk scored off the rush to beat Carter Hart and make it 3-1, the Sharks finally had a win over the Knights in their home rink in regulation.It was a huge bounce back for San Jose, which had been shut out 7-0 in Anaheim Wednesday night. Michael Mina's goal off a rebound of Alexander Wennberg's shot beat Hart with 4:09 left in the second to open the scoring for San Jose. Adam Gaudette made it 2-0 5:22 into the third as the hill got a little steeper for the Knights.And with Askarov holding down the fort, stopping 27 of the 28 he faced, the Sharks were able to put their poor performance against the Ducks behind them as Macklin Celebrini sealed things with an empty net goal with 2:11 remaining as the Sharks have leaped over Calgary into the third spot in the Pacific Division. So if the playoffs started today, San Jose would be in.For second-place Vegas, it snapped a two-game road win streak and kept the Knights three points behind first-place Edmonton in the Pacific. Saturday, they'll face a Canadiens squad which has dropped three straight, is dealing with injuries, but has the services of veteran center Dylan Larkin."We have to continue to play the way it got us those chances," Craig said of the early opportunities that didn't go in. "When the goaltender has a good period to start, we have to find a way to stay with our game, continue to push and have some patience."I thought we got away from managing the puck. They were a team that came ready to play. We talked about following up the win (in Edmonton). Our guys were ready. We had some opportunities in the first period. It's a matter of staying with it."

Are Ultra-Processed Foods Raising Your Risk of Stroke and Obesity?

📅 Nov 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

High intakes of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) have been linked to multiple adverse health outcomes, including an increased risk of stroke, heart disease, cancer, and obesity.

A recent study published in JAMA Open Network showed that a higher intake of UPFs may be linked to brain damage and could lead to an increased risk of stroke and cognitive decline later in life.

Another large study in Family Medicine and Community Health found that people who consumed the most UPFs were at a higher risk of developing chronic diseases, such as diabetes, obesity, heart disease, and cancer.

An earlier review in Nature Metabolism also showed that fructose in UPFs may contribute to obesity and metabolic diseases.

Together, these studies add to research on the effects of eating high levels of UPFs on your health.

A recent study published in JAMA Network Open found that healthy adults who eat higher levels of UPFs may be more likely to experience damage to their brain that could increase the risk of stroke and future memory problems.

The cross-sectional study analyzed 8,783 adults between the ages of 35 and 69 without cardiovascular disease. The researchers used MRI scans to evaluate participants’ brains and measured UPF intake using a food frequency questionnaire.

UPFs accounted for an average of 25% of participants’ diet. However, among those with the highest intake, these foods accounted for 45% of their daily intake, compared with 16% among those with the lowest intake.

When comparing the highest intake to the lowest, the researchers found that those in the higher group had 1.7% more body fat and had a higher risk of vascular brain injury (VBI).

The findings suggest that there may be a link between high UPF intake and brain changes that could increase the risk of stroke and cognitive decline.

“These findings are concerning, but it is important to remember that an association does not prove cause and effect,” Michelle Routhenstein, a preventive cardiology dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline in a previous interview.

“While we can’t conclude from this study that limiting UPFs would improve brain health, we have decades of literature demonstrating that higher intakes of green leafy vegetables, brightly colored fruits, whole grains, and lean protein sources are good for brain health,” Suzanne Judd, PhD, director of the UC College of Medicine’s Center for Public Health, who wasn’t involved in the study, previously told Healthline.

Routhenstein advised starting simply with one manageable swap to help lower your UPF intake.

“I want people to feel empowered, not fearful, when making food choices,” she said. “Healthy eating does not mean having to cook everything from scratch. What matters most is building consistent, nourishing habits that support both heart and brain health.”

A large recent study published in Family Medicine and Community Health found that people who ate the most UPFs were more likely to develop chronic diseases, such as:

It also found that these individuals may have a higher overall risk of death.

The research team analyzed 51 studies involving nearly 9 million participants from around the world, with follow-up periods ranging from 2 to 32 years.

They found that consuming high levels of UPFs was associated with an increased risk of many chronic health issues.

Those with the highest levels of UPF consumption showed a:

These numbers were derived by comparing people who ate the most UPFs with those who ate the least.

Johannah Katz, MA, a registered dietitian at Nourish, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline in a previous interview that there isn’t currently a cutoff point at which ultra-processed foods become harmful.

“What this new research suggests is more of a dose-response relationship; the more ultra-processed food in the diet, the higher the observed risk for several chronic diseases,” she said.

Reducing UPF intake may help decrease these risks.

A recent review published in Nature Metabolism highlighted the role fructose can play in driving disease, distinct from its role as a source of calories.

Fructose is a simple sugar that is found both naturally, in fruits and honey, and in sweeteners like high fructose corn syrup and sucrose (table sugar).

One reason that fructose can be problematic is that it has twice the sweetness of glucose, which can lead to increased cravings for more fructose-containing foods and drinks.

“Fructose found in these processed forms is not handled the same way as regular blood sugar,” Routhestein, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline in a previous interview. “When it is consumed, it goes to the liver, where it easily turns into fat, which can build up in the liver and around the organs and contribute to conditions like fatty liver and metabolic syndrome.”

This recent review highlights how fructose metabolism bypasses key steps in energy-processing pathways. This can lead to various effects, such as:

“The emerging body of research suggests that fructose is not metabolically identical to all other carbohydrates, especially when it is consumed in excess through sugar-sweetened beverages and ultra-processed foods,” Serena Poon, certified nutritionist & longevity wellness advisor, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline in a previous interview.

“Excess calories still matter, but fructose may affect metabolism differently than other sugars, particularly in the liver … In my view, this supports a more nuanced conversation because it is not just about sugar in the abstract, but also about the form, dose, and dietary context in which fructose is consumed,” she said.

How to get adrenaline in GTA 6

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Adrenaline acts as an emergency boost to all your stats in Grand Theft Auto 6, as it temporarily negates health, stamina, and focus loss. It's meant to get you out of an emergency situation safely or help you burst into a tense situation and mop it up fast. Unlike Zombix and other restoratives, though, you can't buy adrenaline — not from anywhere we've found so far, at least — nor can you expect to find it in abundance throughout Leonida. There is, however, a small set of locations you can reliably get adrenaline from.

Below, we explain how to get adrenaline in GTA 6.

You'll normally find at least one shot of adrenaline where you need it most — illegal operations, which have yellow marked on the map. That's true for the big ones, like the bowling alley the Kolt group operates out of, and the small ones, like the meth labs and grow houses operating out the back of gas stations. Basically, any convenience stores, gas stations, bars, and so on that lets you explore more than just the customer area nearly always has a trauma kit or first-aid kid in a staff area, bathroom, or kitchen. These will always have Zombix, but there's also a chance that they'll contain one shot of adrenaline. The kits might be lying around on a countertop, or they may be little cabinets nailed to the wall. If they're on the wall, you'll have to break them open, which takes a few seconds. Bear that in mind if you've got enemies close behind.

This is not to be confused with the other robbery opportunities that have green markers or stick ups that have gray markers. These areas can have adrenaline, but they're a bit rarer.

There's also a chance to find adrenaline in the back of ambulances, either loose or in a trauma kit. Getting it this way is pretty tough until you reach Chapter 3 and can explore Vice City more freely, though. The city hospital always has a few ambulances around back in the parking lot. They aren't very well attended, so you can break in with comparative ease. They are alarmed, so make sure to slim jim the door first, and then open the back to avoid setting off the alarm.

You might see ambulances driving along the roads of Leonida or the streets of Vice City. If you're really desperate for adrenaline, you can stop them and break into the back. This is a pretty big and unnecessary risk to take if you're trying to keep a low profile, though.

Lucia and Jason can only hold three adrenaline apiece, so you can't stock up on them and let loose in a particularly tough mission. If you want to have it on-hand before taking on a dangerous job, instead of scrambling to find the location's bathroom when the bullets start flying, your best bet is to swipe it from an ambulance before setting out to work.

Just make sure to keep an eye on the stat bars in the upper left corner of the screen. They turn yellow when you use adrenaline, and the yellow gradually fades back to the stats' normal colors as it wears off. Consider it your action timer. By the time it's almost completely run out, you should be finding cover or fleeing for safety.

If you're just getting started in GTA 6, we have plenty of guides to help you live the high life in Leonida. Check out our GTA 6 beginner guide to get a grasp of the basics and our tips for what to do first, recommendations for the best ways to make money fast, and an explainer for how to keep stolen weapons and cars to make the most of your free time.

Our selection of GTA 6 guides includes help for things like how to steal the Phoenix in "Unwanted Attention" and whether you should defuse Mandy, too. There's a set of explainers for GTA 6's more open-ended side activities, like how to rob the Exotic Animal Front and where to find tree effigies for the Skunk Ape mission, and we've got all the GTA 6 cheat codes. You'll have to put in some effort before you can use those, though.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

5 Things We’re Saying No to This Holiday Season

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 25, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Before you say yes this holiday season, ask yourself what you’re saying yes to. Consider the feelings that come up for you when you think about it. Is it excitement about seeing the people you love? Is it dread at the anticipation of planning, conflict, or grief? For some, it’s all of the above.

Recent polls find that nearly 9 in 10 (89%) U.S. adults report holiday stressors (APA, 2023). Tensions arise due to finances, family conflict, grief, and other factors. Stress takes away from the pleasurable aspects of the season, which should include reconnection and rest.

Sometimes that is easier said than done. Here are 5 things we're saying no to this holiday season and strategies to achieve the happy holidays we deserve.

Sometimes that family holiday announcement sounds like a demand rather than an invitation. “Dinner is at 4 pm sharp. See you there!” Everyone assumes you’ll be there, and no one asks questions. They don’t ask if you can take time off work, afford the trip, or have other plans. You feel obligated to attend despite any barriers you are facing.

Almost no one will tell you that you are allowed to consider your own needs before you RSVP yes. Ask yourself whether you have the time, energy, and money to participate this year. If the answer is no, it’s alright. Your family may be disappointed, and you may feel a little guilty, but it’s worth asking which will weigh on you more: the guilt of saying no or the cost of saying yes.

In toxic families, it’s the once-over as soon as you enter the room. Before you’ve even had a chance to sit down, someone has made a joke about your weight or graying hair, offered a backhanded compliment about the dish you contributed, or slipped in a dig about your personal life.

You may feel hurt, sad, or even angry, and you don’t have to ignore those feelings to preserve the holiday spirit. You can be silent and stone-faced to let the “joke” fall flat. You might excuse yourself and regroup in another room. If you want to address it directly and call out the hidden meaning, you can say, “That felt like a dig,” or ask, “What do you mean by that?” What you don’t have to do is tolerate disrespect.

When you want everyone to feel loved, it’s easy to slip into spending more than you intended. If you’ve ever hosted a large family gathering, you know how quickly the groceries and gifts add up. And if you’re the one who hosts every year, it can feel like the work and the bill are yours by default.

Before you do anything, set a budget. Then share the work in ways that play into everyone’s strengths. Maybe enlist the nephew who can roast meat or the cousin who loves to bake, and assign cleanup to the one you prefer to stay out of the kitchen. Suggest rotating hosting duties. Do a gift exchange rather than buying individual gifts for everyone. You can show your love to your family with limits.

In some families, playing the dutiful daughter (or son) means everyone assumes you’ll make the holidays happen. Often it’s the family matriarch or the eldest daughter who initiates the first call about the holiday plans and is the one still in the kitchen long after everyone else has gone home. She is the family “glue” who keeps track of the invites, remembers who can’t eat what, and figures out where to seat the relatives who aren’t speaking. By the time the holiday arrives, she’s too tired to enjoy the fruits of her labor.

If that sounds like you, name it. People who have never hosted may not realize all of the hidden effort that goes into an event. Bring it to their attention. Set limits on what you will do and ask for help, but don’t micromanage. If the person assigned to bring the rolls forgets, skip the rolls this year. If no one steps up, step back anyway. Plan a smaller, more intimate gathering that won’t leave you frazzled at the end of it.

The holidays aren’t the same after you’ve lost a loved one, and the first holiday season after a loss can be especially difficult. Family traditions may be upended. The person who always hosted and made everyone’s favorite dish is gone. Tensions that surface after the loss may still be hanging in the air.

Take a moment to evaluate your feelings. Ask yourself what you feel up to this year. Maybe it’s not the year to take on hosting duties, or perhaps you stop by for dessert rather than staying the entire day. You can choose to honor your loved one by retaining old traditions or step into the next chapter with new traditions in their memory. You can miss someone deeply and still make room for moments of joy this holiday season.

It might seem strange that saying no could be the start of a happy holiday. The beauty is that you get to decide what a happy holiday looks like for you, and that it can change from year to year. Ask yourself what would make the holidays feel good to you this year and do it.

American Psychological Association. (2023). Even a joyous holiday season can cause stress for most Americans. https://www.apa.org/news/press/releases/2023/11/holiday-season-stress

How to Maneuver Generational Differences Over the Holidays

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 25, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

With the holidays yet again upon us, people will be setting their holiday gathering plans and preparing for an onslaught of multi-generational togetherness. When coming together for the holidays, the goal isn’t necessarily for everyone to think or celebrate alike; it’s to celebrate harmony and happiness while making room for the differences that you know may come with getting so many people of varying ages together in one room.

Here, we discuss how to make room at one’s table for generational differences while protecting the deep thread of connection that makes everyone family.

We’ve all experienced it. The sideways glances at the child who won’t eat their turkey. The sighs and stories about “Back in my day…” The admonishment to the teenager to “put your phone away.” The mom in the middle of it all who just wants a group photo of everyone before they dig in.

These are the sights and sounds of most holiday family gatherings. The younger generation has taken over the planning and organization of events, while the older generation sits in the wake of it all, wondering why anything had to change.

Even though the holidays bring together people who love one another, they can also bring friction. According to a 2023 American Psychological Association poll, 89% of U.S. adults reported holiday-related stress, and 22% identified the experience or anticipation of family conflict as a source of that stress.

However, generational differences don’t have to derail a holiday gathering. Once we learn to stop anticipating conflict and start remembering the reason we get together in the first place, we can begin looking forward to the fun stories, skill sharing, and memory-making that make the holidays so special.

It can be easy to get immediately annoyed when Grandpa starts talking about how they used to discipline kids or Great Aunt Margaret says that your stuffing was made incorrectly. Try to reframe statements before irritation or misunderstandings get out of control.

For example, instead of getting bothered when an older member of the family admonishes a child for being on their device, think about why they may be mentioning it. Perhaps they want to spend more time talking with the child, or maybe there are some cultural expectations around the holidays at play.

Researchers found that interactions and education can reduce or even eliminate ageism, which can be at play during multi-generational events. By teaching the younger generation about aging and helping them connect with older people, you can eliminate miscommunication, fear, and negative feelings.

Laying out expectations and boundaries can also be an effective way to help the holiday get-together go more smoothly between the generations. Prior to the event, invite the older generations into a conversation about plans. This way, there will be less chance of surprise or disappointment, and the older generation family members will feel like they are contributing.

Take care to ask about meaningful traditions in the family and choose a few to carry on while leaving room for something new. Discuss important topics like gift budgets, transportation, locations, and food options before the holidays so that there is less room for conflict.

Boundaries can sometimes sound like a dirty word to older generations, but they can still be important for intergenerational harmony during the holidays. First, distinguish between what you are willing to compromise on to keep the peace and a personal boundary that absolutely must be respected.

For example, letting Grandma tell stories about her childhood on the farm may not necessarily hurt anyone, but comments about your body, children declining a hug from a relative, or politics may be in need of some boundary-setting intervention. If boundaries that are important to you are repeatedly ignored, have a place you can go to take a break or a code word with your partner to signal that you need some help getting out of a conversation.

When you get different generations of family together for the holiday, there is bound to be some friction. We live in increasingly divided times, and people may feel more comfortable around their family letting conflicting opinions fly. Holiday hosts should aim to connect the generations instead of steeling themselves for inevitable discourse.

The ultimate goal isn’t to resolve all differences and agree every step of the way (that’s likely impossible). Instead, families should make room for stories, laughter, and the kind of connection that’s only possible between people who know one another as well as family members do.

1. “Even a joyous holiday season can cause stress for most Americans”. (2023, November 30). American Psychological Association. Retrieved November 2, 2026, from https://www.apa.org/news/press/releases/2023/11/holiday-season-stress.

2. Burnes, D., Sheppard, C., Henderson, C. R., Wassel, M., Cope, R., Barber, C., & Pillemer, K. (2019). Interventions to reduce ageism against older adults: A systematic review and meta-analysis. American Journal of Public Health, 109(8). https://doi.org/10.2105/ajph.2019.305123

Antihypertensives Still Worth It for the Healthiest 10%, Large Study Suggests

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— Drop in major cardiovascular events evident across spectrum of predicted risk

Across hypertensive individuals with varying predicted cardiovascular risk, blood pressure (BP) reduction was associated with a lower occurrence of real major cardiovascular events, according to a meta-analysis.

Using individual participant data from randomized trials, investigators found that major cardiovascular events were significantly less likely with every 5-mm Hg reduction in systolic BP from pharmacotherapy over 5 years (11.4% vs 13.0% with placebo or other control; HR 0.90, 95% CI 0.88-0.92).

This arguably covered people across the whole spectrum of cardiovascular health, as indicated by a study-specific risk model that put people with 5-year predicted risk <4% in the lowest decile and those with estimated risk >27% in the highest decile, reported Kazem Rahimi, DM, MSc, of the University of Oxford in England, and colleagues in the New England Journal of Medicine.

"BP lowering was associated with a decreased risk of major cardiovascular events across the risk spectrum, including a predicted 5-year risk below 4%," the researchers concluded.

The study adds to the limited literature on BP-lowering treatment in low-risk populations. Without better evidence, guideline recommendations for BP reduction in these individuals had been based on the assumption that the observed benefits of BP-lowering therapy in higher-risk individuals may be extrapolated to peers at lower risk.

"Many of the hypertensive trials, such as the SPRINT trial, have noted cardiovascular risk reduction in a high-risk group of patients with antihypertensive therapy. This meta-analysis shows that antihypertensive therapy reduces cardiovascular events across the risk spectrum and notably low-risk patients have a similar relative risk reduction as higher-risk patients," commented Matthew Sorrentino, MD, of University of Chicago Medicine.

Nevertheless, Rahimi's team found that the greatest absolute reduction in cardiovascular events occurred in the highest-risk group. The number needed to treat to prevent one major cardiovascular event over 5 years was 38 in the highest decile of predicted risk versus 255 in the lowest decile.

This is to be expected, according to Sorrentino. "The number needed to treat in a low-risk population will be significantly higher than in a high-risk cohort because of a lower incidence of events in the low-risk group, but the important finding is that risk reduction can be demonstrated in the low-risk cohort."

"Antihypertensive therapy will benefit anyone with consistently elevated BP regardless of calculated risk," he stressed.

Sorrentino said that the study supports the American Heart Association and the American College of Cardiology guideline on BP treatment, which does not suggest holding treatment in lower-risk individuals unable to achieve BP target with a lifestyle program.

Starting in 2025, the American groups said that low-risk individuals with an estimated 10-year cardiovascular disease (CVD) risk <7.5% (based on the PREVENT-CVD calculator) should initiate antihypertensives if average systolic BP remains at least 130 mm Hg (or average diastolic BP remains at least 80 mm Hg) after 3-6 months of lifestyle intervention, a class I recommendation.

Of note, Rahimi's team had an internal risk estimator for the meta-analysis, not PREVENT or another calculator used in clinical practice.

"This study is helpful to help us understand how BP lowering can be important even in lower-risk groups. While not all risk calculators are comparable, the overarching message is the same," maintained Sadiya Khan, MD, MSc, of Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine in Chicago.

"The key thing we know is that treating BP may be one of the most important things we can do to prevent cardiovascular disease even if someone may be at lower risk," she told MedPage Today. "If they are at higher risk, they will get much more absolute benefit from the treatment, so for individuals who have already had a heart attack or stroke, this could not be more important."

For their meta-analysis, Rahimi and colleagues relied on individual participant data from 51 randomized trials comparing antihypertensive treatment with placebo, an active comparator, or usual care.

The 357,440-person cohort had a mean age of 65 years and was 42% women. Mean baseline BP was 152.4/87.3 mm Hg, and the median predicted 5-year risk of a major cardiovascular event was 12.3%.

The primary outcome of interest was a first major cardiovascular event (i.e., stroke, ischemic heart disease, or heart failure resulting in death or hospitalization). Studies reached a median follow-up of 4.2 years in this report.

As for safety, the investigators opted for a narrower evidence base of 19 trials that compared antihypertensive treatment with placebo or usual care. The three prespecified safety outcomes consistently trended higher with the former:

Patients of all predicted risk levels shared an increase in the risk of safety events with antihypertensive treatment.

"Because the absolute reduction in the 5-year cumulative incidence of a major cardiovascular event in lower deciles was smaller than in higher deciles and the relative risk of adverse events was similar across the risk spectrum, the balance between benefit and harm narrowed as the predicted risk decreased and is unlikely to be captured by any single risk or BP threshold," Rahimi and colleagues noted.

"Quantifying this balance would require methods that account for differences in the severity, duration, and irreversibility of the outcomes concerned, which was beyond the scope of this analysis," they cautioned.

Rahimi's group also acknowledged that event counts under trial conditions may differ from those in routine practice, where the duration of treatment is often shorter and typically characterized by lower adherence and less protocolized care.

Adam Schefter shares details of how serious Puka Nacua's knee injury was

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Puka Nacua suffered a knee injury on the final offensive snap of Week 9 against the Washington Commanders, an injury that has now sidelined him for the rest of the year. Initially, the Rams expected him back in mid-December after placing him on injured reserve, but Sean McVay revealed last week that Nacua decided to have surgery on his MCL and will be out for the year.

Based on the details of the injury, it's not hard to understand why Nacua opted for the surgical route. Adam Schefter of ESPN revealed how severe Nacua's knee injury was while discussing the receiver's future on "The Adam Schefter Podcast" Tuesday. Schefter said Nacua tore his MCL completely off the bone and also had a posterior oblique tear, which were not going to "heal with the stability that they needed."

Had Nacua opted not to have surgery, there would've been a "higher chance of earlier degenerative changes," which would be bad for his long-term health.

"In this particular surgery, he had a Grade 3 MCL tear completely torn off the tibia, which is the shin bone," Schefter said. "Completely torn off the shin bone. And he had a tear of the posterior oblique portion of the MCL, as well. So, that wouldn't heal with the stability that they needed. And essentially, it would be performance-limiting with a higher chance of earlier degenerative changes. So, they had to go in and repair the knee."

As for Nacua's future, Schefter said he thinks the Rams "want to keep him" and he also thinks "they plan to keep him," but he can't say for certain that Nacua will remain in Los Angeles after this season because he's set to be an unrestricted free agent.

He's expected to miss the next 4-5 months after having surgery last week, but he's still on track to be ready for the start of the 2027 season, regardless of which team he's playing for. There's almost no scenario where the Rams don't at least tag Nacua and attempt to trade him because simply letting him walk in free agency would only net them a third-round compensatory pick in the 2028 draft.

Stomach problems in autistic children raise new questions about gut bacteria

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A study found that autistic children with more severe digestive symptoms tended to have more difficulty picking up on social cues.

Children with autism often have stomach problems. What causes them, however, is still up for debate. Some scientists believe the lining of the gut becomes damaged or inflamed.

Little evidence for that explanation was found in a new study.

Autistic children with more severe digestive symptoms tended to have more difficulty picking up on social cues, according to parent questionnaires.

The researchers also found links between certain gut bacteria and digestive symptoms.

The study was led by J. Marc Rhoads. He is professor and chief of the Division of Gastroenterology, Hepatology, and Nutrition at McGovern Medical School at UTHealth Houston.

Many children with autism are unable to explain where they feel pain. In such cases a change in behavior may be the first sign of a problem.

“Families may notice increased irritability, repetitive behaviors, or self-injurious behaviors when a child is experiencing gastrointestinal issues such as gas, constipation, or diarrhea,” Rhoads said.

“For children who are nonverbal, these behaviors may be one of the few ways they can communicate discomfort.”

About one in 31 American eight-year-olds had been identified with autism in 2022. This estimate was published by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention.

Significant digestive problems have been reported in 31% to 84% of people with autism in earlier studies.

The researchers examined 72 children with autism who were between four and 16 years of age. All of the children had been enrolled in a probiotic trial. Their data were collected between 2016 and 2023.

Boys made up about 78% of the group and roughly a quarter of the children did not speak. Children with known digestive, liver or kidney diseases were excluded from the study. Those who had recently taken antibiotics or probiotics were also left out.

Several symptoms were assessed by the team. These included constipation, gas, stomach pain, unusually smelly stools and waking up at night.

A score of seven or more on a 17-point scale was taken to indicate significant digestive problems. About 63% of the children were placed in this group.

Parents were also asked to complete a questionnaire about the social behavior of their children. Higher scores on this questionnaire reflected more pronounced autism traits.

Higher scores were generally seen in children with worse stomach problems. The connection was not particularly strong but it reached statistical significance.

The clearest difference was observed in social awareness. This area covers abilities such as noticing the facial expressions, feelings or actions of another person.

Children with significant digestive problems scored about 7.5 points higher in this area. Their scores were compared with those of children who had milder symptoms.

The cells that line the intestines are packed closely together. The spaces between them are sealed by specialized proteins.

Substances from inside the gut may pass into the bloodstream if these seals are weakened. Inflammation could then be triggered in the body. This process is commonly described as a leaky gut.

Blood samples were tested for two proteins associated with these seals. Another blood protein linked to intestinal inflammation was measured as well. Stool samples were also analyzed for calprotectin, a protein routinely used by doctors to detect gut inflammation.

Most of the results fell within the normal range. Elevated calprotectin was detected in only two children and neither of them had significant digestive symptoms.

“Biomarkers of intestinal inflammation and gut permeability remained within normal ranges and were not associated with gastrointestinal symptom severity or autism symptom severity,” Rhoads said.

Children with worse stomach problems therefore did not show more signs of inflammation or a leaky gut on these tests.

Mixed results have been produced by earlier research. High calprotectin levels were found in up to a third of autistic children in some studies. No significant difference was found in a more recent review.

The bacterial findings proved to be more revealing than the blood tests.

Additional stool samples were obtained from Texas Children’s Microbiome Center. This allowed the analysis to be expanded to 135 children. The group included children with autism, their siblings and unrelated children without autism.

Fewer types of gut bacteria were present in children with autism than in their siblings. A similar difference was seen when they were compared with unrelated children. However, this second result may have arisen by chance.

The bacterial communities of children with autism also resembled one another more closely. Greater variety was observed among the other children.

Two groups of bacteria were more abundant in autistic children with significant digestive problems. One of these groups is known as Ruminococcus.

Children with higher levels of these bacteria reported more gas and stronger-smelling stools. Night waking was also more frequent in these children. Their overall digestive symptom scores were higher as well.

Ruminococcus feeds on the mucus that protects the intestinal wall. Higher levels of this bacterium in one-year-olds were linked to greater social difficulties at age three in an earlier study.

Another bacterial group called Sarcina was associated with gas, stomach pain, diarrhea and night waking. The researchers noted that Sarcina had not been linked to autism in previous studies.

The study captured a snapshot of digestive symptoms, gut bacteria and behavior.

The children were examined at a single point in time. It was therefore not possible to tell which change came first.

Gut bacteria may cause stomach problems, or digestive problems may alter the bacterial population.

Diet could also play a part in this relationship. Many children with autism prefer a limited range of foods. The bacteria living in the intestines are strongly influenced by what a person eats.

The study also had a few other limitations. Blood was not collected from healthy children for ethical reasons. Results were compared with established normal ranges instead.

Children with known digestive diseases were excluded by the team. Some of the analyses did not account for other factors that might have influenced the findings.

Children could be followed over several years in future studies. Such work may show whether changes in gut bacteria occur before digestive or behavioral changes.

Rhoads believes that everyday habits deserve more attention.

“This study highlights the importance of supporting a healthy gut microbiome in children with autism,” Rhoads said.

“A varied diet rich in fruits, vegetables, and whole grains, along with healthy toileting habits, may help improve both gastrointestinal health and behavior.”

“Our team has also found promising results with probiotic treatment.”

The results of the probiotic trial have not yet been published. Scientists are still working to determine which probiotic supplements are helpful and under what conditions.

The most useful lesson for parents may concern the recognition of discomfort rather than bacteria.

A child may suddenly become irritable, sleep poorly or show more repetitive behaviors. Such changes may be caused by something as ordinary as constipation or stomach pain.

Many children are unable to put this discomfort into words. Behavior may be one of the ways in which they ask for help.

The study is published in the journal Pediatric Research.

RFK Jr. weighs in on plan to detonate toxic material in Iowa

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Amanda Erickson is a news editor at STAT.

Jonathan Wosen is STAT’s West Coast biotech & life sciences reporter. You can reach Jonathan on Signal at jwosen.27.

We aren’t sending a newsletter tomorrow, so a moment of sappiness today ahead of the holiday — I’ve been at STAT for 16 months, and I’m so thankful for the opportunities I’ve had to write and edit here. I learn something new just about every day (most recently that international graduate student enrollment has declined precipitously), and I’m lucky to have such wonderful, kind colleagues explaining the world of health and medicine.

If you, like me, are thankful for STAT’s coverage and hungry for more, we’ve got an amazing Cyber Week deal running. Pay $20 for six months of STAT+, and then $150 for the next year.

There were the hours spent prepping for Joe Rogan’s podcast, an afternoon filming videos at Kid Rock’s home in Nashville, and a meeting with the CEO of the company that makes Tylenol, just weeks before President Trump urged pregnant women not to take the drug because he claimed, without evidence, it was linked to autism.

These are some of the appointments documented in health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s calendar. In a show of “radical transparency,” HHS released the secretary’s partial calendar, which reflects his activities from Feb. 13, 2025, to June 19, 2026.

The calendar — part of a much broader release of documents from the Department of Health and Human Services — includes significant redactions and missing information. It shows, for example, only one meeting with one of his closest aides, Stefanie Spear. What else did our reporters find in this treasure trove of 92,000 documents? Read on.

This morning, the Army plans to detonate a cache of “dangerously degraded” toxic material in Iowa. Experts warn the planned explosion could send particles of lead into the air; an Army analysis found a plume could travel up to 100 miles, impacting homes and 350 farms.

The issue is attracting the attention of any number of federal agencies. The explosion’s after-effects are being tracked by the Environmental Protection Agency; FEMA is supporting possible evacuations. And Kennedy has weighed in several times from the perspective of HHS. Last week, former Iowa gubernatorial candidate Zach Lahn posted on X that he had spoken with Kennedy about the planned detonation and that the secretary connected him with the White House to express his concerns. On Tuesday, Kennedy said on X that HHS is “working with state and local officials to assess potential lead exposure and support Iowa’s public health response,” adding that health department officials will “remain on the ground afterward to support the response and assist our partners with necessary cleanup.”

With MAHA advocates expressing concern about the planned detonation, it could be an opportunity for Kennedy to reassert the primacy of the movement. As my colleague Chelsea Cirruzzo put it in a recent story: “Even as Republicans pick up the pieces following devastating midterm election losses, spirits are high at the Department of Health and Human Services, where recent changes in the administration — namely the appointment of Stephen Miller as White House chief of staff — have put wind under the wings of health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.”

Even as judges have blocked visa restrictions and other limits on foreign scholars and scientists, the Trump administration is winning outside court. New data show a clear drop in the enrollment of international graduate students, a key piece of the U.S. biomedical workforce.

That paradox was on full display last week. On Nov. 16, a federal judge issued a final ruling blocking a proposed four-year cap on how long international students and postdoctoral researchers can remain in the country. The next day, a judge in a separate case issued a preliminary injunction preventing the administration from narrowing eligibility for a work authorization program widely used by foreign students.

But data released the same day as the ruling in the visa time limit case paint a different picture. A survey of more than 800 U.S. institutions conducted by the Institute of International Education found that foreign student enrollment for the current academic year has decreased 9% overall compared with 2025, with a 15% drop among newly enrolled students and graduate students. Read more here. — Jonathan Wosen

That’s the percent of newborns who received the vaccine against hepatitis B at birth in the first half of 2026, down from 80% in 2022. The findings, published this week in the New England Journal of Medicine, likely reflect “continuing vaccine hesitancy and confusion among parents after recent changes in recommendations,” the authors write. (In December 2025, a CDC panel remade by Kennedy recommended delaying the birth dose of the vaccine.)

A STAT piece from last year helps illuminate how the hepatitis B vaccine birth dose nearly eliminated the disease — which can cause chronic liver disease and even death — in kids and teens. “Transmission can happen in the household or at day care, in the infinitesimal-seeming blood exposure that might stem from a razor nick or shared toothbrush, a bite or scratch from another kid,” my former colleague Eric Boodman wrote, adding that “there are antivirals that can help control hepatitis B, but there is no cure. Once people have it, they have it for life.”

If you, like me, are unwilling to splurge on anything besides the most basic Hulu package, you’ve probably had to sit through countless drug commercials.

Turns out, those brightly colored spots about the benefits of GLP-1s for weight loss might not be so great for public health. In a new survey of 1,000 people, researchers found that adults exposed to higher levels of GLP-1 ads were more self-conscious about their weight and appearance. The study also showed, likely to the delight of advertisers everywhere, that people who’d seen more GLP-1 commercials were more likely to want to try GLP-1s.

Shamash Alidina: Gratitude with Room for Everything Else

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The most trusted resource for mindfulness and meditation.

A guided practice for noticing small moments of goodness without pretending that everything is fine.

Gratitude practices sometimes feel like one more thing to get right, as if we’re supposed to feel thankful on cue no matter what’s happening.

Our guest this week is Shamash Alidina. He offers a gentler approach. You make room for whatever you’re feeling, and then you see whether one small, ordinary thing you appreciate can sit alongside it.

Read and practice this meditation on your own, pausing after each step. Or listen to the guided audio.

Shamash Alidina has been practising mindfulness since 1998 and runs his own successful training organisation. He is the author of Mindfulness For Dummies and most recently, The Mindful Way Through Stress. He frequently pops up in newspapers, magazines and on radio shows. Based in London, he runs online trainings and speaks at conferences all over the world. He’s been teaching mindfulness full-time since 2010.

A 5-Minute Stretch Routine to Relieve Tight Joints and a Stiff Back

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

If you’ve been feeling ultra stiff lately, a five-minute yoga-inspired stretching routine break to release physical tension can feel like a full-body reset, helping you feel better in your body—and even more productive—so you can do all of your favorite activities.

This short practice is ideal when you need a short break during the workday, but it can also serve as a grounding moment before you sit at your desk or when you’re winding down in the evening. You only need a yoga mat and a sturdy surface to practice on. Come exactly as you are.

Lie on your back with your arms and legs straight, as if you were in Savasana. Take some small movements, maybe wiggling your fingers and toes or moving your head side to side. Then let yourself relax for a moment. Inhale as you reach your hands overhead and your feet away from you, stretching your entire body from head to toe. Exhale and release.

Bend your knees, plant your feet on the mat about hip-distance apart, and rest your arms at your sides. Inhale, press your hands and feet into the mat, and lift your hips in Bridge Pose. Exhale and lower your hips to the mat.

Separate your feet slightly wider than your hips. Bend your elbows and draw your arms into a cactus shape. Exhale and allow your knees to lower toward the left. Inhale and return your knees to center. Exhale and lower your knees toward the right. Repeat Windshield Wipers two more times on each side.

Hug both knees toward your chest, grasping your shins or the backs of your thighs with your hands.

Come to a comfortable sitting position. Lift your shoulders toward your ears, then roll them back, down, and forward. Continue drawing circles with your shoulders in one direction for a few rounds, then switch directions.

Interlace your fingers behind your head with your elbows out to the sides. Exhale as you draw your navel toward your spine, round your back, and lower your chin toward your chest in a Cat Pose variation. Inhale as you lift upright and arch your back, drawing your elbows back slightly in a Cow Pose variation. Repeat a few times.

Inhale and reach your arms overhead. Exhale and twist your chest toward the left. Place your right hand on your left knee and your left fingertips on the mat behind you. Take a few breaths here.

Still sitting, come back to center and bring your arms into a cactus shape. Open your hands as wide as you can, spreading your fingers apart. Then shut your hands into tight fists. Repeat opening and closing your hands as many times as feels good.

Sit on the mat with your knees bent, your feet flat on the mat, and your hands on the mat behind your hips, fingertips pointing toward you or toward the back of the mat. Press into your hands and feet as you lift your hips off the mat. Draw your chin toward your chest and gaze forward. Take a few breaths here, then lower your hips to the mat.

Draw the soles of your feet together. Inhale and lift your chest. Your feet can come closer to you or stay farther away. Exhale and fold forward, grasping your ankles or feet in Bound Angle Pose. You can stack your fists on top of each other and rest your forehead on them. Breathe here.

Lie on your back with your arms resting at your sides and your palms pressing into the mat. Bend your knees toward your chest and straighten your legs so your feet are reaching toward the ceiling. Keep your legs slightly bent if it’s more comfortable. Take a few breaths here, then shake out your arms and legs and lower your feet to the mat.

Lie on your back, resting your arms, legs, hands, and feet against the mat in Savasana. Or find a comfortable seated position. Take a few deep breaths before returning to the day.

What Is Smart Aging? Experts Explain the Skin Care Shift Replacing Antiaging

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Hailey Bieber’s preferred term reframes aging as prevention, protection, and smarter skin care.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Beauty terminology has evolved quite rapidly in recent years—particularly around aging. What was long labeled antiaging has shifted toward more realistic definitions of what happens to skin and the body as we get older. But the latest buzzword, longevity, as ubiquitous as it’s become, doesn’t exactly fill the gap for those seeking ways to keep skin, hair, and the body looking youthful. Hailey Bieber’s recent use of the term “smart aging” just may be the word substitute the category has been missing.

Natalia Guzman, FNP-C, CANS, a board-certified nurse practitioner at Atelier Aesthetix in Beverly Hills, explains that smart aging is a proactive, science-based approach to maintaining healthy, youthful-looking skin while embracing the natural aging process. “Rather than trying to ‘stop’ aging altogether, which is biologically impossible, smart aging focuses on understanding how our skin changes over time and making informed decisions to preserve a healthy, strong, overall youthful appearance,” Guzman tells ELLE.

From a dermatologic perspective, Dr. Brianna Olamiju, a board-certified dermatologist with Spring Street Dermatology in NYC, offers this explanation: “Smart aging is about being proactive, making informed skin care choices, and supporting the skin’s natural functions over time. It involves protecting the skin from environmental damage, maintaining a healthy skin barrier, stimulating collagen production when appropriate, and addressing concerns such as fine lines, wrinkles, uneven pigmentation, and loss of elasticity,” says Olamiju. Ultimately, she says, smart aging is about helping your skin look and feel its best at every stage of life while embracing the natural aging process.

Guzman emphasizes that aging beautifully isn’t about looking young. “It’s about looking and feeling like the best version of yourself at every stage of life.”

As skin evolves, collagen production gradually declines, cellular turnover slows, and environmental factors such as UV exposure, pollution, and lifestyle habits contribute to visible signs of aging. Guzman adds that smart aging means addressing these changes strategically through preventative skin care, healthy lifestyle choices, and, when appropriate, evidence-based aesthetic treatments. “I like to think of smart aging as investing in your skin’s future and being proactive rather than waiting until significant changes occur and attempting to reverse them,” Guzman says. “A lot of the time, the latter takes much more work, and sometimes it cannot be completely corrected.”

According to Guzman, the biggest difference between smart aging and traditional antiaging is the philosophy behind it. “For decades, the beauty industry has promoted the idea that aging is something we need to fight, prevent, or erase,” says Guzman. The term “antiaging” often focuses on correcting visible signs of aging, such as wrinkles, volume loss, pigmentation, and skin laxity.

Both Guzman and Olamiju agree that smart aging takes a more holistic, comprehensive, and realistic approach. Olamiju says that anti-aging creates the expectation that we should try to look younger indefinitely. “Smart aging shifts the focus toward skin health, prevention, and maintaining long-term skin quality. Rather than viewing wrinkles, fine lines, and changes in skin textureas something that must be eliminated, smart aging encourages us to support the skin as it naturally evolves. It is less about fighting aging and more about aging well,” she explains. This approach, she adds, also emphasizes consistency, prevention, and individualized treatments rather than chasing every new product or cosmetic trend.

Guzman adds that smart aging, instead of fighting age, means working with the skin’s biology to help it function and look its best for as long as possible. “The main difference is that we’re not simply chasing wrinkles. We're creating a long-term strategy that prioritizes skin quality, natural-looking results, and overall skin health,” Guzman emphasizes. “To me, anti-aging is often about correcting what we see today, while smart aging is about preventing and protecting what we’ll see tomorrow.”

Olamiju and Guzman are completely aligned on the first step in a smart aging routine: wearing sunscreen daily. “The foundation of smart aging starts with a consistent skincare routine and healthy lifestyle habits. Daily broad-spectrum sunscreen with an SPF of at least 30 is arguably the most important step because ultraviolet exposure is one of the leading contributors to premature skin aging, including wrinkles, loss of elasticity, and uneven pigmentation,” says Olamiju. Guzman adds that UV exposure contributes significantly to collagen breakdown and changes in skin texture, noting that sunscreen is essential even on cloudy days.

Olamiju also recommends incorporating topical antioxidants, such as vitamin C, to help protect against environmental oxidative stress, along with moisturizers containing ingredients like ceramides and hyaluronic acid to support hydration and the skin barrier. “Retinoids, including retinol and prescription tretinoin, can be beneficial for improving fine lines, skin texture, and collagen production, provided they are appropriate for the individual,” Olamiju describes.

Paula's Choice Resist Youth-Extending Daily Hydrating Fluid SPF 50

Sunday Riley Ceramor Ceramide Barrier Replenishing Cream

Bio-Therapeutic bt-sculpt Gen2 Microcurrent Facial Sculpting System

Once your at-home routine is in place, Guzman recommends strategically incorporating preventive and corrective aesthetic treatments. “Depending on the patient’s age, anatomy, and concerns, treatments such as neuromodulators, collagen-stimulating injectables, microneedling, and energy-based devices may help maintain or improve certain aspects of skin appearance,” says Guzman. The key, she adds, is choosing the right treatment for the right patient rather than following every new aesthetic trend. Olamiju includes chemical peels in a very similar list of in-office treatments, depending, of course, on an individual’s concerns, skin type, and goals.

Finally, the experts agree: don’t underestimate lifestyle. Adequate sleep, regular exercise, balanced nutrition rich in antioxidants and healthy fats, avoiding smoking, managing chronic stress, and limiting excessive alcohol consumption all contribute to overall health and can influence how our skin looks and functions.

The best time to start is earlier than most people think, Olamiju shares. Ideally in your 20s, when prevention can have a meaningful long-term impact. “This does not mean that someone in their 20s needs an elaborate skincare routine or aggressive cosmetic treatments. Rather, it is an opportunity to establish healthy habits, including daily sunscreen use, gentle cleansing, and consistent moisturizing,” says Olamiju.

Guzman advises starting a smart aging routine before you feel like you need to correct something, but it’s also never too late to begin. “I encourage patients to think of smart aging as something that evolves with them rather than a routine that begins at a specific birthday,” she adds, offering this decade-by-decade breakdown:

In your 20s. The priority should be prevention. This means establishing healthy skincare habits, wearing sunscreen consistently, maintaining hydration, and introducing targeted active ingredients when appropriate. At this stage, the goal isn’t to aggressively treat aging. It's to protect the skin and build a strong foundation.

In your 30s. You may begin noticing subtle changes in skin texture, fine lines, pigmentation, or elasticity. This is often a good time to reassess your skin care routine and consider professional treatments based on individual needs. It may also be time to consider a little Botox, energy-based treatments, and biostimulators, all depending on the individual’s needs.

In your 40s and 50s. Changes in collagen, facial volume, skin elasticity, and hormonal influences may become more noticeable. A smart aging approach at this stage may include a combination of medical-grade skin care, collagen-supporting treatments, and continued careful selection of aesthetic procedures.

In your 60s and beyond. The focus remains on maintaining skin health, supporting the skin barrier, addressing concerns such as dryness and uneven pigmentation, and choosing treatments that respect the skin’s changing needs.

Olamiju adds that even patients who begin prioritizing their skin health later in life can benefit significantly from consistent sun protection and an appropriately tailored skin care regimen.

Guzman says this is one of the most important conversations we should be having in modern aesthetics. Both experts say smart aging is not a one-size-fits-all concept. They explain that different skin types and skin tones can experience visible aging differently, and understanding those differences is essential to creating a safe and effective treatment plan.

Individuals with lighter skin tones may notice fine lines, redness, and visible sun damage earlier, particularly if they have a history of significant UV exposure. Those with darker skin tones may have more visible concerns related to uneven pigmentation, post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation, and changesin skin tone. “Melanin provides some natural protection against ultraviolet radiation, but it does not eliminate the risk of sun damage or skin cancer, so daily sunscreen remains essential for all skin tones,” Olamiju warns.

Guzman adds that these pigmentation concerns also become especially important when we introduce aesthetic treatments such as lasers, chemical peels, or microneedling. “Certain procedures and treatment settings carry a greater risk of pigmentation changes in patients with more melanin-rich skin, so proper patient selection, device choice, and clinical expertise are critical,” Guzman notes.

Overall, smart aging should combine science, prevention, consistency, and thoughtful aesthetic care. “Aging is inevitable. How we care for ourselves throughout that process is where we have the opportunity to make a meaningful difference,” Guzman concludes.

‘Root-to-Tip’ Eating: Are You Missing Out on Fiber by Discarding Scraps?

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Eating fruit and veggie scraps may boost fiber intake and cut food waste.

This study from King’s College London found that peels, skins, seeds, and stalks of fruits and vegetables—parts often discarded—contained significantly more dietary fiber than the commonly eaten portions in 10 of 12 produce types analyzed.

In some cases, the fiber content was up to five times higher in these “root-to-tip” components.

These findings, recently published in the International Journal of Food Sciences and Nutrition, are noteworthy, since around 95% of U.S. adults aren’t getting enough fiber. What’s more, around one-fifth of all food produced globally is wasted every year.

The researchers identified 12 fresh produce items that were deemed suitable for “root-to-tip” inclusion:

In the laboratory analysis, they measured the dietary fiber content of both the “root-to-tip” components (skins, peels, seeds, stalks) and the parts that people typically eat.

The key findings of the study showed that in 10 of the 12 produce types, the “root-to-tip” components contained more fiber than the parts typically consumed.

However, broccoli stalks contained 28.4% less fiber than the florets that people usually eat, meaning individuals are already consuming the most fiber-rich part.

Most participants in focus groups saw the nutritional value of the “root-to-tip” approach as more of a motivator than its sustainability.

However, these findings show that a “root-to-tip” approach to many fruits and vegetables could not only support higher dietary fiber intake but also reduce food waste.

Kristin Kirkpatrick, president of KAK, Consulting, LLC, and a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness & Preventive Medicine, said that the takeaway isn’t to eat every peel or seed, but to look for safe, simple ways to get more fiber from the foods you’re already buying. Kirkpatrick wasn’t involved in the study.

This applies to some of the plants analyzed in this study, but not all produce. For instance, you can only eat a kiwi that’s peeled. This is still a better choice than no kiwi (or a processed-food snack in its place) at all.

Fiber is important for promoting health and preventing chronic conditions such as cardiovascular disease, type 2 diabetes, colorectal cancer, and obesity.

“Fiber does much more than support regular bowel movements. It helps regulate blood sugar, supports beneficial gut bacteria, and certain types of soluble fiber can help lower LDL cholesterol by increasing bile acid elimination,” noted Michelle Routhenstein, a preventive cardiology dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study.

On average, adults are recommended to get 25–30 grams (or more, depending on age) of fiber daily.

Both nutrition experts told Healthline that people generally aren’t getting enough fiber because of their food options or choices.

“Most likely because individuals are not consuming enough plants, and overconsuming processed and ultra-processed foods which are typically very low in fiber,” said Kirkpatrick. “Simply not eating enough fruits and vegetables or choosing white refined grains over whole grains can be why a person’s diet is lacking fiber.”

Routhenstein added, “I think it is important that when we talk about fiber we point out that it doesn’t have to mean completely changing how you eat. I encourage people to incorporate fiber-rich ingredients into familiar, culturally meaningful foods they already enjoy.”

“Start with small increases with foods you enjoy and build gradually to allow your digestive system to adjust.”

It is important to always research any new plant parts before eating them, wash thoroughly, and consult a healthcare professional if you have digestive conditions or concerns.

And remember that not all produce scraps are edible or recommended.

“Any dietary change will be dependent on personal preference. Some individuals may not enjoy or may not know how to add components of a plant they are not used to,” said Kirkpatrick.

She noted that, based on the study’s examples, an easier option might be to add more kiwi peels and seeds. “In fact, kiwi skin is probably the most practical fruit peel to highlight for consumers,” she said.

Routhenstein added, “I think there is a knowledge gap that the skins, peels, from the root to the tip are edible and nutritious, so we need to start there.”

She suggested that one way to start is by eating the skins of apples, pears, and well-scrubbed carrots. Trim and thinly slice broccoli stalks for stir-fries or soups. Sautée beet greens and stems just as you would spinach. Freeze appropriate vegetable trimmings, such as carrot ends and celery leaves, for homemade broth.

“Root-to-tip eating helps reduce food waste by encouraging us to use more of the edible portions of fruits and vegetables rather than discarding them. It also maximizes the nutritional and economic value of the food we purchase while making better use of the water, land, and energy required to produce it,” said Routhenstein.

“The key is finding simple, enjoyable ways to incorporate these parts into meals we already eat.”

Petunias reveal an unexpected link between flower color and scent

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists studying petunias discovered that the same biological machinery responsible for flower color also shapes their scent.

Petunias use the same raw ingredient, an amino acid called phenylalanine, to make pigments and many of their scent compounds.

Researchers at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem found that a protein called EOBV works with two proteins involved in pigmentation to regulate the flower’s scent.

When the researchers removed this protein, the flowers released more compounds associated with the smell of cloves and vanilla.

However, they released less methyl benzoate, normally the most abundant scent compound in these petunias.

Oded Skaliter and Alexander Vainstein ran all of it in petunias. The chemistry moved. Nobody has checked whether a nose, human or moth, can tell the difference.

Wild petunias have different ways of attracting pollinators. One species releases its fragrance at night to attract moths, another attracts bees with purple flowers, and a third uses red flowers to attract hummingbirds.

Petunia pigment is run by a committee of three, known to biologists as the MBW complex.

Two of these proteins, AN1 and AN11, remain the same. The third can be any one of several proteins belonging to a family called MYB, and its identity determines which genes the group controls.

Skaliter and his colleagues examined when AN1 and AN11 were most active.

Activity peaked as the flowers opened and remained high for another two days, around the same time that scent production reached its peak.

Then they knocked each gene down, one at a time, with a plant virus. Air from around the petals went into the analyzer.

Both treatments increased the release of eugenol and vanillin. The amount of methyl benzoate roughly doubled.

Rather than helping the flowers produce more fragrance, the pigment proteins appeared to hold scent production back.

The researchers suspected that the missing piece of the puzzle was the third protein in the group.

Six MYB proteins were already in the frame here, all of them previously linked to petunia fragrance.

Each went up against AN1 and AN11 in yeast cells that change color when two proteins bind. EOBV was the only hit.

The team repeated the experiment in living plant tissue. This time, they used a system that produces betalain, the red pigment found in beets, when proteins interact.

Leaves of a tobacco relative turned red when they received both AN1 and EOBV. Leaves that received AN1 alone produced a weaker color signal.

The strongest red color appeared when all three proteins were present. Removing any one of them greatly reduced pigment production in the test.

AN11 and EOBV would not bind each other. AN1 had to stand between them.

The researchers also found very little EOBV gene activity outside the petals. Within the petals, activity was about 1.8 times higher in the upper surface layer, through which fragrance escapes.

Previous work from the same laboratory found that a gene responsible for giving petals their identity also activates scent production.

The next step was to remove EOBV completely. A virus carried CRISPR gene-editing tools into petunia tissue, and the team recovered three groups of edited plants.

In one group, the instructions for starting the protein were gone, along with the first 42 amino acids. Production in the other two stopped after 58 and 91 amino acids.

The edited plants still looked and flowered like the originals.

Two-day-old flowers then went into sealed jars, two flowers to a jar, and whatever they gave off over the next 24 hours was trapped and measured.

Without a working version of EOBV, the flowers released more eugenol, both forms of isoeugenol and vanillin. These compounds give cloves and vanilla their familiar aromas.

However, the flowers released less of almost every other scent compound, including methyl benzoate. The total amount of fragrance released also fell.

The team then ground up petals to measure how much of each compound remained inside. The clove and vanilla compounds had increased there, too.

The petals contained similar amounts of methyl benzoate and several other scent compounds even though the edited flowers released less of them.

A repressor has to act on something, so the next question was which genes EOBV holds down. Gene activity was compared between edited and unedited flowers.

Most showed little difference. However, two genes, known as C4H2 and ADT3, became more active after the researchers removed EOBV.

C4H2 controls the carbon supply to the branch that produces eugenol, isoeugenol and vanillin.

The rise in C4H2 gene activity may help explain why the edited flowers made more eugenol, isoeugenol, and vanillin.

The team then tested that link directly. They connected the region of DNA that controls C4H2 activity to a system that produces red pigment.

Petals that received EOBV showed no visible red color. Petals without the additional EOBV produced more than twice as much pigment.

Two other fragrance genes, PAAS and BSMT, became less active in the edited flowers. Since EOBV does not appear to bind directly to the regions controlling these genes, the changes were probably indirect.

Researchers found a similar pattern in 2011 when they reduced the activity of another petunia gene called PhMYB4. PhMYB4 and EOBV are about 80% identical. Genetic testing confirmed that PhMYB4 itself had not been edited.

EOBV becomes more active when petunias grow in warmer conditions. The researchers wanted to see whether removing it could protect flowers from heat.

They grew the plants in a greenhouse for a month before moving them into two rooms. The cooler room had temperatures of 72°F (22°C) during the day and 61°F (16°C) at night. The warmer room reached 82°F (28°C) and 72°F (22°C), respectively.

After measuring 38 flowers, the team found that unedited flowers became 12% narrower in warmer conditions. Edited flowers shrank by only 6%.

Heat also reduced the amount of scent released by unedited flowers, mainly due to lower emissions of benzaldehyde and methyl benzoate. Edited flowers maintained their overall scent output.

Meanwhile, PhEXP1, a gene that helps petal cells expand, became less active in both groups by similar amounts.

Heat-shrunk petunia flowers were traced to slower cell division in a 2021 study, a step this team did not measure.

More vanilla and clove compounds might sound appealing, but would these petunias actually smell better?

Nobody has asked people to smell the edited flowers. The researchers haven’t tested pollinators either.

Removing EOBV also has limits. In purple varieties, reducing EOBV activity left pigment levels unchanged.

EOBV belongs to the color machinery without fixing the shade of purple itself.

The team also crossed the edited plants with petunias lacking EVER, another scent regulator. Those flowers did not outperform either parent. The authors suggest limited carbon, or a limited capacity to release scent.

The authors also disclosed a commercial interest. Skaliter and Vainstein are chief technology officer and chief scientific officer at CapsiBreed, which uses gene editing in hot and sweet peppers. Peppers and petunias are relatives.

One obvious next experiment is to switch EOBV off in plants that are already missing AN1 or AN11.

The team also wants to know whether other flowering plants are wired this way. And growers will want an answer to a blunter question: does a reshuffled scent give them flowers that anyone wants to buy?

The full study was published in the journal Plant Physiology.
