Fascia Release Is the Key to Regulating Your Nervous System, Getting Better Sleep, and Improving Skin

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Fascia, the thin web that envelops the face and body, is more powerful than you might think. Here’s how to release it

I’m curled up on a treatment bed in the Corinthia spa, feeling a knot of tension dissipate slowly from my neck and shoulders. Within minutes, tears prick my eyes. I’m familiar with the sudden catharsis bodywork can bring, but this is different. Known as “myofascial release”—a targeted therapy that works with fascia, the continuous matrix of connective tissue that wraps every muscle, nerve, and organ—it aims to reduce tightness and release restricted tissue, restoring its natural glide.

“Fascia is deeply connected to the entire sensory network,” explains Nadia Alibhai, a London-based osteopath who was unfazed by my heightened emotions. “When you move or hold stress, forces are transmitted through this interconnected system. It senses everything and dictates how the whole body responds. This treatment directly influences how we perceive and react to tension.”

Once dismissed as mere biological wrapping paper, fascia—made up of water, collagen, elastin, and hyaluronic acid—is now understood as a living, three-dimensional web. “It’s part of a complex system involving movement, circulation, lymphatics, posture, and tissue health,” Alibhai explains. When it works well, it keeps everything moving and prevents systems from becoming sluggish, but when it becomes restricted, often from scrolling, sedentary habits, or chronic stress, the fluid between the layers can thicken. This affects physical mobility and emotional well-being, leading to issues ranging from pain and stiffness to a dysregulated nervous system.

Fascia and neurology are intrinsically linked, explains skin-health expert Annee de Mamiel. “Fascia changes according to how we move, breathe, and repeatedly load the body,” she says. “Cortisol and adrenaline change our physiology; the nervous system alters breathing, muscular tone, and stress response. Over time, these repeated patterns affect the tissues.” It’s a catch-22: greater stress can tighten fascia, which in turn can send your body into fight-or-flight mode, prolonging the anxiety that caused the damage in the first place.

This mind-body connection is just one reason why fascia has piqued the wellness industry’s interest. Google searches for “fascia release” have soared in the past year, while facials targeting the system are becoming more popular. Robert Schleip, the director of the Fascia Research Group at Ulm University, attributes this to its holistic possibilities. “Fascia is now studied in relation to the autonomic nervous system, and therefore emotional regulation, as well as the immune system and aging,” he says. The latest findings show that supporting your fascia goes far beyond easing a tight neck; it's linked to reduced arterial stiffness and improved cardiovascular health.

Fascia release has also entered the skin-health conversation. Tight fascia can be an overlooked culprit behind loss of luminosity, puffiness, and premature aging, says de Mamiel, because it’s heavily involved in fluid drainage and lymphatic pathways. “The diaphragm plays a role in pressure changes and fluid movement through the body, and the fascial system connects these areas,” she explains. “When I work on movement and tension through the neck, chest, and rib cage, I often see a change in how congested or puffy the face appears.” However, releasing fascia effectively doesn't require aggressive force. “If I use so much pressure that your body braces, I’ve produced the opposite response—it’s about creating conditions where the nervous system and tissue can both settle.”

Facialist Nichola Joss achieves similarly impressive results with her signature Inner Facial, which fuses lymphatic drainage, fascia release, buccal massage, and sculpting techniques. “Fascia surrounds and supports our muscles; when restricted, it affects how freely muscles move,” Joss explains. This affects lymphatic flow and your skin’s natural glow. “By releasing muscular and fascial tension, you lift, tone, and sculpt contours while boosting circulation and softening fine lines.”

Beyond professional treatments, supporting fascial health depends on consistent daily habits. First, fascia thrives on varied movement: try walking, twisting your body, reaching overhead, and squatting to support overall health. Unfortunately, there are no true quick fixes: Spending the day glued to an office chair and then going straight into an intense HIIT session can actually be counterproductive. Instead, interrupt patterns throughout the day: Stand during phone calls instead of sitting, swing your arms gently during screen breaks, and make sure you’re not holding your breath during stressful periods at work. Counteract hours spent hunched over a device with this simple doorway stretch: Place your forearms against the frame, step forward gently, and hold for 20 to 30 seconds while breathing deeply.

Schleip recommends aligning exercise with your circadian rhythm. “Incorporate dynamic, elastic, or bouncing movements in the morning, ideally outdoors, and slow stretches in the evening,” he suggests, adding that sustained yoga positions, such as a child’s pose held for at least 30 seconds, can be particularly helpful. “Regular stretching improves joint mobility and may benefit cardiovascular markers such as arterial stiffness,” he adds. Finally, make nutrition and hydration a priority: fascia is 70 percent water and needs hydration to glide freely.

As Alibhai reflects at the end of my session: “The goal isn’t to constantly release fascia; it’s to keep your body moving, adaptable, and well nourished.” I leave feeling lighter and more grounded—and I instinctively skip the Tube and walk home.

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Kennedy Jr.’s calendar.While many of the records were previously available, the health department heralded the launch of the site as a sign of its “radical transparency.”

Scientists Discover an Ancient Herbal Remedy May Help Curb Binge Drinking

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A supplement derived from kudzu, a plant used for centuries in traditional East Asian medicine, may help people with alcohol use disorder reduce binge drinking without taking medication every day.

In a clinical trial led by researchers at UC San Francisco (UCSF), people who took a standardized kudzu extract before anticipated episodes of heavy drinking experienced significantly fewer binge-drinking days than those given a placebo. The benefits persisted for three months after participants stopped taking the supplement.

The findings, published in the American Journal of Psychiatry on November 4, suggest that taking a treatment during periods of heightened risk could offer a new strategy for managing problematic alcohol consumption.

Binge drinking accounts for more than half of the roughly 80,000 annual U.S. deaths attributed to excessive alcohol consumption. Despite the scale of the problem, fewer than 10% of people with alcohol use disorder have ever received medication to treat the condition.

“Binge drinking is incredibly common, but we don’t have many tools to offer people who want to cut back,” said Glenn-Milo Santos, PhD, MPH, the study’s lead author and a UCSF professor of community health systems. “Our goal is to expand that toolkit. What’s exciting about this approach is that people can use it when they know they may be at risk of heavy drinking, rather than taking a medication every day.”

The trial involved 120 adults with alcohol use disorder who engaged in binge drinking at least once a week. Binge drinking was defined as consuming at least five alcoholic drinks on a single occasion for men and at least four for women.

Participants were divided into two groups, with half receiving a standardized kudzu extract and the other half receiving a placebo. Over 12 weeks, they were instructed to take their assigned treatment approximately 2.5 hours before they anticipated drinking heavily. Everyone also received brief weekly counseling sessions.

By the end of the trial, participants taking kudzu had reduced their average number of binge-drinking days from 2.15 to 1.42 per week. Those receiving the placebo showed little improvement, with their weekly average declining from 2.15 to 2.06 days.

Kudzu also reduced how frequently participants experienced weeks involving any binge drinking. The proportion of such weeks dropped from 88% to 58% among those taking the extract, compared with a decline from 88% to 81% in the placebo group.

Three months after the treatment period ended, participants who had received kudzu continued to report significantly fewer binge-drinking days than those in the placebo group. Researchers suggest that drinking less during the trial may have helped participants reconsider their drinking habits and develop greater confidence in their ability to control their alcohol consumption.

Participants tolerated the kudzu extract well, experiencing no more side effects than those receiving the placebo. No serious adverse effects were linked to the treatment.

However, the researchers caution against assuming that commercially available kudzu supplements will deliver comparable benefits. The trial used a standardized formulation, whereas the composition of products sold over the counter can vary considerably. Larger clinical studies are needed to confirm the results across more diverse populations.

Reference: “Targeted Use of Kudzu for Binge Drinking Among Adults With Alcohol Use Disorder: A Phase 2b Randomized Trial” by Glenn-Milo Santos, Phillip Coffin, Allie Dunham, Thomas Hoffmann, John Walker, Tim Matheson and Steven Batki, 3 November 2026, American Journal of Psychiatry. DOI: 10.1176/appi.ajp.20251354

This study was funded by National Institutes for Health (R01AA025930). Santos is also supported by a K24 career development grant from the National Institute on Alcohol Abuse and Alcoholism (NIAAA), which is part of the NIH.

Toxic Port Pollution Costs Europe €2.5bn A Year In Health & Environmental Damages — Study

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

New research reveals a single ship emits, on average, as much toxic sulphur at port in a year as 19,300 cars, leaving taxpayers and hospitals to pay the hidden cost of shipping pollution.

Commercial ships docked in European ports inflict €2.5 billion a year in societal and environmental costs, according to new research by T&E. While upcoming EU port rules will cut at-berth damages by 40% by 2030, most vessels remain exempt. Previous research has shown that residents living close to a commercial harbour already face significantly higher risks of cardiovascular and respiratory mortality¹. Tightening port pollution rules would save society billions and protect the health of port communities, says T&E.

Instead of plugging into the electrical grid when they dock, thousands of commercial vessels run diesel generators around the clock to power onboard systems. These idling engines release harmful pollution directly into neighbouring coastal and port communities. Analysis of Europe’s 100 largest ports shows that Rotterdam is the most polluted with costs at €350 million, followed by Antwerp (€220 million), Marseille (€120 million), Hamburg (€120 million), and Genoa (€110 million).

According to the report, removing loopholes in the EU rules would save Europe €1.56bn in health and climate costs. Under current EU rules, outlined in the Alternative Fuels Infrastructure Regulation (AFIR), only large container ships, ferries and cruise ships are required to plug into dockside electricity sockets, meaning oil and gas tankers, bulk carriers, car carriers and other cargo ships are left out entirely. Requirements to connect should be progressively extended to all ship types in the upcoming review to cut emissions and grant ports the necessary long-term predictability.

Eloi Nordé, shipping policy officer at T&E, said: “Port communities are paying the price for ships running their engines 24/7 at port. Fossil fuels only seem cheap because companies offload health and climate costs onto the public. Fortunately, the solution already exists. Plugging ships into shoreside electric power would save society billions and make port cities far healthier to live in.”

On paper, marine fuels appear less costly than drawing electricity from the port grid. However, this is largely because the associated healthcare and environmental burdens are absorbed by the local population rather than ship operators. Once these external costs are factored into energy pricing, generating power from fossil fuels at dock is twice as costly as using shore electricity.

This policy gap creates a lot of planning uncertainty for EU port authorities and electricity grid operators. Without a predictable policy progressively including all vessel types, ports are disincentivised from making long-term investments in green infrastructure, says T&E.

The EU will review the regulation at the end of this year. T&E urges EU policymakers to use the AFIR review to expand shore power to all ships, batteries, and boilers, backed by national port electrification roadmaps before 2032.

Its call is echoed today in a joint letter to the European Commission from the Clean Port Cities (CPC) coalition, uniting industry, civil society, and maritime and energy stakeholders including DFDS and Green Transition Denmark, urging predictable shore power timelines for all vessels, battery charging rules, and faster grid access.

¹ The port-health research cited refers to Mei et al. (2023), “Long-term effects of air pollutants on respiratory and cardiovascular mortality in a port city along the Adriatic sea,” BMC Pulmonary Medicine.

The Rise of Astrosociology: What Can Analogue Astronaut Studies Teach us About the Future of Humanity in Space?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It's no secret that some highly ambitious visions for the future of space exploration are taking shape today. Beyond NASA and China's plans to send crewed missions to the Moon by the end of the decade and create permanent infrastructure there, both space agencies hope to put boots on the surface of Mars by the 2030s or 40s. Meanwhile, the European Space Agency (ESA) is active in the Artemis Program and plans to send the ExoMars Rosalind Franklin rover to the Red Planet in 2028. India aims to send the first "gaganauts" to space next year (as part of its Gaganyaan program).

On top of all that, there are plans to "commercialize Low Earth Orbit" (LEO), build infrastructure in cis-lunar space (Ã  la asteroid mining), and even proposals for sending commercial astronauts to "space tourists" to the Moon and Mars. All of this begs the question of how humans intend to live in extraterrestrial environments and among the stars. What is needed to ensure that humans can not only survive the hazards of being off-world, but also flourish? As Mars City Design CEO Vera Mulyani would say, "How can we thrive, and not just survive?"

This is where the emerging field of Astrosociology comes into play, an umbrella term that looks at spaceflight through the lens of the social sciences. In addition to questions related to engineering and astronaut health and safety, astrosociology takes a more holistic outlook and examines the social, cultural, and behavioral patterns of humans related to outer space. Consider Abraham Maslow's "Hierarchy of Needs," which begins with the basics (physiological and security needs) and extends upwards to include the immaterial, such as love, self-esteem, and self-actualization.

This challenge motivated Dr. Jim Pass to found the Astrosociology Research Institute (ARI) in 2008, a California-based nonprofit educational organization dedicated to developing astrosociology as a multidisciplinary academic field. As Pass describes on the organization's website, astrosociology is both a subdiscipline of sociology and a multidisciplinary field that includes (but is not limited to) "psychology, anthropology, economics, social psychology, political science, space history, space law, space policy, philosophy, as well as the arts."

At its core, the ARI's mission comes down to a simple but highly relevant question: "How can social and behavioral scientists collaborate with physical and natural scientists to further the success of space exploration and settlement in the future?" In their latest newsletter, titled "Analog Space Missions: The Astrosociological Factors That Affect Analog Space Missions - Part 1," Pass and his colleagues share the perspectives of analog astronauts who participated in their call for papers and expert commentary by educators, space medics, and systems engineers.

As Mia Belle Parkinson, a postdoctoral student at the University of Edinburgh and ARI's editor-in-chief, wrote in the issue:

When we talk about preparing for space exploration and settlement, we too often default to the technical: propulsion systems, life support, radiation shielding. These things matter enormously. But so do the quieter, harder-to-quantify questions â how do people build trust in isolated environments? How do cultural backgrounds shape group dynamics? What does it mean, psychologically and socially, to live on the edge of the unknown?

While analog astronaut training may seem like a relatively new idea, it's actually part of a long-standing tradition almost as old as the "Space Race" itself. Before astronauts landed on the Moon, NASA conducted a series of training missions known as the "Apollo Geology Field Trips" between 1964 and 1972. The first field trip saw the first two astronaut groups travel to Flagstaff, Arizona, for a two-day mission to Meteor Crater and other locations that resembled volcanic features on the Moon.

Other locations included the Grand Canyon, the volcanic fields in Hawaii, the Nevada National Security Site, and mountain formations in the Sonoran Desert, New Mexico, Oregon, Alaska, west Texas, and Sudbury, Ontario. Additionally, the Soviets conducted the BIOS-3 experiment within the closed ecosystem built at the Institute of Biophysics in Krasnoyarsk in 1965. This was followed by Tektite I and II in 1969 and 1971, which studied human behavior in a confined, isolated, pressurized habitat on the ocean floor near the U.S. Virgin Islands.

JAXA (Japan Aerospace Exploration Agency) astronaut Satoshi Furukawa pedals on the upgraded CEVIS system, keeping muscle and bone density levels during spaceflight. (Credit: NASA)

Since that time, research aboard space stations such as Mir, the International Space Station (ISS), and the Chinese Tiangong space station has provided invaluable knowledge about living in space and the effects of microgravity on humans' mental and physical well-being. However, these studies are not directly relevant to proposed missions that will send astronauts to the Moon, Mars, and other celestial bodies. As Parry writes:

While extremely valuable for zero-gravity spaceflights, living and working at the bottom of a gravity well presents altogether different conditions, including an inability to manufacture drugs or lift heavy objects. Planning for bases and settlements on the surfaces of the Moon, Mars, and eventually beyond can only be simulated on the surface of the Earth to humankind's best approximation even in 2026, because gravity matters!

The first analog astronaut (hereafter AA) profiled was former NASA Advisor, science communicator, and space exploration expert Beth Mund. Mund is an especially experienced AA, having participated in the HI-SEAS (Hawai'i Space Exploration Analog and Simulation) program on the summit of Mauna Loa, Hawai'i, the LunAres Research Station, and the Jules Undersea Lodge. The first involved an all-female crew working together during the height of COVID in an uninsulated temporary habitat with minimal privacy and tough conditions.

The second took place in a converted World War II bunker in Poland located inside an old hangar. The hangar itself was filled with sand to simulate lunar regolith, and steel doors sealed the habitat and its six-person crew inside a rather confined space. At the Jules Undersea Lodge, located in Key Largo off the southern tip of Florida, Mund and her colleagues entered a submerged habitat with no escape hatches, accessible only through a "wet room" portal.

Looking back at these three missions, Mund noticed patterns that transcended any single environment. While each had its own geography and logistics, confinement was the common theme. From her experiences, Mund identified four universal human responses that were consistent throughout. The first, she wrote, was the importance of improvisation:

No matter how carefully equipment is designed or schedules are drawn, something always fails... each failure forces us to understand the problem and formulate a response to it in the context of very tight resource constraints. And in adapting, we discover more about ourselves than about the broken machines... These small acts of invention prove that ingenuity is not a luxury in confinement. It is a decisive factor in survival.

Photo of analog astronaut Beth Mund sitting outside the HI-SEAS habitat on the summit of Mauna Kea, Hawai'i. Credit: Beth Mund

The second lesson was the importance of ritual: "At HI-SEAS, our crew naturally gathered each evening for what I came to think of as our 'campfire,' a circle of debriefing, storytelling, and laughter, even in the cold dome with no flame. In Poland, games became our escape valve, a way to puncture the heaviness of the surveys and the claustrophobic walls. Underwater, simply gazing out the porthole together felt like a ceremony. In every analog, the crew instinctively created moments of reconnection that grounded us, even in places designed to isolate."

The third lesson for Mund was continuity, as she and her colleagues inherited experiments started by crews they never met and which would be handled by future analog crews. "It gave weight to our confinement, transforming it from a private trial into a communal contribution," she said. "That continuity across crew "generations" is perhaps the most powerful sociological insight of analog missions. We are practicing not only how to live together, but how to build across time, leaving work - and maybe even some data/ insights - for crews who serve next."

Lastly, there was the inevitability of testing. While every mission includes tests of equipment, plants, and methods for living in space, the experience also tests the limits of human endurance, creativity, and cohesion. This, she wrote, is the true essence of analog astronaut training:

From Mars-like lava fields to a Polish bunker to the ocean floor, I have learned that analog missions are not about pretending to be astronauts. They are about practicing human endurance in places that strip away comfort and certainty. In that practice, in all the different shapes it takes across environments, facilities, missions, and crews, lies the real preparation for humanity to endure no matter the circumstances.

Mund is merely one of many analog astronauts profiled in the Astrosociological Insights Forum's newsletter. To include as many testimonials and experiences as possible, the issue was released in two parts, incorporating perspectives not only from AAs but also from several experts in the burgeoning field of Astrosociology. More to follow, so stay tuned! In the meantime, check out some other articles we have about organizations committed to analog astronaut training:

His work is featured in The Ross 248 Project and Interstellar Travel edited by NASA alumni Les Johnson and Ken Roy. He also hosts the podcast series Stories from Space on Patreon. iRestore Elite Laser Hair Growth System Review: Husband-Approved

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The Aging Process is hard. It’s jarring, for example, when the luscious, flowing locks of your youth start to disappear before your very eyes. But there's nothing to do about this natural hair loss—or is there? While still the subject of ongoing research, red-light therapy has been used to treat inflammation, pain relief, and wrinkles, and—as it turns out—it may also help with hair regrowth. It’s also a much more accessible option than flying to Istanbul for a full-on hair transplant.

I learned about iRestore’s Elite Laser Growth Hair System after it came across my TikTok FYP. My algorithm likely served it to me because of my own hair woes (scalp irritation and eczema). Upon learning more about the system, I hoped it could help me with cell regeneration and kill off the bacteria causing flares on my scalp.

However, an even better test subject stood before me: my sweet husband, who is dealing with a rapidly receding hairline. So I roped him into testing this laser helmet—for science, of course. And to better understand said science, I spoke with Zakia Rahman, MD, FAAD, a clinical professor of dermatology at Stanford University School of Medicine.

Red-light therapy didn’t start as a treatment for antiaging and recovery. It actually came about as an accidental solution for hair loss in the 1960s, when Hungarian scientist Endre Mester was researching whether exposure to red light could treat cancer in hairless mice. Imagine his surprise when the mice started growing hair.

Red-light therapy has come a long way since then. According to Rahman, red-light wavelengths are thought to activate the mitochondria in skin cells, which may encourage an uptick in the production of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) to promote new growth. Red-light therapy may also improve blood flow to hair follicles.

Importantly, iRestore's devices are FDA-cleared—a safety designation from the US Food and Drug Administration indicating that a product is “substantially equivalent” to another legally marketed device. There are three hair-growth devices in iRestore's lineup: the entry-level Essential ($425), the mid-tier Professional ($799), and the Elite ($1,699). Each contains iRestore’s proprietary Lumitech technology, which combines LED lights and medical-grade lasers. These laser diodes, called Vixo lasers, operate in the range of 655 to 680 nanometers (nm). (Preliminary studies with mice have shown that using red light in the 600- to 660-nm range, and infrared wavelengths in the 800- to 850-nm range, can promote cell regeneration, pain relief, and more.)

The idea is that this dynamic duo of lights and lasers can penetrate deeper into the skin than ordinary red light with lasers, while the LEDs ensure light is distributed across the scalp for maximum efficacy. Together, they reach a maximum of 2,500 milliwatts (mW) in the Elite, compared to the Professional's 1,410 mW and Essential's 600 mW.

The Elite is iRestore’s top-of-the-line offering and has the most LEDs of the three laser hair-growth systems, with 500 diodes in total—that's 300 lasers and 200 LEDs. The Essential has 51 lasers and 69 LEDs for a total of 120 diodes, while the iRestore Professional has 82 lasers and 200 LEDs for a total of 282 diodes. The Elite was also designed to cover more of the scalp than the other two models and requires 12 minutes of daily use, while the Professional and Essential require 25 minutes every other day.

If price is not a factor, the Elite is the no-brainer choice due to its higher-concentrated light therapy, shorter use time, and potentially faster results.

The helmet arrived in hefty red packaging containing the helmet, a portable battery pack (annoyingly, this is a $99 add-on purchase), a charging cord, and a red-and-black zippered travel case. It feels very fragile, and I wouldn't throw it around—then again, you probably wouldn't plan to shove such an expensive device in the back of your bathroom vanity.

During the daily 12-minute sessions with the Elite helmet, you'll feel as if you're sitting under bright, hot sunlight. The remote displays a countdown of how many minutes you have left, and it beeps once the cycle is complete. Because I was testing the helmet to treat my dry scalp and eczema, I grew increasingly concerned with every passing minute of feeling warmer and warmer. (Sweating is a big no-no for eczema, so I wasn’t thrilled to experience this side effect.) With time, my husband was able to get used to the heat-lamp effect, as it’s not painful, just initially uncomfortable.

Within the first two weeks of continuously using the helmet, I was thrilled to see baby hairs begin to pop up around my husband's hairline. The growth continued through two months of regular use. The top of his crown had more volume and overall hair density, while new growth appeared in the more drastically affected areas near his forehead. While the additional hair didn’t completely cover his scalp, the change was stark enough for old college friends and family members to remark on how good his hair looked.

The effects aren't permanent, though. Unfortunately, my husband stopped his iRestore routine after a few months due to travel. While the iRestore Elite comes with a case that makes it super portable, my husband was nervous about misplacing it while we were on the go. (Also, the thing takes up a decent amount of space.) Unfortunately, stopping the treatments reversed the progress in a matter of weeks. Just like that, he was back to square one.

Rahman likens this phenomenon to the importance of a commitment to a healthy daily diet and regular exercise; one instance of either won't make a difference in your health. Rather, consistency is required to drive results. When I told her that my husband's hair growth had fallen off, she strongly encouraged us to pick it back up.

In addition to using the red-light therapy helmet as recommended, iRestore suggests adding thickening haircare products (shampoo, conditioner, supplements, and serum) to your regimen to get the best results. The star ingredient in the serum and shampoo is plant-based Redensyl, which is meant to fortify hair follicles during their regrowth. Additional elements—like the shampoo's castor oil and biotin, and the conditioner's vegan keratin and argan oil—act as natural strengtheners.

The supplements contain natural dihydrotestosterone (DHT) blockers derived from saw palmetto. These blockers work to prevent the enzyme (5-alpha reductase) from transforming testosterone into DHT. In essence, this hormone is a driver of pattern hair loss and can lead to general inactivity in hair growth.

The supplements, strengthening serum, shampoo, and conditioner come included with the purchase of the iRestore Elite. The extras didn't seem to boost my results but may have supported my husband's regular use of the helmet. (Full disclosure: He was also using his usual Rogaine throughout this entire process.)

Ultimately, these included products are a nice bonus, but you don't need to go out of your way to buy them from iRestore or to replenish your stash. Plenty of anti-hair-loss treatments are available, including oral and topical medications and hair-transplant procedures. All have their merits in slowing hair thinning and promoting fuller hair. Even so, “combination therapy will work better than a stand-alone," says Rahman, in response to my question about whether red-light therapy could work as an isolated treatment or if it's best to include it as part of a multicomponent regimen. Essentially, the potential benefits of red-light therapy only stand to increase when you supplement it with minoxidil and other highly researched hair-loss measures.

As for my eczema, the iRestore Elite did quell my scalp flare-ups, but I don't think I'd throw down so much money for just that. For hair regrowth, that's a totally different story. My husband was already using Rogaine before the iRestore Elite showed up. But combined with the helmet and iRestore's hair-growth products and supplements, he experienced greater results. For those pursuing thicker hair at home, the iRestore Elite is surprisingly effective.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/voters-expect-lawmakers-address-healthcare-affordability-access-poll" hreflang="en">Voters expect lawmakers to address healthcare affordability, access: poll</a>

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Voters expect lawmakers to address healthcare affordability, access: poll" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Half of voters said they are paying more this year in premiums and out-of-pocket costs, according to a new national poll.

Lake Research Partners and Bellwether Research & Consulting surveyed 1,000 voters between Oct. 20 and Oct. 28 on behalf of the National Health Council, and 50% said their premiums and out-of-pocket costs are higher this year compared to last year. Among those with commercial insurance, 63% said their premiums have risen and 60% said their out-of-pocket costs have grown year-over-year.

In addition, 25% of those surveyed said they had a claim for a prescription denied, and 24% said they waited more than two weeks for a prior authorization decision. Twenty-one percent said that they struggled to find a specialist.

Healthcare costs and the broader economy were major factors in Democrats' significant victories in the latest midterm election. And the NHC poll finds that voters expect Congress to act in addressing the costs that they face.

Most of the surveyed voters (78%) said they believe that the healthcare system warrants major changes or a complete overhaul, and 84% said they would support a candidate that would make it a priority to reform the healthcare insurance system.

However, 74% of those surveyed rated the effort from elected officials to address affordability and access as either fair or poor.

“Holding onto a plan isn’t the same as being protected by it. Patients are watching their costs rise and their coverage change, and they’re worried about what that means for their families down the road,” said Randall Rutta, CEO of the National Health Council, in an announcement. “The new Congress has a real opportunity to change that, and patients across the country will be watching.”

The survey highlights a number of potential policies that would have broad voter support. For example, 91% of those surveyed said they would support eliminating surprise medical bills and policies to require price transparency.

In addition, 90% said they would back efforts to reduce out-of-pocket costs, while 89% said they favor requiring health plans to cover treatments that were prescribed by doctors. Eighty-eight percent support efforts to increase screening and early detection for medical risks.

"Voters aren't asking lawmakers to talk about health care differently," said Christine Matthews, President of Bellwether Research and Consulting, in the release. "They want to see it work differently. Reform is a priority they expect the new Congress to deliver on."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/unpacking-implications-cms-latest-proposed-medicare-advantage-changes" hreflang="en">Unpacking the implications from CMS' latest proposed Medicare Advantage changes</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Unpacking the implications from CMS' latest proposed Medicare Advantage changes" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Late last week, the Trump administration released a proposed rule that includes a slew of technical changes in Medicare Advantage for the 2028 plan, citing enhanced competition as a key goal.

Analysts dove into the beefy regulation, and project that changes to MA's star ratings program would likely cull more than $100 billion in federal spending over the next decade.

Per a note from Leerink Partners, estimates from the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services suggest that the changes would cut federal spending by $11.5 billion in 2031 and $107 billion through 2037.

The Leerink analysts estimate that about 58% of Medicare Advantage contracts would see lower scores using the newly proposed methodology, with about 20% of contracts losing quality bonus payments, which are tied to star ratings performance.

In the rule, CMS proposed moving to a set of 15 measures to determine the star ratings scores, which are focused on member experience and outcomes. The proposed rule would also eliminate the Reward Factor, and in tandem, these changes "would remove a cushion for contracts sitting at the critical QBP threshold, while concentrating performance across fewer measures," according to the Leerink report.

The analysts said that the potential impact of these proposals "reminds us just how many plans have been living at 4 stars for years with the hugely important Reward Factor."

The report estimates that the changes to the star ratings, if finalized, would have an uneven impact on insurers. For example, Humana would see the number of contracts earning four or more stars decline from 95% to 82.9%. Clover Health, meanwhile, would likely maintain 100% of its contracts earning at least four stars.

UnitedHealthcare, by comparison, could see a drop from having 67.9% of its contracts in plans with at least four stars in 2027 to just 8.8% under the new methodology. For Aetna, the Leerink analysts project a decrease from 70% to 0%.

Given the scale of the changes, the Leerink analysts write that the rule sets the stage for a significant overhaul of the policies and rules that govern Medicare Advantage. The proposal is "almost overwhelming in size, scope and the impact on the industry."

Molly Turco, a policy analyst at TD Cowen, wrote in a note Monday that the proposed rule "is more significant than expected, in both revenue impact and scope and touches most core aspects of MA."

"It appears CMS aimed to reform long-standing concerns, especially Stars, in a way that takes money out of the program, while also loosening regulation on things like agents/brokers, seamless enrollment, network adequacy, etc. to help the program grow," Turco wrote. "However, we think these growth/deregulation policies are unlikely to offset the headwinds for MA."

She noted that while most of the most painful changes wouldn't impact plans until 2031 at the earliest, the delay does suggest that CMS may be comfortable with including more significant short-term changes to risk adjustment in the forthcoming 2028 Advance Notice, which is expected in January.

Ryan Langston, an equity research analyst at TD Cowen, wrote in the note that the proposed rule would likely be net negative for insurers with large, multi-state contracts and more people enrolled with plans earning at least four stars.

The delayed timeline of at least four years does grant plans time to adapt, Langston wrote, but the financial impacts would likely force benefit changes. The proposed rule could also spook investors concerned about what's included in the 2028 rate notice, he said.

"The combination of a smaller measure set, elimination of reward factors and reduced improvement measure protection likely lower Star ratings across the industry and force plans to mitigate lost revenue via benefit revisions," Langston wrote.

The Leerink analysts wrote that health plans are almost certain to fight the regulation. Lawsuits have become commonplace in the stars space, with multiple payers suing the feds over the most recent round of scores.

"We believe a lot of what has been proposed will change and/or not be implemented at all," they wrote. "Expect industry litigation."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/wtw-global-health-insurance-costs-set-rise-102-next-year" hreflang="en">WTW: Global health insurance costs set to rise 10.2% next year</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
WTW: Global health insurance costs set to rise 10.2% next year" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Global health insurance costs are set to rise by double digits in 2027 for the second straight year, according to a new survey.

WTW released its latest Global Medical Trends Survey, which estimates that the average gross cost for medical benefits will increase by 10.2% in 2027. This follows a 10.1% increase in 2026 and a 9.8% increase in 2025, according to the report.

In the United States, the cost for medical benefits is expected to rise by 10.3% on average, on par with 2026 and up from a 9.4% increase in 2025. Across regions, the highest increases are expected in the Asia-Pacific, averaging 12.6% in 2027.

The next-highest region was North America, with a projected 10.5% average increase.

The survey was conducted in June and August 2026 and includes responses from 393 insurers offering coverage across 92 countries. Just under half (49%) of those surveyed said they anticipate elevated medical and pharmacy costs to impact them over the next three years, and likely persist beyond that window.

The WTW analysts said this likely means that insurers will remain focused on cost containment and sharing measures in the near future.

"In the US, healthcare costs remain under sustained pressure from new medical technologies, specialty pharmaceuticals, and the rising use of GLP-1 therapies, alongside higher provider costs and the ongoing demands of chronic and complex conditions," said Courtney Stubblefield, strategy and specialty leader in Health & Benefits at WTW. in an announcement.

More than three quarters (76%) of the surveyed insurers named new medical technologies as the leading cost drive for both medical and pharmaceutical costs. Sixty-five percent cited provider fee increases, and 45% named pharmaceutical advancements.

Cancer was the leading condition driving costs, the survey found, with 62% naming it as the top cost condition. Sixty-one percent of those surveyed said they have seen an increase in cancer diagnoses among individuals under age 40 when compared to the past two years.

However, when looking at the conditions where costs are growing the most quickly, cardiovascular disease rises to No. 1, cited by 54% of the surveyed insurers. Cancer was second on this ranking, and named by 51%, while diabetes was third at 28%.

"To manage this, employers need a multiyear, multifaceted strategy that addresses pharmacy spending and high-cost conditions—such as cancer, cardiovascular disease and diabetes—while keeping care affordable and accessible for employees," Stubblefield said.

By 2040, 51% of People Living with HIV Could Be Over 50

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Advances in antiretroviral therapy (ART) have helped people living with HIV live longer, contributing to a growing population of older adults with the virus.

In fact, a recent report published in The Lancet HIV projects that approximately 51% of people living with HIV worldwide will be over age 50 by 2040. An estimated 96% of these older adults will live in low- and middle-income countries.

The report’s authors emphasized the “pressing need” for HIV prevention and screening services tailored to older adults, who face additional age-related challenges when accessing care.

These challenges include stigma related to both age and HIV, a higher burden of chronic health conditions, taking multiple medications, delayed diagnoses, physical limitations, and increased financial and social vulnerability.

Factors like these “influence HIV risk for older adults in complex and dynamic ways,” according to the report authors.

Several factors have contributed to the growing population of older adults living with HIV, including:

Many adults over age 50 who are living with HIV acquired the virus during young adulthood in the late 1990s and early 2000s. During that period, some experienced prolonged periods of uncontrolled HIV and exposure to older ART medications with more severe side effects, which may have contributed to long-term inflammation and other health complications.

William Schaffner, MD, professor in the Division of Infectious Diseases at Vanderbilt University, who wasn’t involved in the report, told Healthline, “That older persons with HIV are on track to outnumber younger persons with HIV infection is a double-barreled success story.”

“First, the effective treatment of persons with HIV infection has resulted in the expectation of normal or near-normal lifespans; thus, more older persons living with HIV infection,” he said. “This is a great success; both in creating effective, easy-to-use medicines as well as developing medical care systems that can reach such patients and provide lifetime care.”

Schaffner said the second success has been educating younger people about HIV prevention, helping reduce new infections.

Older adults also account for a growing proportion of new HIV diagnoses. They are more likely to receive a diagnosis later in the course of infection, which can delay treatment and increase the risk of serious health complications and death.

Mathematical models project that the average age of people living with HIV worldwide will increase from 41 in 2025 to 50 by 2040, largely driven by demographic shifts in low- and middle-income countries.

While the World Health Organization (WHO) does not specify an upper age limit for HIV testing in its published overview, U.S. guidelines focus on specific age groups.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends routine HIV testing for people ages 13 to 64, and the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends testing for people ages 15 to 65.

However, these age limits don’t mean older adults shouldn’t be tested for HIV, as both U.S. organizations also recommend testing older adults who may be at risk.

Older adults can still be sexually active and have a chance of acquiring HIV. Certain physical changes that come with age, such as thinning of vaginal tissue, may also increase the risk of HIV infection.

Despite these risks, older adults are often overlooked in HIV prevention campaigns, research, and clinical trials. The report’s authors say more needs to be done to address these gaps.

“Targeted and tailored HIV prevention and screening services for older adults are a pressing global need,” the report authors stated.

Schaffner agreed with this and added, “Increasing the effectiveness of diagnosis and treatment of HIV infection in older adults will increase their expectation of a healthier, longer life.”

The report recommends several approaches to improving HIV prevention and screening among older adults, including:

While Schaffner noted that HIV treatment has “advanced spectacularly over the past 20 years,” making it possible for people living with HIV to lead longer, healthier lives, access to these treatments remains a challenge in many parts of the world.

If You Can't Do These 5 Things, Your Divorce Will Be Hellish

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 22, 2026 | Reviewed by Ekua Hagan

Jared hated his soon-to-be ex, Ashley, more than words could say. In his eyes, she'd used him for his money, cheated on him with his best friend, Miguel, and then dumped him like garbage. He was hurt, betrayed, and furious. Who wouldn't be?

Nan never expected her marriage to end. She'd always been anxious, but now the bottom had fallen out of her world. Had he ever loved her? Would the kids be traumatized? Would she lose the house—the one place she felt safe? Would she have enough money to live on? Could she end up homeless? Would she have a breakdown like her mother did?

Sean felt nothing when his husband left a note saying he'd moved out and wasn't coming back. He sat in front of the TV for days, barely eating or getting off the couch. Friends called, but he let everything go to voicemail. He was emotionally numb.

Although these reactions may seem extreme, they're not unusual after a sudden split or an ugly breakup. Normal, that is, in the beginning.

Divorce is one of the toughest transitions anyone will go through. Even under the best circumstances, it can destabilize your home, finances, parenting, friendships, and sense of identity.

Looking at divorce through Maslow's hierarchy of needs, many folks feel knocked down to the two lowest levels: basic survival and safety. The higher-functioning levels of love, esteem, and self-actualization take a back seat.

When you're dividing assets, potentially doubling your expenses, and perhaps splitting time with your kids, you're understandably more focused on getting through today than on what you might gain tomorrow.

The amygdala is a small structure deep within the brain that helps detect threats and trigger emotional responses. It's part of a larger system involved in our fight, flight, or freeze reactions.

Jared's rage, Nan's fear, and Sean's shutdown illustrate different responses to overwhelming stress. Clients have told me they feel like their big emotions mean they're "going crazy." I tell them they're probably not going crazy. They're being stretched emotionally, sometimes beyond anything they've experienced before.

Ideally, as the initial shock wears off, rage softens into anger, terror becomes manageable fear, and numbness begins to thaw. But not everyone moves out of survival mode easily—especially if the loss triggers previous unresolved losses or traumas.

Fortunately, another part of the brain can help: the prefrontal cortex.

Located just behind your forehead, it helps with reasoning, planning, impulse control, and thinking through actions and their consequences. It’s the part of the brain that asks, "Is this really a good idea?" “Should I say exactly what’s on my mind right now?”

Under intense stress, these skills can become compromised. You're more likely to react impulsively, see things in black-and-white terms, and seek immediate relief, rather than consider long-term consequences.

The amygdala and prefrontal cortex work together, but when our threat-response system is on high alert, thoughtful decisions become harder.

That's why people sometimes do things during divorce they'd never dream of doing otherwise. They send vicious texts, threaten to take the kids away, spend thousands fighting over possessions worth far less, or make financial decisions that hurt them more than their spouse.

These actions may feel justified in the moment. But there's a difference between reacting to pain and making decisions that serve you.

Just because your spouse sends an inflammatory text doesn't mean you have to answer immediately—or at all.

Your first impulse may be to defend yourself or fire back. Don't. Unless there's an emergency, give yourself time to settle down. Sometimes an hour is enough. Sometimes you need a day.

2. Distinguish immediate emotional discharge from long-term benefit.

There can be something satisfying about telling your spouse exactly what you think of them or making sure they don't "get away with" what they've done.

But that satisfaction is usually short-lived. The consequences may not be.

Ask yourself: "Will this help me build the life I want after divorce, or am I just trying to feel better right now?"

3. Consider your children's needs without disregarding your own.

Some parents become so consumed with their hurt that they lose sight of what their children are experiencing. Others sacrifice their own well-being because they believe putting the kids first means putting themselves last.

Neither extreme is helpful. Children need stability and emotional safety. They also benefit from parents who take care of themselves.

I've seen people spend more on attorney fees fighting over furniture than the furniture was worth. I know of one wealthier couple who spent thousands of dollars fighting over cleaning products (I couldn't make that up)!

At that point, it's clearly not about the item being fought over as much as it is about feeling wronged, wanting control, or needing to win.

Before digging in your heels, ask: "Will this matter five years from now?"

Some things absolutely are worth fighting for, including safety, your children's well-being, and financial security. But not every disagreement deserves a battle.

5. Be willing to compromise without abandoning yourself.

One of the hardest things about divorce is accepting that you probably won't get everything you want. In some cases, you'll feel like your spouse is getting more than they deserve. It can perpetuate, or even exacerbate, your pain.

Ask yourself if you really want to keep fighting, or do you want to be done so you can move on? That's not to suggest that you should surrender or let your spouse take advantage of you. It means recognizing that a workable resolution may be better for your long-term mental health than winning every argument.

Just a note about safety: When abuse, coercion, or intimidation is involved, safety and legal protection come first.

You're not going to feel calm and rational every minute. Divorce hurts. Sometimes terribly. You're allowed to be angry, frightened, or devastated. The problem isn't having these feelings. It's what you do with them.

Here's how to engage your higher brain: Before taking action, ask yourself:

"Am I trying to solve a problem, protect myself, or punish someone?"

Sometimes you're protecting yourself, and that's appropriate. Other times, you may realize you want your spouse to hurt as much as you do. That's human, too. But acting on that impulse rarely gets you where you want to go.

You may not control your spouse's behavior or how the divorce unfolds. But you can recognize when you're operating from fear, anger, or overwhelm.

The goal isn't to divorce without feeling deeply. It's to make sure temporary emotions don't create permanent consequences.

After all, divorce isn't the last chapter of your life. You still have to live with your decisions long after it's over. The intense feelings you feel are in your body, not theirs. How long do you want to live with those emotions hijacking your pleasure and taking away your power? Practice letting go daily. Join a support group. Get into individual therapy. Read helpful books. Meditate. Your power is in where you are headed, not where you've been. Keep moving forward.

When We Forgive, What New Meaning Do We Find in Life?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

Suffering could lead to despair, depression, hopelessness, and even not liking oneself (Yu et al. 2021; Reed & Enright, 2006). Yet one aspect of the forgiveness process, especially as one nears the end of the forgiveness journey, is the sometimes surprising development of finding meaning in that suffering and a new purpose in life. Finding meaning centers on an important question: What have I learned from going through this suffering, and what have I learned as I walked the journey of forgiveness? This is a cognitive exercise that can lead to new short-term and long-term goals (Enright, 2015). In other words, the cognitive exercise of finding meaning can lead to behaviors that support new purposes in one’s life.

The psychotherapist and Holocaust survivor, Viktor Frankl (1969/2014), pioneered this idea of finding meaning in suffering as he endured the persecution of his imprisonment. His motivation was to resist being destroyed by the abuse. By finding meaning, one can transcend suffering and make it more bearable.

When I discuss the theme of finding meaning in suffering through forgiveness, I am not focusing on the injustice but on its aftermath. The basic question is this: What are you learning about life as you extend mercy to those who were deeply unfair to you? This post explores the different meanings people tend to generate as they forgive.

Let us first start by examining what finding meaning in suffering is not, because it can be misunderstood.

Consider three themes that sometimes are confused with finding meaning in what an unjustly acting person has done to the forgiver.

First, the forgiver is not denying the disappointment, sadness, anger, or grief in what was suffered. The injustice did happen, and the negative response is real. In other words, finding meaning is not some kind of non-feeling, interpreted, at least temporarily, as relief from the effects of the injustice.

Second, the person does not play games with themselves, stating, for example, “Oh well, things happen for a reason, and I’ll just make the best of it by accepting it.” Yes, people can “make the best of it,” but if this is an exclusive meaning of suffering, the one who forgives is not going after the woundedness inside, which may linger for a long time. The “oh well” approach is passive. To heal from the effects of the injustice, a person needs a more active approach to the pain.

Third, the forgiver does not distort reality by thinking, “Well, all things happen for good reasons, and so I will try to see the good in what happened to me.” The injustice was not good. Some aspects of what happens after the injustice may be good, but the injustice itself is not.

1. Being more sensitive to the suffering of others, even those who never hurt you: James, upon forgiving, realized he had what he called a “short temper,” in which he imposed his anger on unsuspecting others. He has now committed, for example, to being more patient and kind to cashiers in grocery stores and pharmacies as he waits in line. He is more sensitive to the fatigue and stress of those working behind the cash register. This gives him a new sense of purpose, as he recognizes his intemperance in these contexts and now works to overcome his anger.

2. Being motivated to help others in their suffering: When Dr. Freedman (Freedman & Enright, 1996) worked with female incest survivors on a long-term forgiveness intervention, she saw that many of them, upon forgiving, became motivated to help other incest survivors overcome their deep resentment. They were not choosing as a goal to become mental health professionals, but instead to be of assistance to others who have suffered as they have.

As another example, I talked with a man who is imprisoned for life. Upon forgiving those who abused him, he said to me that he knows he will never get out of the correctional institution, but now he is motivated to help his cellmates forgive and to be unburdened of their anger. This led to a new purpose, or the behavioral manifestation of this desire to assist others.

3. Standing in the pain so you do not throw that pain onto others: The paradox of standing in the pain is this: As people bear the negative effects of unjust treatment by not throwing that pain back to the offending person or to unsuspecting others, the pain-bearers realize they are stronger than they thought. They see the positive outcomes of not seeking revenge. As Gandhi said, if we keep taking an eye for an eye, the whole world will be blind. The pain-bearers realize they can now translate this into a new purpose of contributing to a more peaceful world.

4. Forgiving helps us think more deeply about justice: If people are continually treated unjustly, they can find meaning in living a life of mercy and fairness integrated together. Thus, their understanding of justice can become wiser, and therefore their appropriation of justice can become more helpful to others.

5. Eventually, you can develop an overall philosophy of life, such as committing to be a conduit of good in this world: This is what Dr. Frankl called supra-meaning, or a new philosophy of life that encompasses an overall approach to how one interacts with others and why one does so in this way. For example, a person can commit to living a life of greater respect, kindness, and love toward both offenders and others. For example, Sally decided to resist impatience and negative emotions toward her own children, as a way of life in her family, so that these life-giving moral virtues can become norms there.

6. As people find meaning in their suffering, they can put their future purpose in life into sharper perspective: Finding meaning, in other words, becomes the cognitive rationale and emotional motivation to seek what matters to them.

In summary, our suffering is not for nothing. People who suffer and forgive can develop as they think carefully about what they are learning about suffering, their own humanity, and life itself. As Dr. Frankl learned and passed his wisdom on to us, finding meaning in suffering opens unexpected doors for survival and growing in our humanity.

Enright, R.D. (2015). 8 keys to forgiveness. Norton.

Frankl, V. (1969/2014). The will to meaning: Foundations and applications of logotherapy. Plume.

Freedman, S. R., & Enright, R. D. (1996). Forgiveness as an intervention goal with incest survivors. Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology, 64(5), 983-992.

Reed, G. & Enright, R.D. (2006). The effects of forgiveness therapy on depression, anxiety, and post-traumatic stress for women after spousal emotional abuse. Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology, 74, 920-929.

Yu, L., Gambaro, M., Song, J., Teslik, M., Komoski, M.C., Wollner, B., & Enright, R.D. (2021). Forgiveness therapy in a maximum-security correctional institution: A randomized clinical trial. Clinical Psychology and Psychotherapy, 28, 1457-1471.

Cardiovascular Screening Test Called Into Question

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

— MedPage Today reporters offer further insights into recently covered topics

The following is a transcript of the podcast episode:

Rachael Robertson: Hey everybody! Welcome to MedPod Today, the podcast series where MedPage Today reporters take a deeper look into the week's biggest healthcare stories. I'm your host, Rachael Robertson.

Cheryl Clark is kicking off today's episode with her reporting on an ongoing debate on whether to test all adults for Lipoprotein(a). Then, Kristen Monaco will fill us in on major developments in the GLP-1 medication space. To close out, I'll give us a rundown on the latest measles numbers and what it means that the U.S. lost its measles elimination status after 26 years.

A March recommendation from the American College of Cardiology (ACC) and 10 other medical societies for the first time said all adults should have their blood tested for something called Lipoprotein(a), also called Lp(a) or "little a," which is a risk factor you may have never heard of. But many cardiologists and primary care doctors are strongly resisting doing this. Cheryl Clark is here to tell us more.

Cheryl Clark: Thanks, Rachael. Yeah, "little a" – this is a component in blood that is believed to be strongly associated with one's risk of getting an earlier cardiovascular event or aortic stenosis. A growing number of studies seem to show that. But it's genetically inherited, so the score you get basically stays the same throughout your whole life. Diet, exercise, lowering low-density lipoprotein (LDL), body mass index (BMI), they don't change it. So for a long time, doctors said there was really nothing they could do about it. So why measure it?

Scores, it turns out, that are higher than 125 nanomoles per liter might just scare people, especially those without established disease or symptoms. And some doctors are reporting otherwise healthy patients, some even in their 70s, with scores in the high 300s or even higher. So it seems like a high score is survivable, but it is scary.

Now, European cardiology associations in 2022 and again in 2025 began to rethink this. The idea was that if you knew your score, you could change other elements in your life that raise your risk, like trying harder to lose weight. I mean, like really try harder to lower LDL, blood pressure, improve your diet, exercise, do all of that stuff.

The American groups, including the ACC and the American Heart Association, in March joined in. They issued similar recommendations in a 134-page document, basically agreeing with the Europeans. So now this is a standard recommendation that everybody, all adults, should get this at least once in their lifetime.

It might be that their thinking was rooted in the fact that now pharmaceutical companies have been developing drugs that lower this "little a," but they needed clinical trials to prove it. They also needed to prove that it would prevent heart disease and strokes, which of course is the point. Why would we do this otherwise? There are at least three drugs in development, so now lots of smart people are pretty darn sure they'd be successful with this drug and save lots of lives. So a few weeks ago, the first drug trial for a drug called pelacarsen was released, but the results were shockingly disappointing.

Clark: Well, it was called the HORIZON study. It was a large placebo-controlled trial, and it found no reduced risk in these cardiovascular events. It tested pelacarsen, which did lower "little a," but participants were still getting diagnosed and dying. So there is something else clearly in play that's causing heart disease. Maybe Lp(a) is a marker for something else, or maybe something else is protecting some people. Who knows?

So I asked a lot of doctors, were they ordering these tests? Especially now that we have the HORIZON results, and several said they were. Some said they were just ordering these tests in people with established familial linkages, like genetic predisposition. But many said they vehemently opposed such a universal recommendation or aren't ordering it at all. My own doctor orders it, but said other doctors in her office practice are not.

One cardiologist said otherwise healthy patients are seeing their high scores online in their portals and are freaking out, crying, and thinking they're going to die tomorrow, and they have to spend time reassuring them. One physician told me the committee should have prioritized calcium CT scoring instead of "little a." He thinks CT scoring shows real disease that can be treated. And Rita Redberg, MD, MSc, a prominent cardiologist at UCSF – she's known for less is more approach – called "little a" a useless test.

And by the way, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services has not joined the bandwagon on testing for "little a." It allows regional Medicare jurisdictions across the country to make case by case determinations about whether to cover the tests. So some beneficiaries are going to get stuck with paying for this out-of-pocket.

Robertson: It sounds like there's still a lot of confusion.

Clark: Yes, the bottom line seems to be: live a healthy life. If you get a high score, be thankful and adhere to strategies that we know reduce your LDL and your other risk factors. We'll see in a few years if any of these other three drugs now in clinical trials clarify this complicated issue.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for your reporting here, Cheryl.

Robertson: Pediatric obesity took center stage at ObesityWeek in National Harbor, Maryland. There were two major phase III trials showing strong weight loss results for GLP-1 medications in kids and teens. This was just days after the FDA added a new vision-related safety warning across the entire drug class. Kristen Monaco is here to break down the latest developments.

So, Kristen, starting with the adolescent data from ObesityWeek, what did the SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS trial show for tirzepatide?

Kristen Monaco: SURMOUNT-ADOLESCENTS was a phase III trial simultaneously published in JAMA that looked at the very popular dual GLP-1/GIP receptor agonist tirzepatide, also known as Zepbound, in 160 adolescents ages 12 to 17 with overweight or obesity. All participants also received lifestyle intervention. At week 72, teens who were taking tirzepatide saw an average BMI reduction of 18.5%, compared with a 0.5% increase in those taking placebo. Nearly half on tirzepatide achieved at least a 20% reduction in BMI, and over half were no longer considered to have obesity by the end of the trial.

Notably, researchers also saw improvements in quality of life and physical comfort scores, with no signals of disruption to growth or pubertal maturation, which is a concern of using these agents in this age group. Tirzepatide is currently only approved for adults with obesity or kids 10 and older with type 2 diabetes. So these data lay the groundwork for potential obesity indication in teens. However, rival GLP drug semaglutide is already approved for teens with obesity.

Robertson: Those are really impressive results. Researchers also presented data looking at even younger children, those ages six to 11. What did the STEP Young trial find for semaglutide?

Monaco: STEP Young, which was published in the New England Journal of Medicine, looked at semaglutide, also known as Wegovy, in 167 children, ages 6 to 11, with obesity alongside lifestyle counseling. Right now, semaglutide is only approved for obesity in patients ages 12 and up. There are no GLP-1 therapies right now that are FDA approved for this very young age group. So treatment options are largely limited to lifestyle modifications.

In the trial at week 68, semaglutide reduced BMI by 13.5%, and nearly 40% of children on the injectable no longer had obesity by the end of the trial. Children on treatment also saw improvements in cardiometabolic markers like waist circumference, triglycerides, and blood pressure. Gastrointestinal side effects like vomiting, diarrhea, and nausea were more common, especially during the dose escalation phase, which is expected with GLP-1 drugs. Though they were mostly mild to moderate and transient, with no safety signals affecting growth or pubertal development. Drug maker Novo Nordisk hasn't announced yet whether it plans to submit these data to the FDA, so that will be something to look closely for.

Robertson: That's super interesting. So stepping back from your conference coverage, you also reported on a major regulatory update involving GLP-1 drugs and eye health. What is this FDA warning, and what led to it?

Monaco: Yes, so the FDA is now requiring manufacturers of all currently marketed GLP-1 medications to include a warning about the potential risk of non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy, also known as NAION, which is a rare but potentially blinding eye condition caused by reduced blood flow to the optic disc. This update follows a wave of observational studies, including a 2024 JAMA Ophthalmology study on semaglutide, that prompted this FDA review. The agency concluded from its review that there is indeed a rare but potentially increased risk of NAION events in patients taking GLP-1 drugs. Cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products.

So the FDA told healthcare providers to tell their patients to report any sudden, painless vision loss or visual field changes in one eye immediately, and then to refer them promptly to an ophthalmologist. They should also consider discontinuing the GLP-1 drug unless the treatment benefits clearly outweigh the risks.

Robertson: Well, thank you so much for this important context as these medications continue to expand into younger populations. Thanks for being here, Kristen.

Robertson: The U.S. has officially lost its measles elimination status after 26 years. It's no surprise, considering there have been at least 830 hospitalizations and 13 measles-associated deaths in the past 2 years.

Losing this status is a big deal. Our editor-in-chief, Jeremy Faust, MD, sat down with pediatric infectious disease expert Paul Offit, MD, of Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, and public health researcher Noel Brewer, PhD, of the University of North Carolina in Chapel Hill. They talked about the resurgence of measles outbreaks, declining vaccination rates, timing of the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, the influence of vaccine misinformation, and the challenges facing public health officials as they work to restore confidence in vaccination and prevent further transmission. It's a lot, but I really recommend you listen to the full conversation. We've linked it here.

Last week, there were 334 cases of measles, and there were 319 the week before that. Pennsylvania is still really struggling with more than 100 cases in the last 2 weeks, but things are also really heating up in Wisconsin, which had more than 200 cases in that same time period. The other states that we're keeping an extra close eye on are New York, Indiana, and Minnesota. With holiday travel coming up, we'll see in the coming weeks how that influences the spread of measles.

As always, check out our interactive measles map, which is updated at the top of each week, and we've linked it right here.

Grand Theft Auto VI Welcomes a New Wave of (Virtual) Gym Bros

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After a 13-year drought, Grand Theft Auto VI has finally hit the masses. Predictably, a large swath of guys have called off work to give themselves just enough time to explore the immersive fictional land of Leonida, where players can do just about everything they can in real life—from driving cars and renovating homes to playing video games within a video game. In Grand Theft Auto VI, that now also includes hitting the gym. And if you want to progress through the story as efficiently as possible, becoming an in-game gym rat might be the best thing you can do.

Expanding on a gameplay mechanic introduced in Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas, working out in GTA levels up your character to help with progressing through the story. The fitter you become in the game, the more stamina you have to sprint, the more health you have before you’re Wasted, and the more focus you have to take down enemies. Just like in real life, pumping iron makes daily life a whole lot easier.

To get fit, you can use the weights in Jason’s outdoor gym, which features the big three full-body lifts: squats, bench presses, and deadlifts. While your character handles the heavy lifting, all you have to do is carefully time your button presses to complete reps without causing an injury. Once you have enough money, you can purchase memberships at gyms across the city, giving you access to treadmills, free weights, and workout machines. The exercise options are so extensive (you can even do zone 2 cardio) that gamers have already started trading tips to the best GTA 6 workouts for maximizing your character’s gains.

As your character gets stronger, their avatar transforms accordingly, developing more defined muscles, a six-pack, and even a chiseled face. Neglecting your fitness and overeating (a real thing you can do in the game) has an alternate effect as your player visibly gains weight and adopts a “dad bod.”

Inevitably there will be many players that skip the gym this weekend so they can pump a little virtual iron with their thumbs. There’s something undeniably ironic about looksmaxxing your GTA character while spending hours sitting on the couch playing a video game. But don’t think that just because it’s all on a screen that you won’t walk away from a session with a little back pain, so maybe schedule a few breaks in between. You could even build a home gym of your own like Jason’s—it’s already got one fitness expert’s approval.

A Deep Dive Into Brittany Snow and Hunter Moreno’s Increasingly Public Relationship

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Yes, Brittany finally confirmed that they’re engaged.

In case you missed it: The Hunting Wives star Brittany Snow is engaged to photographer and creative director Hunter Moreno, and if the news took you by complete surprise due to having literally no idea she was dating anyone, you’re not alone. Their relationship is extremely low-key—like, so low-key that they’ve only been photographed together on one or two occasions, and the only reason we, the people, first learned about their engagement is because Claire Danes was caught asking about it on a red carpet.

That said, there are some cute details to go over, so keep reading for a full timeline of Brittany and Hunter’s relationship!
