Why "Face-to-Face Only" Might Be Costing You Good Therapy

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Tyler Woods

I’ve been noticing a trend, a shift, across psychology and therapy platforms. It’s the “only face-to-face” request. “In person only,” “No telehealth,” from referral requests to consumers, I see it everywhere.

Look, I get it. I truly get it. Prior to COVID, I swore I would never do telehealth. Only in rare cases, where it was the only way someone would get support, would I even consider it.

So, understandably, the start of COVID was not an easy transition for me. Lockdowns meant that those rare cases where I would consider it now applied to everyone, and people needed support more than ever. So, like many of my colleagues, I did the training, dived deep on what was possible, and did the best I could. And what I found were... results. It was not the terrible outcome I had anticipated, but more than that, it was not just something to hold the fort until I could get back to ‘real therapy’. It worked. So, post-COVID, I started doing more, until now, full disclaimer: I run a telehealth-only practice, so it’s fair to say I may hold some bias in this space.

I also get why the pendulum has swung. Post-COVID, we're living in a world where a lot of us are lacking face-to-face connections. We spent years on screens, we worked on screens, we saw our families on screens, our kids went to school on screens, we threw our birthday parties on, yup, screens. So it makes sense that we've come out the other side and decided, maybe rightly so, that online interactions just aren't as good. Here’s this thing, I back this, we absolutely do need face-to-face interactions. But this is where we get stuck in black-and-white thinking. Online bad; in-person good. Rather than leveraging screen time to buy us more face-to-face in the spaces that matter most.

But you folks know me, let’s check what the evidence actually says about it all.

Telehealth therapy has actually been shown to be as effective as face-to-face therapy. A 2021 meta-analysis comparing live video therapy with in-person therapy found that therapy is no less effective when it's delivered by video, with the strongest results for CBT for depression and anxiety. The authors concluded that video therapy isn't just the convenient choice, it's one now backed by evidence. A 2025 meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials in children, adolescents and their parents found the same thing. Video therapy was as effective as in-person treatment, both straight after treatment and at follow-up.

This was seen in couples' work too. A study of over 1,100 married clients in couple therapy found teletherapy was as effective as in-person therapy for outcomes including relationship satisfaction, sexual satisfaction and the therapeutic alliance.

The big worry I hear, from clinicians especially, is about the therapeutic relationship. Can you really connect through a screen? It's a fair question, and the honest answer from what I have found is that the alliance holds up well. A review of studies on this found a good working alliance and good outcomes across all of them, and outcomes that were equivalent to face-to-face. Now, the alliance scores sat a touch lower on average, and the research isn't conclusive yet that they're fully equal. But the outcomes held up.

This is the big bit I think we're missing. If what we're craving is more in-person connection, telehealth might actually be part of how we get it. It potentially allows us more time to engage in person in the situations that are more meaningful.

We can fit our therapy around our work and our lives, and save that important face-to-face time for family, friends, and community. The hour you'd have spent driving across town, finding parking, sitting in a waiting room and driving home again? That could be dinner with your kids. A walk with a friend. Actually turning up to the thing you keep saying you'll go to. This is no way going to be the case for everyone, but again, if we get stuck in that black-and-white thinking that one is superior to the other, then those who would actually benefit end up missing out.

The other thing to consider, particularly when we look at sex therapy, is that there's some emerging evidence it might actually be easier for us to talk about things like sex, and those topics that feel vulnerable and exposing, when we have this perceived barrier or distance between us.

Psychologist John Suler described this back in 2004 as the online disinhibition effect: some people self-disclose more often, or more intensely, online than they would in person. We usually talk about the toxic side of that, the trolls and the pile-ons. But Suler also described the flip side, where people share more openly and ask for help more freely.

We see a version of this in sexual health research, too. Studies comparing computer-based questionnaires with face-to-face interviews consistently find people report more sensitive sexual behaviours when there's a screen rather than a person in front of them. In one survey from the Cleveland Clinic, 44% of men said they'd prefer to talk to their doctor about sexual health problems online or by phone because they were too embarrassed to do it face-to-face.

Back to COVID, when sexologists in Portugal were surveyed about moving their work online during lockdown, many reported that their clients felt less exposed, with fewer psychological and emotional barriers to get past. So being at home could ease some of the tension about opening up and remove some of the barriers when it comes to getting help for sexual issues. Some also said it made it easier for clients to talk about gender and sexual orientation, with less stigma. That tracks with what I see in my day-to-day too.

Absolute disclaimer: None of this means telehealth is right for everyone, or every time. Some people don't have a private space at home. Some couples find it hard to get uninterrupted time together without kids. Some people need the ritual of leaving the house and going somewhere. And yes, sometimes that disinhibition means people share a lot, quickly, and, as therapists, we need to pace that with care.

But "face-to-face only" as a blanket rule? That's the black-and-white thinking I’m not so sure about. It might be ruling out a therapist who's exactly right for you. It might be ruling out the very format that would make it easier to say the hard thing. Like most things when it comes to psychology, I suggest we move into a space of curiosity rather than good/bad and make a choice that fits best for individual needs.

To find a therapist near you, visit the Psychology Today Therapy Directory.

Bradford, A. B., Johnson, L. N., Anderson, S. R., Banford-Witting, A., Hunt, Q. A., Miller, R. B., & Bean, R. A. (2024). Call me maybe? In-person vs. teletherapy outcomes among married couples. Psychotherapy Research, 34(5), 611–625. https://doi.org/10.1080/10503307.2023.2256465

Costa, I. B., Manão, A. A., & Pascoal, P. M. (2023). Clinical sexologists' perceptions of the potentials, downfalls, and best practices for digitally delivered therapy. Behavioral Sciences, 13(5), 376. https://www.mdpi.com/2076-328x/13/5/376

Cleveland Clinic survey via Sexual Health Matters (ISSM): https://www.sexhealthmatters.org/patients/news/new-survey-reveals-men-prefer-telemedicine

Two-Thirds of Dementia Content On TikTok Is Misleading, Researchers Say

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A new survey suggests that about two-thirds of dementia advice videos on TikTok are misleading

Of the videos examined, the analysis shows that nearly half contained inaccurate and potentially damaging information.

The researchers say that approximately 44% of TikTok creators who share dementia content lack credibility and appropriate qualifications.

Although misleading dementia content may appear on other social media platforms, this research examined only TikTok content, warranting further investigation.

The survey was commissioned by Homewatch Caregivers and has not been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

A spokesperson for TikTok told Healthline that if health-related content is potentially harmful, it is flagged for removal. The spokesperson added that 99.6% of misinformation deemed violative was proactively removed from the platform in 2026.

The spokesperson added that TikTok relies on fact-checking and guidance from public health authorities to help assess the accuracy of content. The platform may also apply warning labels to content that cannot be verified as accurate.

Looking to social media for health information is not uncommon. A recent poll reports that 31% of adults in the United States use social media at least once a month to look for health information and advice.

Experts advise seeking information about dementia from trusted sources, such as a primary care doctor and organizations like the National Institute on Aging (NIA) and the Alzheimer’s Association, rather than social media, where content creators who share advice often lack expertise.

“It’s normal to want answers quickly when you’re caring for someone living with dementia, and a short video from someone who seems to know what they’re talking about can be really convincing,” said lead researcher Jennifer Winarski, senior manager of care operations and quality for Homewatch CareGivers.

“Despite there being some genuinely helpful information out there, you don’t always know whether it’s backed by medical evidence, so we wanted to bring our own expertise to uncover whether popular TikToks can be trusted,” she told Healthline. “We hope our report will encourage people to be a bit more careful about what they act on.”

For the survey, researchers selected a sample of 500 of the most-watched dementia-related videos on TikTok with hashtags such as:

Researchers then transcribed the video data to assess its accuracy.

The researchers found that two-thirds (66.4%) of the popular TikTok videos contained misleading information. Of those videos, about half (47.6%) contained potentially damaging misleading dementia information.

“It’s clear that many of the creators mean well, but much of what we saw was personal opinion rather than proven information, and in a few cases, general good health tips were presented as if they were specifically helpful for individuals living with dementia,” Winarski said.

“If families act on that, they could end up spending time and money on things that won’t help — and in the worst of cases, it could delay getting the support their loved one really needs,” she added.

Dung Trinh, MD, an internist for MemorialCare Medical Group and chief medical officer of the Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, said it’s important to be aware of the volume of misleading dementia information on social media. Trinh was not involved in this survey.

Some people may be making decisions about their health based on incomplete, misleading, or incorrect information, he said.

“For example, someone might see a video claiming that a particular supplement can reverse Alzheimer’s disease or that certain memory problems automatically mean someone has dementia,” Trinh said.

“These messages can lead to unnecessary fear, false reassurance, wasted money, or delays in getting a proper diagnosis.”

“We are entering an exciting era in Alzheimer’s care, with advances in blood-based biomarkers, earlier detection, and treatments that may slow disease progression in appropriately selected patients,” Trinh continued.

“Getting an accurate diagnosis and receiving timely medical guidance are becoming increasingly important.”

The researchers examined the content creators behind the TikTok videos included in their analysis. They found that 44% of video creators behind the misleading videos had no relevant qualifications.

Rehan Aziz, MD, vice chair of education in the Department of Psychiatry at Hackensack Meridian Jersey Shore University Medical Center and associate professor of psychiatry and neurology at the Hackensack Meridian School of Medicine in New Jersey, said that content creators offering dementia advice should have relevant qualifications. Aziz wasn’t involved in the survey.

“Start by looking at the creator’s profile for a full name and a specific credential, such as MD, DO, PhD, or RN, along with a specialty,” Aziz told Healthline.

“For dementia, the most relevant experts include neurologists, geriatric psychiatrists, geriatricians, neuropsychologists, and nurses, including advanced practice nurses, or social workers who specialize in dementia care.”

“You can confirm a doctor’s license through your state’s licensing board website and check board certification at certificationmatters.org,” Aziz said.

As of 2020, more than 55 million people worldwide were living with dementia — a neurological condition that negatively affects the brain. Researchers predict this number will hit 78 million by 2030.

The increase in the number of people seeking dementia content online is a reflection of this trend.

When looking for accurate dementia information and guidance, Nestor Beltre, MD, a neurologist with Baptist Health Kenneth C. Griffin Neuroscience Institute, part of Baptist Health South Florida, said a great first step is to speak with their primary care physician. Beltre wasn’t involved in the survey.

“Memory changes can occur for many different reasons, and some causes, including vitamin deficiencies, depression, and certain medical conditions, may be treatable,” he told Healthline.

“Early evaluation can help identify what may be contributing to these changes and determine the most appropriate next steps. Depending on the individual’s symptoms, a neurologist, psychiatrist, or neuropsychologist can provide further evaluation and guidance,” Belter continued.

Beltre advised turning to trusted resources such as the Alzheimer’s Association and the National Institute on Aging, which can increase public understanding of memory-related conditions and available treatment options.

“The most important message is that patients and families have resources available to help them better understand and manage cognitive health,” he said.

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Why One Big Meal Isn’t the Real Holiday Health Problem

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In the classic Thanksgiving comedy Planes, Trains and Automobiles, hapless hero Del Griffith (played by the late, great John Candy) delivers an emotional riposte to Steve Martin’s uptight ad man Neal Page that stands as a beautifully dignified defense of kindness, empathy and self-love in the face of his own perceived shortcomings.

“You want to hurt me? Go right ahead if it makes you feel any better…I could be a cold, hard cynic like you. But I don’t like to hurt people’s feelings. You think what you want about me, I’m not changing. I… I like me. My wife likes me…because I’m the real article. What you see is what you get.”

For many of us around the holidays, particularly after a night of excess, Del and Neal’s exchange could read as our internal monologue – your kinder inner voice battling the internal bully who judges your every overindulgence. The jury’s out on whether the kind voice is ever as potent as Del’s is in that moment. But it should be, because when it comes to your health around the holidays, the bully has usually got it wrong.

The truth is, for most of us, one day of immoderation during Thanksgiving is unlikely to matter in the grand scheme. As such, you would do well to avoid the litany of holiday season health myths out there, many of which seem hell-bent on punishing you for taking a day off:

Like all misinformation, these kinds of medical myths are loud and tend to die hard, but they almost always trade on quick fixes that target the wrong thing.

A 2020 review of 23 observational studies found that most showed significant weight gain during festive periods, averaging roughly a pound and a half. One of the original studies, a landmark NIH study from 2000, found that much of that weight tends to stick around. So the question is not what you ate on Thanksgiving Thursday and how quickly you can “undo the damage,” but rather whether the extended holiday season becomes a two-month period where your healthy routine goes out the window. Some things to look out for on that front:

None of this is a free pass for everyone. If you manage a health condition, you likely have less margin for error, even when talking about a single meal or a drink. Critical factors include timing, sodium intake, alcohol intake, and missed medication. A few non-exhaustive examples:

Whatever condition you’re managing, the move for all of them remains the same – be prepared. Check your plan with your care team before the holiday, not after, and call them if something feels off afterward.

Technically, feeling good on Black Friday starts on Thursday. A short and light walk after a feast can help smooth out post-meal blood sugar – the earlier the better, ideally within an hour of eating. Staying upright for a couple of hours to help with digestion, particularly if you’re prone to reflux, is also a great idea. If you’re on medication, remember to take it on time. And, at the risk of sounding prudish, and this goes for everyone: try to get to bed at a sensible hour.

Above all else, Thanksgiving is a holiday built around gratitude and kindness, so channel your inner Del Griffith and remember to be both grateful and kind, to yourself and to those around you – evidence suggests that’s also good for your health.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

Jeffery Simmons’ Knee Issue Raises Real Fears For Bears Defense

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The Chicago Bears paid a massive price to get Jeffery Simmons, trading away two first-round draft picks for his talent. That deal feels like a gamble now that the star defensive tackle is limited with a knee injury on Wednesday. This is not the kind of news fans wanted to hear just days before a crucial game against the New Orleans Saints. The team needs him healthy to keep their playoff hopes alive, and any doubt about his status is dangerous.

Simmons was supposed to be the anchor of the defense for years to come. On Wednesday, the estimated injury report listed him as limited due to a knee issue instead. According to Mike Garafolo of NFL Network and ESPN, the update arrived during a funky, post-bye schedule. The report did not say how severe the injury is or when it happened. That lack of detail makes the situation feel even more uncertain for the front office.

Simmons has a history of knee trouble that makes this news particularly worrying. He tore his left ACL before the 2019 NFL Draft, which caused him to drop to the 19th overall pick. He then hurt his right knee in December 2023 and missed the rest of that season. Even though he was "limited" rather than out, the pattern of injuries is a red flag for a player the Bears bet so much on.

While Simmons sits on the sideline, other key players are getting back to full strength. Running back Kyle Monangai was a full participant in practice for the first time since returning on Nov. 6. "It feels amazing just to be back out there," Monangai said Monday. Wide receiver Luther Burden III also cleared concussion protocol and practiced fully after a hard hit in the last game.

Burden explained the hit that knocked him out, saying, "Got hit with a helmet,". "(He) didn't get fined. … It's all good. On to the next." These positive updates help the offense, but the defense still faces a major question mark. Cornerback Kyler Gordon remains a nonparticipant with a shoulder injury, leaving the secondary thin if he cannot play.

The team returns to practice Thursday at Halas Hall, where the real test of Simmons' health will happen. Friday's report will determine who plays in the game. The Bears will face the New Orleans Saints at Soldier Field on Sunday, Nov. 22, at 1 p.m.

What a city’s sewage can reveal about the health of the people upstream

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Eight Ohio cities' sewage carried gut bacteria whose mix lined up with local obesity, blood pressure, and stroke rates, a pilot study finds.

During the pandemic, health departments learned to read a city’s sewage for the virus behind COVID-19.

The same water carries something else from every household that flushes into it: the bacteria living in people’s guts.

In eight Ohio cities, the mix of those gut bacteria in the sewage lined up with how the cities scored on chronic disease.

Cities with more of one group of bacteria that produce hydrogen sulfide had higher rates of obesity, high blood pressure, stroke, and several other conditions.

The study rests on 27 samples collected over seven weeks in May and June 2022, and the authors wrote that the links do not show cause.

What they show is that a sample from a treatment plant can carry a readable signal about the health of the people upstream.

Emma Lancaster, then a doctoral student in the College of Public Health at Ohio State University, led the study with Jiyoung Lee, professor and chair of environmental health sciences.

Lancaster is now an environmental health consultant in Boston.

“During the pandemic, the world learned how to use wastewater to track infectious disease. We asked, what else can it tell us?” Lancaster said.

Sewage arriving at a treatment plant is a blend of everything a community has flushed.

One earlier study the authors cite found up to 97% of the microbes in human stool in untreated sewage.

So the water is a pooled sample of a whole population’s gut bacteria, collected without asking anyone to take a test.

Earlier sewage studies elsewhere had tied a community’s bacterial profile to its rates of obesity, high blood pressure, and smoking.

The Ohio team set out to build full gut bacteria profiles for a set of cities and test them against a wide range of health and social measures.

The team collected a liter of untreated wastewater from each of nine treatment plants, on three days in May and June 2022. Two of the plants serve Columbus, the state capital.

The rest serve smaller cities in central and southeastern Ohio: Zanesville, Marysville, Newark, Lancaster, Athens, Circleville, and Marietta.

Together the plants handle sewage from more than 1.3 million people.

The smallest serves about 15,000 and the largest nearly 548,000. So the cities range from small towns to a major metro area.

Four of the cities count as rural under the federal definition the team used, with fewer than 50,000 residents. The other four are urban.

Each sample was processed the same day. The team sequenced a marker gene to identify the bacteria present. Then they kept only those known to live in the human gut, about 37% of what they read.

For the health side, they pulled public estimates for the census tracts inside each plant’s service area, 329 tracts in all.

The CDC’s PLACES database supplied rates of 23 health conditions and behaviors.

The agency’s Social Vulnerability Index supplied the social and economic measures, such as poverty and unemployment.

Each city’s sewage had its own bacterial profile, and the profiles differed enough that the cities could be told apart.

Several of those differences lined up with health. Cities where smoking was more common had more of one large group of bacteria, the Firmicutes.

Cities with less physical activity had more of two genera, Desulfovibrio and Tolumonas.

Desulfovibrio, a genus of bacteria that produce hydrogen sulfide, appeared again and again. Its abundance was associated with eight health measures.

They were high blood pressure, obesity, stroke, mobility disability, chronic kidney disease, coronary heart disease, chronic lung disease, and cancers other than skin cancer.

Studies of patients have found more Desulfovibrio in people with colorectal, stomach, and breast cancers than in healthy people, the authors wrote.

Researchers suspect its hydrogen sulfide may damage cells.

Asthma ran the other way. Cities with more asthma had a less even mix of gut bacteria and less of a genus called Akkermansia, which is widely treated as a marker of a healthy gut.

Social conditions showed up too. Cities with more unemployment had less diverse gut bacteria overall and more Firmicutes.

The four rural cities carried more Desulfovibrio, more Akkermansia, and more of a major group called Bacteroidota than the urban ones.

They also generally had higher rates of the chronic conditions in the dataset.

“In many cases, poor health outcomes are worse in rural areas and to see these distinct indicators of poorer gut microbiome health in these rural areas offers us clues to some of what is driving that,” Lancaster said.

Marysville, an urban city, sat at the healthy end of nearly every measure, with the lowest rates of smoking, depression, unemployment, and poverty among the eight.

“Wastewater offers a unique way to understand the health of whole communities, not just the people who show up in clinics,” Lee said.

Eight cities give eight data points. The authors wrote that their results should be read as exploratory, not as reliable estimates for any one city.

Columbus alone accounts for most of the 1.3 million people, which could tilt comparisons.

The health figures are also modeled estimates for census tracts, not counts of diagnosed patients. And the samples cover seven weeks of one year.

A deeper problem is in the pipes. Desulfovibrio and Acetobacterium live in sewers as well as in guts, and their numbers in wastewater can rise or fall with how long sewage stays in a pipe.

The authors wrote that future studies should record sewer conditions to separate the human signal from the plumbing.

A larger study could test whether the same patterns hold across more cities and over longer periods while accounting for diet, access to health care, and sewer conditions.

Wastewater already tracks viruses such as those behind COVID, flu, and mpox, Lee said.

Tracking a community’s gut bacteria the same way, she said, could eventually show whether a nutrition program is improving its gut health.

The full study was published in the journal Environmental Research.

The Power of Kindness in a World Driven by Probability

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 19, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

"We place life’s bets by countless calculations of probability, conscious and unconscious, only to discover over and over how short they fall of the wildest reaches of the possible, which always includes but exceeds the probable." —Maria Popova

“I’ve been searching for ways to heal myself, and I’ve found that kindness is the best way.” —Lady Gaga

"The question is not: what have I achieved? But rather: who can I be when I am with you? Who can you be when you are with me?" —Nora Bateson

We live in a world, at least in our hectic Western-based culture, that is driven by probability. What is probability? How does it taste, smell, or feel? Does it just linger in our complexity, waiting for a never-achieved form? We calculate within probability both with our awareness and unawareness. It penetrates our educational, economic, political, and medical systems. It assumes that we will automatically accept models of certainty. Yet our problems still exist in the domain of probable content.

Probability is all-powerful and designed to make you believe its outcomes are absolute. It waits for our lives to pass through it with definitive rules to fit all. However, it can never seem to guarantee success. It tries to be the basis of caution, yet it can also be the source of the other side of safety. It can, in many instances, be the origin of violence and discrimination.

Why? Probability measures the likelihood or chance that a specific event will occur. It survives on unresolved paradoxes of this or that, and that misses the yin/yang, messy/beautifulness of life in all its complexity. It can be funny, as in humor, and puzzling with choices, but when not harnessed, it feels separate and fragmented, avoiding the ecological process of how all patterns connect.

Probability evades the wisdom of understanding how love, altruism, and empathy can breed kindness, which some might consider the regulating homeostasis basis of living systems. Anthropologist Gregory Bateson more than hinted at the misunderstanding of probability’s faults by suggesting that absolute outcomes hide how "Human actions involve play, context, and the probability of disaster or risk within a relationship." Probability makes us too busy to savor what happens between relationships, yet when we do, it can help us step back from absolute outcomes and certainty and find improvisational potential. When we do, with encouragement, this can create all kinds of possibilities. Maria Popova, for instance, mentions in the first stanza of her poem "The Wildest Bet Is the Winning Bet":

"You wouldn’t have bet on it,the battered rockorbiting a starfrom the discount binof the universe"

Probability dissolves when you zoom outward with your personal lens and widen your perspective, making room for kindness to be born. Probability, in this case, begins to blend with how we share our possessions. It becomes an advocate for tending to others' needs, which gives ultimate meaning to who we are as humans. It becomes the terrain of kindness, the quality or state of being friendly, generous, considerate, and genuinely concerned for the well-being of others. Through kindness, we can choose to blend with the paradoxes inherent in unchecked probability to see an ecological unity in a manner felt by C.S. Lewis, who wrote in Surprised by Joy: "Only when your whole attention and desire are fixed on something else—whether a distant mountain, or the past, or the gods of Asgard—does the 'thrill' arise." That is the prerequisite for kindness.

Using kindness as an ecological framework supports a sense of order in contrast to probability’s search for finite answers. This demand for finitude is what excludes kindness because it depends on producing outcomes. Kindness, however, is driven by understanding the constraints and interdependency of any living system. This is why it is woven into global popular symbolism and metaphors. It is based on maintaining what may be good for humanity rather than accumulating something. It is forming a kinship based on forgiveness, not to condone past pain but to move forward with altruism and empathy. Kindness emerges in what poet Allen Ginsberg poetically called a state that "stretches thru every probability." It is the beginner's mind of temporary emptiness, which can at first seem conceptually liable, mistaken for sheer nothingness, yet is in fact the improvisational potential for possibilities of kindness.

We must recognize this need for kindness, especially in today's struggle to accept diversity. This underscores a profound reminder, particularly useful as we celebrate Thanksgiving, that the Anglo experience in North America began as a big mistake in probability, as Garrison Keillor reminds us: "...we survived only by the kindness of strangers who had every reason to be cruel." In a quote often attributed to Mark Twain, this type of innate empathy is "...the language which the deaf can hear and the blind can see."

When the dictates of probability, with seemingly good intentions, succumb to the dark side of fragmented content in our educational systems, isolated profit-driven health mandates, and sociopolitical polarization, the antidote is massive acts of kindness. This releases feel-good neurotransmitters like dopamine, serotonin, and endorphins. It activates the brain's reward center, creating euphoria and emotional warmth. It further reduces stress by soothing the vagus nerve, which recharges and rejuvenates when it feels secure.

It is interesting how unchecked probability is the cloak of "science" that constantly tries to quantify or refute the effects of kindness, when it only needs to observe how it strengthens social bonds like a welcome adhesive. Kindness can increase trust and collaboration, and encourage greater equity in the distribution of wealth, security, and opportunity, allowing for our innate interdependency with each other and nature. Another worthwhile attribute is the resulting togetherness that correlates with lower rates of anxiety and depression. Consider this in contrast to the array of physical ailments that can occur with unchecked probability, and it's a choice worth exploring.

Here are some prompts to help you sustain kindness as an antidote to probability. Use them with someone else or in groups. After each response, follow up with at least five “How did that affect you?” questions and ask how you can make a difference. Then discuss using paraphrasing to provide feedback about what was heard.

Bateson, Gregory. "A Theory of Play and Fantasy." In Steps to an Ecology of Mind: Collected Essays in Anthropology, Psychiatry, Evolution, and Epistemology, 177–193. San Francisco: Chandler Publishing Company, 1972.

Planning to Stop Taking a GLP-1? Here’s How to Maintain Weight Loss

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Switching GLP-1 medications or undergoing bariatric surgery may help people maintain greater weight loss than stopping treatment entirely, according to a new study presented at ObesityWeek and published in eClinicalMedicine on November 14.

The Cleveland Clinic study compared five strategies for managing weight after treatment with GLP-1 medications, analyzing data from 20,486 adults with obesity or type 2 diabetes.

People who stop taking GLP-1 medications often regain weight. Researchers examined whether continuing treatment, switching medications, or undergoing bariatric surgery could help limit weight regain or support further weight loss.

The findings showed that people who underwent bariatric surgery or switched between semaglutide (Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Zepbound) experienced the greatest overall weight loss.

The study participants had an average age of 56.7 years, an average weight of 109 kg (240.3 lbs), and an average body mass index (BMI) of 38. All had been taking either semaglutide or tirzepatide for at least 90 days.

The researchers categorized the participants into five groups:

After 2 years, those who underwent bariatric surgery saw an average loss of 28.2% of their body weight or about 31.5 kg (69.4 lbs).

“We increasingly view medications and surgery as complementary tools rather than competing ones,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“For patients with severe obesity (typically a BMI of 35 or higher, or 30+ with metabolic conditions like type 2 diabetes), bariatric surgery remains the most durable, cost-effective intervention available,” he added. “Using a GLP-1 beforehand can safely reduce liver volume and lower surgical risk, while surgery provides the permanent anatomical and hormonal reset needed to maintain that weight loss long-term without relying indefinitely on weekly injections.”

During the same period, those who switched between semaglutide and tirzepatide lost 16% of their body weight, or about 18 kg (39.6 lbs).

Participants who switched from semaglutide to tirzepatide lost 3 kg (6.6 lbs) more than those who continued taking semaglutide. However, participants already taking tirzepatide lost 4.5 kg (9.9 lbs) more by continuing treatment rather than switching to semaglutide.

The other groups experienced lower amounts of weight loss:

After one year, participants with type 2 diabetes experienced HbA1c reductions of 1.76% following bariatric surgery, 1.43% after switching GLP-1 medications, and 1.32% after continuing their original medication, compared with 0.48% among those who discontinued treatment entirely.

After one year, participants with hypertension (high blood pressure) also experienced greater reductions in blood pressure following surgery or switching GLP-1 medications than those who discontinued treatment entirely.

“[This study] reinforces the need for a long term, multi-modal continuum of care rather than treating GLP-1s as a temporary fix,” said Ali.

Ali also recommended the following strategies to help maintain weight loss after stopping a GLP-1 medication:

The findings highlight the importance of having a long-term weight management plan in place before stopping a GLP-1 medication.

Before stopping a GLP-1 medication, talk with a healthcare professional. They can help you explore your options and determine which strategies may be most appropriate for your health needs.

Ali told Healthline that identifying the reason for stopping treatment is an important first step. He noted that people often discontinue GLP-1 medications because of gastrointestinal side effects, supply issues, or loss of insurance coverage rather than because they no longer need treatment.

He said that if side effects are the issue, adjusting the dose or switching to a different agent often solves the problem.

Ali also emphasized that stopping a GLP-1 medication shouldn’t be viewed as a test of willpower. Increased hunger after discontinuing treatment is a biological response, not a personal failure.

“Have a concrete ‘off-ramp’ plan in place with your doctor before taking your last dose, so you have medical, nutritional, or surgical support ready to protect the health gains you worked hard to achieve,” advised Ali.

Opinion: STAT+: I’m a cardiologist. I’m a little puzzled about the blockbuster new study on deprescribing statins

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We ranked the 25 most valuable publicly traded Chinese drugmakers

Khetpal is a preventive cardiologist and assistant professor of medicine at the Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine. He writes The Workup, a STAT column about cardiology.

At the American Heart Association’s recent annual meeting, the overarching theme coming from the center stage, bathed in Pantone red, was one of high-profile neutral findings.

As I listened to the results of the STREAM trial — which found that older adults without a history of heart attacks or strokes who stopped taking statins didn’t have significantly different outcomes from those who continued taking them — I jotted down that the study continued the theme.

But as the Kaplan-Meier curves were cast onto the screen, something felt off: The curves diverged, rather than stayed together. Theoretically, this scenario can happen in noninferiority studies, where researchers are testing whether an intervention offers similar outcomes compared with a standard. De-prescribing statins in this group, by the expectations set by researchers at the beginning of STREAM, was technically noninferior, but nevertheless, still inferior. The patients randomized to stop taking their statin did worse compared with those who continued. Even the trial’s data safety monitoring board suggested that patients who have stopped their statins should talk to their doctors about resuming their medications.

By Autumn Fiester, Aliza Narva, and Stacy C. Smith

A Study of 50,000 Children Reveals the Real Impact of Daily Iron Pills

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A major scientific review involving more than 50,000 children and adolescents reveals that daily iron supplementation can significantly reduce the risk of iron deficiency anemia.

A daily iron supplement can substantially reduce the risk of iron deficiency anemia in children and teenagers, but it does not appear to improve growth or protect against common illnesses. These findings come from a major Cochrane review of 114 studies involving more than 50,000 participants.

Led by researchers at Cornell University’s Joan Klein Jacobs Center for Precision Nutrition and Health, the review examined daily iron supplements taken alone or combined with folic acid and other vitamins and minerals. Many of the studies were conducted in Asia and involved children younger than five.

The strongest evidence showed that iron alone probably reduces the risk of iron deficiency anemia compared with receiving no treatment or a placebo. Combining iron with folic acid probably offers similar protection, although that conclusion was based on just one study. Supplements containing iron alongside other vitamins and minerals may also help. Evidence suggests that iron supplementation can reduce anemia and iron deficiency more broadly, but confidence in several findings remains limited.

Iron is essential for producing hemoglobin, the protein in red blood cells that transports oxygen from the lungs throughout the body. When iron levels become too low, the body cannot produce enough healthy hemoglobin, potentially leading to iron deficiency anemia.

“Iron deficiency remains one of the most common nutritional problems affecting children worldwide,” says first author Dr Kripa Rajagopalan. “Our review shows that daily iron supplements do what they are meant to do, which is reduce iron deficiency anemia, without any measurable impact on the common illnesses parents and health workers worry about.”

Although iron supplements helped prevent iron deficiency anemia, the researchers found no improvement in children’s height or weight relative to others of the same age and sex. Iron alone also had no measurable effect on the occurrence of cough, fever, or respiratory illness.

Adding folic acid to iron made little to no difference in rates of diarrhea or respiratory illness. Results for other supplement combinations were less conclusive, with evidence ranging from moderate to very low certainty.

Daily iron supplementation may cause stool discoloration, but iron alone appears to have little or no effect on vomiting or diarrhea. Its relationship with constipation remains uncertain. When iron was included in supplements containing other vitamins and minerals, vomiting was probably more common than with otherwise identical supplements that lacked iron.

“While the evidence base supports the use of daily iron supplements where needed, some adverse effects do occur. Stool discoloration and, in some formulations, vomiting were more common with iron, and the certainty of evidence for several outcomes, including anemia and iron deficiency themselves, was low or very low,” explains senior author Dr Saurabh Mehta.

Uncertainty about longer-term outcomes has persisted for years. The World Health Organization’s guidelines, last updated in 2016, identified gaps in evidence concerning the long-term effects of iron supplementation in children. The new review brings together a much larger body of research, although the strength of the evidence still varies across outcomes.

Anemia affects an estimated 874 million children and women worldwide. Young children face a particularly heavy burden, with approximately 40% of children younger than five affected in 2019. Iron deficiency accounts for roughly half of these cases.

Preventing anemia is a central objective of the global health strategy for women, children, and adolescents through 2030. Iron supplementation is already widely used to prevent and treat anemia, particularly among adolescents and women in populations where iron deficiency is common.

“These findings give decision-makers a stronger evidence base for guidance on iron supplementation in children and adolescents,” says co-author Dr Julia L. Finkelstein. “The data supports continued use of iron supplementation where iron deficiency is a concern.”

The researchers caution that their findings do not justify giving every child or adolescent a daily iron tablet. Decisions about supplementation should account for individual nutritional needs, local rates of iron deficiency and anemia, and the specific supplement formulation being used.

Reference: “Daily iron supplementation for prevention or treatment of iron‐deficiency anaemia in infants, children, and adolescents” by Kripa Rajagopalan, Laura Hackl, Juan Pablo Peña-Rosas, Maria Nieves Garcia-Casal, Mochammad Rizal, Neel H Mehta, Michael Roudik, Saiful Islam, Meghan M Trumbull-Kennedy, Nabila Reem Khondakar, Juan VA Franco, Julia L Finkelstein and Saurabh Mehta, 17 November 2026, Cochrane Database of Systematic Reviews. DOI: 10.1002/14651858.CD013227.pub2

Office light may throw off the body’s daily rhythm

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In a study of day-active rats, an hour of simulated daylight at noon helped restore activity rhythms weakened by fluorescent office lighting.

People spend about 90% of their time indoors, under ceiling lights that are a small fraction as bright as the sky outside. The body reads that light as a biological signal.

In a rodent that keeps human hours, living under standard fluorescent office light weakened the animal’s daily rhythm of activity.

An hour of simulated daylight at noon each day, roughly analogous to a lunchtime walk outdoors, partly restored the rats’ daily activity rhythm.

In a second experiment, more of the male rats that spent six weeks under fluorescent light showed diabetic-range blood sugar than rats under daylight-strength light.

A smaller share of sperm were moving in the fluorescent-light group, about 19% compared with 45% under simulated daylight.

The study is small, with eight animals in the first experiment and 20 in the second, and all of them were male.

Lily Yan, a professor in the Department of Psychology at Michigan State University, led the work. The lead author is Antony Bum Kim of the Department of Architecture at the University of California, Berkeley.

“It is a biological signal that influences circadian organization and, potentially, a range of downstream health outcomes,” Yan said of light.

The body’s internal clock helps regulate daily patterns, known as circadian rhythms, in sleep, appetite, and much else.

Nearly every lab rodent is awake at night, which makes it a poor stand-in for a person whose body clock runs on daylight.

The Nile grass rat, a small African rodent, is active in the daytime, and Yan’s lab has used it for years to study how light affects the brain.

Over each cage, the team installed a programmable light that can imitate the color mix of many sources. Its daylight setting ran at about 5,600 lux, roughly the level that building designers treat as true daylight.

The office setting imitated a standard fluorescent tube at about 150 lux, the brightness of a typical indoor workspace.

Eight male grass rats lived under four lighting schedules in turn, two weeks each. Sensors logged their movement day and night.

The first schedule was steady daylight from dawn to dusk. The second was daylight that brightened to the same midday peak and faded again, which delivered about half the total light.

The third was fluorescent office light all day. The fourth was office light with one hour of full daylight at noon.

The rats were most active, and most sharply divided between a busy day and a quiet night, under steady daylight.

Under fluorescent light their activity was lower and their rhythm flatter, with its peak shifted later in the day.

The ramped daylight did not match the steady version even though it reached the same peak.

The authors wrote that the total amount of light over the day, more than its brightest moment, seems to set the strength of the rhythm.

Adding the noon hour of daylight to the office schedule raised the strength of the rhythm again, though only partly.

All eight animals showed a burst of activity during that hour. The authors called it a partial rescue.

A separate group of 20 male grass rats lived for six weeks under either steady daylight or fluorescent light, 10 in each group.

The rats’ body weight, belly fat, and average blood sugar did not differ between the groups.

But five of the 20 animals had blood sugar in a range the lab treats as diabetic, and four of the five had lived under fluorescent light.

In a follow-up with a different way of drawing blood, the pattern held. Four of eight rats under fluorescent light crossed that line, compared with one of seven under daylight.

The two groups produced the same number of sperm. Under daylight, about 45% of those sperm were moving. Under fluorescent light, about 19% were.

“Factors affecting health are not limited to drugs, diet and exercise,” Kim said. “The light environments we create in our homes, schools and workplaces are also biological exposures.”

Ten animals per group is a small sample. More rats under fluorescent light crossed the study’s threshold for diabetic-range blood sugar, while average blood sugar was similar in the two groups.

The authors also wrote that blood drawn after euthanasia can read differently, which is why they ran the follow-up.

Every animal was male, so the study says nothing about females. And a grass rat under a programmable lamp is not an office worker with windows, a commute, and a phone.

The authors wrote that the findings support daylight deficiency as a risk factor for diabetes in this animal.

The sperm result is new, they wrote, since earlier work on light and sperm looked at day length rather than light quality.

What remains untested is whether the same shifts happen in people. The authors wrote that the next step is for other groups to repeat the work in grass rats or other day-active animals.

The next question is whether an hour of brighter light at midday could help people who work far from a window maintain a stronger daily rhythm.

The full study was published in the journal Journal of Biological Rhythms.

‘They Can Take My Stage’: Macklemore Teases New Song Benefiting Palestinian Mothers & Children

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

"They can't take my faith/ When love is the call," he raps on "Where's Our Love."

Macklemore is continuing his fight for peace in Gaza with a new song called “Where’s Our Love” that will benefit Palestinian mothers and children.

The rapper announced Wednesday (Nov. 18) that the track would drop on streaming services Friday (Nov. 20), sharing a song and music video teaser. Over stirring, reflective piano and clips of both Palestinian suffering and resilience in Gaza, Macklemore spits, “We circled the sun three times/ Have you heard the olive tree cry?/ What have we done?/ Where’s our love?”

The lyric refers to the three years of violence that have unfolded since the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks, in which terrorist organization Hamas killed approximately 1,200 Israelis. More than 74,000 Palestinians have died in Israel’s ensuing war on Hamas, according to Gaza’s Health Ministry via the Associated Press, and the United Nations has classified Israel’s actions as a genocide, something the country and its ally United States deny.

“All proceeds will go to the Mother of Hind Fund, to support mothers and children in Gaza,” Macklemore announced in his caption.

The musician has been one of the most outspoken artists in the stance that Israel is committing genocide, and incorporates “Free Palestine” demonstrations into his performances. This led to his dismissal from Ed Sheeran’s Loop Tour earlier this year as stadium owners such as Robert Kraft — who owns Gillette Stadium — threatened to cancel their scheduled concert dates unless the British pop star’s team dropped the rapper from the lineup.

Macklemore went on to launch his own Free Palestine Tour, selling out arenas in Dublin, Paris and London. When he tried to keep the trek going in the U.S., however, he alleged that multiple venues had more or less turned him down.

“They can take my stage/ They can taint the cause,” he raps on “Where’s Our Love.” “They can’t take my faith/ When love is the call.”

The “Thrift Shop” artist has previously released music in support of Palestinians, dropping “Hind’s Hall” in 2024 and “F–ked Up” in 2025.

The "shameful" measles data that lost the US its elimination status

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Measles became endemic in the US months ago—and it’s only accelerating.

International health authorities formally announced on Tuesday that the US has lost its measles elimination status, a hard-fought public health triumph won in 2000 after decades-long vaccination efforts. The loss had been widely expected by public health experts for months, and the decision was leaked by the press last week. Still, having the designation officially stripped stings.

After the announcement Tuesday, public health experts, doctors, and political leaders decried the dramatic backslide for the US, calling it “shameful” and a “national embarrassment.” News articles point to the staggering vertical rise of measles cases this year, currently over 5,400 cases, more than any other year since 1991. An explosive measles outbreak in Pennsylvania accounts for over 1,500 of those cases. There have been 10 confirmed deaths across three states, several deaths just in the last week.

But the measles data that caused the US to lose its status—the bits of it that are public at least—paint a darker picture of where the US is in its rematch with measles, the most contagious virus known on Earth. For one thing, almost none of the data from this year—the cases, the explosive Pennsylvania outbreak, the deaths—factored in to the loss. It was lost earlier. What’s happening now is a runaway epidemic.

The US measles elimination status is a designation granted by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), which is a regional division of the World Health Organization. Last week a committee of experts assembled by the PAHO, called the RVC, met to review measles data for countries across the region, including the US. (RVC is short for the Measles, Rubella and Congenital Rubella Syndrome Elimination Regional Monitoring and Re-Verification Commission.)

For their review of the US data, the RVC heard from an independent committee of experts from the US, called the NSC, which presented measles data collected from January 2025 onward. (The NSC is short for the US National Sustainability Committee for the Elimination of Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome.)

According to the PAHO, an elimination status is lost when epidemiological and laboratory data shows that a single viral lineage has spread continuously for at least 12 months within a country. At this point, the virus is considered to be reestablished or endemic. So, to withdraw the US elimination status, the RVC needed to see evidence that a single introduction of a measles virus led to a 12-month-long chain of cases, which requires a lot of data.

The full set of data the NSC presented to the RVC is private for now. The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention needs to review and approve such data for public release, which can be a lengthy process that has not yet occurred.

The Department of Health and Human Services, which oversees the CDC, did not respond to a question from Ars Technica regarding when the data may be released.

But Ars Technica spoke with the chair of the NSC, Noel Brewer, who tried to talk through the pieces of data in the NSC report to the PAHO that he could discuss—these are pieces that have already been made public at some point by others, such as by state health officials or researchers. Brewer said the NSC presented the PAHO with genetic data spanning January 2025 to January 2026, and also epidemiological data that extended to June 2026.

In a press briefing Tuesday, officials for the PAHO made clear that only some of the data presented by NSC factored into its assessment of the US elimination status. The RVC only looked at the reporting period between January 2025 and January 2026, which doesn’t include the massive outbreak ongoing in Pennsylvania, which began in April. “The number of cases in Pennsylvania did not actually contribute to the decision that was made by the Reverification Commission,” Rhonda Sealey-Thomas, PAHO’s assistant director, explicitly told reporters Tuesday. For 2025, the US reported 2,289 cases nationally.

Still, there was no ambiguity about the US losing its status; “multiple lines of data show clearly that the US has sustained transmission of measles,” Brewer told Ars.

Brewer, who is also the Gillings Distinguished Professor in Public Health at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, confirmed that the beginning of the end of US elimination was January 20, 2025, in Texas—that date was included in the letter PAHO sent to the US informing it of the loss of elimination status. That Texas outbreak was declared over on August 18, 2025, after 762 cases were confirmed in the state. But the virus carried on elsewhere and is still simmering.

“Since January 20, 2025, the US, Canada, and Mexico have been passing measles cases back and forth across borders,” Brewer said, and the national data shows multiple, “genetically distinct” measles introductions into the US. “However, there is one genomic lineage that predominates across the cases and ties many of them, the majority of them, together,” he said.

For people closely tracking the US measles status, a key question has been whether the big outbreaks that followed the initial one in Texas were clearly linked. If they were, it would be immediately obvious that the US had lost the elimination status; but if not, and the outbreaks were driven by different virus introductions, the US could have kept its measles-free status based on the technicality of how the status is defined.

Data that might hold the answer to this question has been elusive. The measles strain in Texas, D8-9171, appeared similar to the viruses seen subsequently in big outbreaks in Arizona and Utah. But the strain was also similar to those from Mexico and Canada. Generally, there can be relatively small genetic changes among measles viruses, and genetic data alone is not used by PAHO to evaluate elimination. In June, ProPublica tried to get at the answer itself, conducting its own genetic analysis of measles strains in the US. It concluded that “the measles virus still spreading in Utah as of this May is very closely related to the one that sickened Texans over a year ago.” But the outlet acknowledged this wasn’t a “smoking gun.”

To date, the question lingers. Brewer cannot yet explicitly disclose what the NSC reported to the PAHO about possible links between the Texas outbreak and any of the subsequent large outbreaks in 2025. That includes the one in nearby Utah or one in South Carolina, which began in October 2025 and ultimately tallied 997 cases by the time it was declared over in April 2026.

For its part, the PAHO did not respond to multiple requests for comment from Ars Technica on linkages between the outbreaks.

But in a twist, Brewer noted that the answer to the question might be moot; the lineage that started in Texas on January 20 might not be the only one that has been sustained and could have lost the country its status.  There are “one or two that have gone on for more than 12 months,” he said.

With the designation now formally lost, health and medical experts are coming to grips with the reality that measles is once again endemic in the US. Details of how the US should move forward are up for debate; the US is now required to submit a “corrective action plan” to the PAHO, outlining how the country intends to regain its elimination status. But the fundamental solution is obvious and straightforward: The US needs to increase vaccination rates.

While the virus has been raging in religious communities with low vaccination rates, the entire US has seen slipping vaccination coverage. That makes it easier for the virus to gain a foothold virtually anywhere.

A protective vaccination rate is considered to be 95 percent; according to the latest data, only 92.4 percent of kindergartners were vaccinated with the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine in the 2025–2026 school year. Only 10 states have MMR vaccination rates at or above the 95 percent threshold. Meanwhile, non-medical exemptions from school vaccination requirements have never been higher, at 4 percent. Currently, 24 states have exemption rates at or above 5 percent, meaning that even if they are able to vaccinate every single non-exempt child, they would still not meet the 95 percent target. And this is just the data for young children. The vaccination coverage for adults is another concern, and the data there is scarce. In Pennsylvania’s massive outbreak, 1,055 of the 1,561 cases this year have been in adults ages 18 and older (less than 1 percent of the cases were fully vaccinated).

While some health officials have noted optimistically that 92.4 percent in children is just a few percentage points from the target, the reality of bringing up vaccination rates is more onerous.

PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa stressed the point in the press briefing Tuesday: It’s “not the national average or the average in the state or even the average in a big city, you need to guarantee that in every community, village, you have this vaccination coverage up to 95 percent. This is very important,” Barbosa said.

The point has been demonstrated in the US. In 2019, the US came very close to losing its measles elimination status. Unlike now—when the virus is on a nationwide rampage, with 47 states and jurisdictions reporting cases—in 2019, the US nearly lost its status from a single, large outbreak that spread in New York. Though it didn’t explode on a national level, it took 11 months to stamp out in those undervaccinated communities. In that year, the US reported 1,274 measles cases total.

The situation in the US has only gotten worse, and the outlook is bleak, Brewer warns. “The measles outbreaks in Canada and Mexico are winding down,” he noted. “However, in the US, our measles problem continues to accelerate. There is no end in sight.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

How to Plan Your Holiday Meals in a Year of Unprecedented Recalls

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Following a series of high-profile food recalls and outbreaks this year, many Americans may be concerned about keeping their families safe from foodborne illness during the holidays.

In fact, according to a recent national survey by Orlando Health, around 60% of U.S. adults say they’re more concerned about foodborne illness this year than in previous years.

Although several major foodborne illness outbreaks have ended and their associated recalls are no longer active, food safety remains an important concern. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that 48 million people in the United States experience foodborne illness each year.

However, there are simple steps you can take to reduce the risk of foodborne illness when preparing holiday meals and storing leftovers.

While there were several food recalls in the United States over the summer, two of the largest involved Salmonella linked to jalapeños and Cyclospora linked to lettuce.

These outbreaks prompted widespread recalls and raised concerns about the safety of certain foods, particularly fresh produce.

Although health officials have since declared the major outbreaks over, some consumers may still be hesitant to purchase foods associated with previous recalls.

Even with this declaration, you may still feel nervous about purchasing certain foods, but experts say you don’t need to worry.

“Here in the United States, we have the safest food supply in the world. The recent recalls show that our food safety system is working,” said William Schaffner, MD, Professor, Division of Infectious Diseases at Vanderbilt University.

Michelle Routhenstein, preventive cardiology dietitian at Entirely Nourished, told Healthline that past recalls do not mean entire food categories need to be avoided. The important thing is whether the specific product you have matches a current recall, based on details such as brand, lot number, package size, and expiration date.

“You should enjoy familiar foods, including properly cooked poultry, washed fruits and vegetables, and pasteurized dairy products,” she said. “The key is checking relevant recalls and practicing safe food handling.”

“Before shopping or cooking, review FDA and USDA (U.S. Department of Agriculture) recall information and compare any relevant notices with the labels on your ingredients,” said Routhenstein.

Check the pantry and freezer, too: something purchased months ago may still be affected.

“Stay alert to any news reports about food recalls and use good food preparation practices. Those simple actions should keep your holidays happy and safe,” said Schaffner.

Consumers can sign up for food recall alerts through the FDA or visit FoodSafety.gov for recall information from both the FDA and USDA.

Schaffner emphasized the importance of following safe food-handling practices when preparing everyday meals and holiday gatherings.

“Remember the longstanding advice: keep food hot or cold. Avoid long times at room temperature,” he said.

Routhenstein advised focusing on four basics: clean, separate, cook, and chill.

Schaffner noted the importance of refrigerating leftovers promptly rather than leaving them at room temperature for extended periods. He recommends clearing the table and refrigerating leftovers soon after the meal ends.

Routhenstein reiterated that you should refrigerate leftovers within 2 hours rather than leaving them out to cool.

Divide turkey into smaller portions and transfer sides and gravy into shallow containers to help them cool quickly, she advised.

Leftovers can typically be stored in the refrigerator for 3–4 days. If you don’t plan to eat them within that time, consider freezing them.

The important thing is to keep an eye out for any new recalls, practice food safety, and enjoy your holiday meals!

Ex-Brewer Jake Bauers says the city of Boston figured into his decision to sign with the Red Sox

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

BOSTON (AP) — Newly signed Boston Red Sox slugger Jake Bauers, fresh off his World Series triumph with the Milwaukee Brewers, says he's grateful for the chance to play in a city that he and his wife, Lauren, had always circled on the schedule during his major league career.

"Boston's one of those cities where we've always been excited to go," Bauers said as the Red Sox introduced him on Tuesday, noting that his mother-in-law has run the Boston Marathon. "Now that we get the chance to put down roots here, call this place home, we're extremely grateful."

The Red Sox finalized a four-year, $58 million deal with Bauers that rewards the first baseman for a breakout season that included being named MVP of the National League Championship Series.

Bauers won't be playing first base full time for the Red Sox, who have Willson Contreras at the position.

Red Sox president of baseball operations Craig Breslow said the team coveted Bauers' bat, and that he'll serve as a designated hitter who can play first base or the outfield as needed.

Bauers "has enough power to hit the ball out of any park but is a particularly great fit here for Fenway (Park) given his ability to hit the ball in the air to left field," Breslow said. "So, we expect a huge season and a lot of doubles incoming."

Bauers never had more than 372 at-bats and never hit more than 12 homers in his first six seasons. He considered retiring before emerging as a power threat to help lead the Brewers to the title.

The left-handed-hitting Bauers, 31, set career highs with a .266 batting average, .874 OPS, 26 home runs and 86 RBIs.

He was a postseason star, including in Milwaukee's NLCS sweep of the Los Angeles Dodgers. He hit a go-ahead homer off two-time Cy Young Award winner Blake Snell to end an 11-pitch at-bat in a 6-2 victory in Game 3 at Los Angeles.

"There's not doubt that he had many options and the fact that he chose to further his career with us is special and we're excited," Breslow said.

Added Bauers: "Winning a championship was an addicting feeling, and so I think over the next four years, the goal is to bring a World Series back to Boston."

Bauers played for Tampa Bay, Cleveland, Seattle and the New York Yankees before finding a home with Milwaukee in 2024. His .235 batting average in 2025 was his career high before his 2026 breakout.

Bauers was drafted in 2013 by the San Diego Padres. He hit .202 for the Yankees in 2023 and batted .199 the following season with Milwaukee. He considered ending his playing career before learning his wife was pregnant. He says he returned because the family needed health insurance.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/pharma/overtons-fda-nomination-has-been-pulled-white-house" hreflang="en">Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House</a>

📅 2026-11-17  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Heidi Overton’s FDA nomination pulled by White House" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The White House pulled the nomination for Heidi Overton, M.D., Ph.D., to lead the FDA just hours before the Senate was scheduled to vote on her confirmation.

The move hardly comes as a surprise after Overton’s confirmation hearing before the Senate Health, Education, Labor and Pensions (HELP) Committee drew contention.

The news was first reported by Axios on Tuesday evening. The White House confirmed the news to Fierce the following morning.

In September, the HELP committee questioned Overton in a hearing marked by tightrope-walking on issues like vaccines, abortions, flavored vapes and whether or not President Donald Trump is capable of being wrong.

Overton conceded multiple times during the hearing that vaccines approved by the FDA, as well as the “abortion pill” mifepristone, are safe and effective. But she stopped short of admitting that Trump has been wrong when dispensing medical information out of line with what scientific evidence shows.

Overton’s nomination faced mounting pushback and uncertainty from senators ahead of a planned HELP Committee vote on Nov. 18. Fierce reached out to Sen. Ed Markey, the veteran Democratic lawmaker from Massachusetts, ahead of the vote, and he told Fierce that he would “vote against her nomination, and I urge my colleagues to do the same.”

“The FDA needs a leader who will stand up for science and for our kids’ safety, not one who stood by while both were undermined,” he added.

Republican Sen. Bill Cassidy of Louisiana, who chairs the Senate health committee, and other Republicans had also raised questions about Overton's nomination.

As support for Overton looked thin, Republicans were discussing Sen. Roger Marshall, a Kansas Republican, as a potential replacement, Semafor reported today. Health Secretary Kennedy reportedly met with Marshall in recent days to discuss filling the role, according to Politico, but the White House told the publication that it is not considering him.

Robert Steinbrook, M.D., health research group director for Public Citizen, commented today urging the HELP committee to reject Overton’s nomination: “The FDA needs a strong commissioner who stands for science and the integrity of the agency,” Steinbrook said. “Overton is the wrong choice for FDA Commissioner and has done nothing to dispel the well-founded reasons to oppose her candidacy.”

Analysts from Citi shared that the White House’s decision to pull the nomination has a negative impact on biopharma, “extending uncertainty around permanent agency leadership.”

“We view the withdrawal as a modest sentiment negative, but not a material near-term change to the regulatory environment facing most drug and biologics sponsors,” the analysts said in the note.

The withdrawal of Overton's nomination leaves a gap at an FDA that has already faced significant upheaval. The White House only shifted its focus to Overton in August, after the agency had spent three months without a permanent leader.

Since former FDA commissioner Marty Makary, M.D., departed the agency in May, Kyle Diamantas, who hails from the agency’s food regulation division, has been at the helm in an interim capacity.
