Polish activists face opposition as they make abortion more accessible

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Abortion laws in Poland are very restrictive. Activists there have found a new way to help women wanting to terminate a pregnancy: the "Abortomat," a locker that dispenses abortion pills. But they face fierce opposition.

Women's rights activists in Poland have opened what they say is the world's first delivery locker supplying abortion pills in a country with one of Europe's most restrictive abortion laws.

Known as the "Abortomat," the locker is in a building opposite the Polish parliament, in what activists say is a clear message to politicians like Donald Tusk. The Polish prime minister pledged to introduce abortion on demand up to the 12th week of pregnancy before the 2023 parliamentary election, but his coalition has been unable to agree on a common position.

Only two European Union member states, Poland and Malta, do not allow abortion on request.

In Poland, abortion is only allowed when the pregnancy is the result of a crime — rape or incest — or when it endangers the health or life of the woman.

Justyna Wydrzynska, an activist from a nonprofit organization called Abortion Dream Team, showed DW the "Abortomat" in a small room behind a tasseled portiere at Abotak, Poland's first abortion center.

Inside, the pink lighting is subdued. Glitter tassels, baubles and spotlights make it feel a bit like a disco, she said.

The "Abortomat," which opened in late September, has 22 pink lockers. To open one, a woman receives a PIN code from the organization Women Help Women after completing a medical questionnaire, which is then checked by a doctor.

Parcel lockers have sprung up like mushrooms after rain in Poland in recent years. Even the smallest villages have them.

"When you walk around Poland, vending machines are everywhere. This is our culture. People in the US have Amazon, we have vending machines for everything," said Wydrzynska.

Women in Poland who have an abortion are not criminalized. But anyone who helps them terminate a pregnancy risks up to three years in prison.

In 2023, Wydrzynska was the first activist to be convicted in Poland of helping a woman to have an abortion by sending her pills. The conviction was later overturned, and the court ordered a retrial.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Inside each compartment at the "Abortomat" is a package that normally contains two drugs, mifepristone and misoprostol, usually taken up to the 14th week of pregnancy.

Because mifepristone is not registered in Poland, the drug must be ordered from abroad where it is legal. Information on how to do this is not easy to come by, which is where Abortion Dream Team and Abotak come in. They help women find information about accessing medication, which is sent from abroad by Women Help Women, or help them to travel abroad for a termination.

Wydrzynska said she believes the "Abortomat" does not break the law because it is a machine, not a person, that is delivering the pills, although she admits she is not sure.

Dr. Sabrina Mana-Walasek, a reproductive rights lawyer representing Wydrzynska, said the legal environment for abortion pills is "complicated."

She said that although mifepristone is not registered in Poland, it is registered in the European Union and, as EU citizens, Polish women can order it and take it.

"If a woman orders pills for herself and pays for them herself, it's not a crime," she said.

The ultra-Catholic legal group Ordo Iuris disagrees and has notified the prosecutor's office that the activists are committing a crime.

"In the case of an 'Abortomat,' the actions enabling a woman to have an abortion are theoretically performed by the woman herself using the device, but the reality is that this machine is operated by people — they send the code required to open the compartment containing abortion pills, they order and procure the pills, and they restock the compartments," said Katarzyna Gesiak, director of the Ordo Iuris Center for Medical Law and Bioethics.

Wydrzynska said anti-abortion activists regularly demonstrate outside Abotak, using horns and loudspeakers to generate loud noise to annoy the center's residential neighbors and pressure the landlord to revoke their rental agreement.

At one demonstration, a woman holding an image of the Virgin Mary told DW she believes abortion "is the work of Satan."

"Abortion is evil, it's demonic, it's against the fifth Commandment, 'Thou shalt not kill,'" she said.

Earlier this year, Diana, a 25-year-old Ukrainian woman living in Warsaw, used abortion pills during her sixth week of pregnancy before the "Abortomat" started up. She got the pills using information provided by Abortion Dream Team.

Diana told DW that she has never wanted to have children.

"I was expecting something like a headache, stomachache, maybe vomiting, but I had nothing like that. It was just like a normal period. I didn't feel any pain. It was really easy for me," she said.

"I felt relief because I knew that it was the right choice for me. I knew that I can live my life," she added.

Another Abortion Dream Team activist, Natalia Broniarczyk, said they help about 130 women daily in Poland. About 125 of these women use pills that cost about €75 ($83), while the remainder travel to a clinic abroad.

In contrast, just 851 abortions were performed in Polish hospitals last year.

Broniarczyk is concerned about the rising support for right-wing parties ahead of next year's parliamentary election, but she's also furious at the inactivity of liberals in Tusk's coalition.

"I'm mad at the liberals because for three years, they did nothing to protect us. They lost three years and now, when we're close to an election, they are fighting for our voices and respect. People are more progressive than politicians," she said.

After taking office in 2023, Tusk's coalition, which includes left-wing and conservative partners, were unable to agree a common position and their separate bills failed to obtain parliamentary approval in 2024.

Afterwards, Tusk said abortion on request would not be possible during the current parliamentary term.

In October, however, two bills were resubmitted to the Sejm, the lower house of parliament, and were narrowly approved.

One reinstates severe irreversible fetal defects as a justification for a legal termination. This justification had been ruled illegal by the Constitutional Court in 2020 when the previous right-wing Law and Justice (PiS)-led government was in power.

The 2020 ruling led to tens of thousands of people, mostly women, taking part in street protests in towns and cities across the country, some of the largest since communism fell in 1989.

Lawmakers also approved a bill partially decriminalizing anyone who helps a woman terminate her pregnancy.

According to the results of a recent poll conducted by SW Research for Onet.pl, almost 61% of respondents said they want the country's right-wing president, Karol Nawrocki, who is backed by PiS, to sign into law the bill on the fetal defect justification.

Nawrocki opposes abortion, and both Wydrzynska and Broniarczyk think he will use his power of veto to block the bills, preventing them from becoming law.

These are some of the strongest 3D printer filaments for durable parts

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Stronger filament doesn't automatically mean a stronger print, and the toughest materials can require better hardware too.

If you're looking for one winner, it's not exactly convenient that a 3D print can be "strong" in a few different ways. A rigid bracket should resist bending, while a part that gets knocked around may last longer if it can flex and recover instead of breaking. In this article, we're going to hone in on rigidity and the types of materials that work well for structural parts.

Carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon (PA-CF) and polycarbonate (PC) are two of the strongest practical materials for the kind of filament-based 3D printers that are accessible to the average maker. Both materials can handle demanding functions, higher temperatures and considerably rougher treatment than everyday polylactic acid (PLA) or glycol-modified polyethylene terephthalate (PETG).

Sure, there are stronger, more expensive specialty materials that could vie for the title of heavyweight filament champ. But let's stick to the pros and cons of carbon-fiber nylon versus polycarbonate, so you can decide which material packs the most punch for your projects.

Carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon (PA-CF) combines nylon with short carbon fibers — hence the name. Nylon already handles demanding functional parts well, and the added fibers make the finished material stiffer and more resistant to bending under load. How resistant, you ask?

Bambu Lab's dry-state test puts its carbon-fiber-reinforced nylon 6 (PA6-CF) at 151 megapascals (MPa) of bending strength. Its heat-deflection temperature is 186 degrees Celsius, meaning it resisted deformation under a specified load until around that temperature. Those figures help explain why carbon-fiber nylon is used for brackets, fixtures and other working parts instead of for items that simply sit pretty on a shelf.

A strong filament, however, does not necessarily make a strong print. How you orient the object on the print bed, plus wall thickness, infill and part shape, can significantly change how much abuse the project can take. Bambu Lab does not claim the 151 MPa result applies to every finished print. Pricing is another factor to note, as Polymaker currently lists a 1kg spool of its Fiberon PA6-CF20 at $52.99 at time of writing

There is some hardware housekeeping to contemplate too. Carbon fibers are abrasive, so your old faithful brass nozzle may not last long. Bambu recommends a hardened steel nozzle, requires PA6-CF to be dried before use, and lists a nozzle temperature of 260 to 290 degrees Celsius with an enclosed printer when using this material.

The National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health (NIOSH) says fused-filament desktop printers can emit respiratory irritants and ultrafine particles, so good ventilation or filtration is important to consider.

Unlike carbon-fiber composites, polycarbonate (PC) produces strong parts without chopped fibers. It's a stiff engineering plastic with good heat resistance, making it suitable for housings, brackets and other parts that need to withstand more than light use.

Once again, you'll need to consider your equipment and workspace before deciding if PC is the best and strongest filament for you. Similar to PA-CF, Polymaker recommends a nozzle temperature of 250 to 270 degrees Celsius, a 90 to 105 degrees Celsius heated bed, and an enclosed printer. The company says large, dense parts require a heated chamber, which helps reduce uneven cooling and warping. Good workplace ventilation or exhaust is recommended.

When it comes to strength, print direction matters, too. Polymaker measured 69.1 MPa of tensile strength across the printed layers and 52.8 MPa between them. PC's bending strength clocked in at 106 MPa, a drop compared to the 151 MPa logged for PA-CF.

The big difference with PC versus PA-CF is the price. Polymaker lists PolyLite PC at $29.99 for a 1kg spool and PolyLite PLA at $19.99at time of writing. The bigger pain point could be your printer, since lower-cost models may struggle to maintain the temperatures PC prefers.

Does Menopausal Hormone Therapy Lower Dementia Risk? Timing and Genetics May Hold the Answer

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Women who have longer reproductive life spans or use menopausal hormone therapy (MHT) may have better brain health as they age, according to a new study.

The research, published November 4 in Neurology, found that longer lifetime exposure to estrogen was associated with fewer signs of damage to small blood vessels in the brain.

The findings could offer new insights into the relationship between estrogen and brain aging, particularly as women account for approximately two-thirds of Alzheimer’s disease cases worldwide.

Researchers also found that certain associations between hormone therapy and brain health differed depending on whether women carried a genetic variant associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer’s disease.

However, the relationship between estrogen and brain health is complex. While the findings suggest that longer estrogen exposure may be associated with healthier brain aging, they do not establish that hormone therapy can prevent Alzheimer’s disease or dementia.

The researchers drew data from the Gothenburg H70-1944 Birth Cohort, a longitudinal study of 1,203 individuals born in 1944 who resided in Gothenburg, Sweden.

The final cohort included 352 cisgender women, with an average age of 70, who had no neurological disorders and reliable self-reported data on their estrogen exposure.

Individual estrogen exposure was measured through structured interviews. It included:

The researchers used this information as a proxy for lifetime estrogen exposure, though the study didn’t measure actual hormone levels.

Participants were also given a brain MRI at baseline and then again at a 5-year follow-up.

The researchers found that those with a longer reproductive span had a lower risk of small vessel disease (SVD). Participants with a longer reproductive life span had an 8% lower risk of having two or more signs of SVD. However, later menopause, rather than earlier menstruation, appeared to be the main driver of this association.

Those who used MHT also tended to have fewer signs of SVD and better microstructural integrity in the white matter.

In individuals who carried the APOE-ε4 variant, which increases a person’s risk of Alzheimer’s disease, MHT was associated with greater cortical thickness in brain regions vulnerable to Alzheimer’s disease. However, there was no association for those without this variant.

The researchers noted that longer estrogen exposure was associated with stable cortical thickness and white matter trajectories over time.

Dung Trinh, MD, internist at MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that estrogen has several important effects on the brain that may help support cognitive health.

It influences communication between brain cells, supports the brain’s ability to use glucose for energy, helps regulate neurotransmitters, and plays a role in maintaining healthy blood vessels and reducing inflammation and oxidative stress.

“As estrogen levels decline during menopause, some of these protective effects may diminish. Replacing estrogen closer to the menopause transition could potentially help preserve some of these functions during a vulnerable period for brain health,” he said. “While these findings are encouraging, MHT has not been proven to prevent Alzheimer’s disease or dementia.”

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed and noted, “Starting menopausal hormone therapy earlier in the menopause transition may help minimize some changes by supporting the brain during this period of hormonal fluctuation.”

“There is growing interest in whether early estrogen therapy may also help reduce the long-term risk of Alzheimer’s disease, although more research is needed before we can establish that benefit,” she added.

Ross told Healthline that this research reinforces the importance of recognizing menopause as more than a reproductive or hormonal transition.

“We know that women are disproportionately affected by dementia and Alzheimer’s disease, and some women experience noticeable cognitive changes beginning in perimenopause,” she said. “If future research confirms that starting hormone therapy earlier can help protect long-term cognitive health, it could influence how we approach menopause treatment and encourage more proactive conversations between women and their healthcare providers.”

“The goal should be to individualize treatment based on a woman’s symptoms, medical history, and overall health rather than taking a one-size-fits-all approach,” Ross added.

Trinh agreed that an individualized approach to menopause management is important.

“This does not mean that every woman should start hormone therapy to prevent dementia. Instead, it highlights the importance of understanding which women might benefit, when treatment should begin, and which formulations are most appropriate,” he said. “Factors such as age, time since menopause, cardiovascular risk, metabolic health, family history, and potentially genetic factors may all help guide future recommendations.”

“We still need stronger clinical evidence before changing current prescribing guidelines specifically for dementia prevention,” Trinh added.

Day 34: The Decision to Walk as a Family

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The premature grief of leaving the experience of the Camino lingered from the night before. Those emotions also surfaced in my dreams. So in the way I processed best, I began walking in the dark.

There were so many emotions for so many aspects of my life.

And even though the emotions were hard, I was thankful to be feeling them. Typically, tears didn’t come easy to me. So I was grateful that I could express anything at all.

I stopped at the crest of a hill because of the giant dog sitting outside the bar. The affectionate beast was the only reason I decided to stop and eat breakfast.

Another tortilla. Another cafe con leche.

Just as I hoped, my brothers arrived soon after and their presence helped shed some of the weight.

A pilgrim we knew came up and told us that someone stole his wallet while he was sleeping in the albergue last night. As a necessary precaution, I asked my one brother, who always forgot his things, if he had his. He did not.

Turns out, he left it at the restaurant where he ate breakfast, 8 kilometers away. The three of us called many places to sort out the problem and eventually coordinated a taxi to take him back to the location to look.

The American and the Argentinian tried to be the calm in the Italian storm, reassuring him that the Camino provides and that his wallet would be there. He didn’t believe us.

Then, fifteen minutes after he left in the taxi, he sent us a picture of his wallet.

He eventually caught up to me, cheerful and relieved. I took the opportunity to share something I’d been feeling.

What I wanted, more than anything, was to spend my birthday with my brothers. If we arrived in Santiago on my birthday, it’d be a dream come true, but I also knew we would have to do more kilometers each day, and I didn’t want to make them change their plans for me.

I told this to him. And he said that even though he started the Camino alone, he wanted to end it all together, just like me.

It was set then. Only one more brother to talk to.

As we walked, we found a tree carving of a Neanderthal. On the sign, it was written in three languages: Spanish, English, and Galician. Like the Basque Country, Galicia has its own language, though the two have very different linguistic roots.

My Italian brother and I arrived at the albergue the three of us reserved together in the morning, Pensión García en Triacastela.

We chose this place simply because it was €2 more than staying in the giant albergue with everyone else. And it was worth it.

We grabbed a late lunch at the restaurant below. A local dressed in neon construction clothes approached our table with a bucket. At first I was confused, but then he dropped a handful of roasted chestnuts on our table. With a big smile, he walked away.

They were still warm, and I knew they were picked from the forest nearby because of the multitude of nuts I’d walked past.

Our Argentinian brother arrived shortly after, along with several other pilgrims we knew.

One from Brazil, whom I met at the halfway mark, began telling us about the legend Jesús—the owner of the albergue we stayed at in Villafranca del Bierzo. The Brazilian told us that Jesús knows Paulo Coelho so well that Coelho actually began writing The Pilgrimage in the building where I slept. This was pivotal because that was the book that brought me to the Camino.

I struggled at that albergue because I wanted to have a meaningful conversation with the legend, but felt my Spanish wasn’t good enough. I blurted this out. The Brazilian reassured me and responded,

“I’m proud of you. I want to be like you.”

Many people on the Camino didn’t attempt to speak the language, but truthfully, most days I spoke more Spanish than English. I could feel myself improving, and the reflections from the people around me helped validate that feeling.

My Italian brother and I presented our idea to our Argentinian brother about getting to Santiago for my birthday. He agreed! Suddenly, all the grief I’d been feeling about leaving them went out the window.

On a walk to the small town supermarket, my Argentinian brother told me that he had an idea for him and me. In that conversation, he also said,

Both brothers had now said this to me one-on-one at some point during the Camino. The dynamic that was formed with these guys surpassed what I could’ve ever imagined for my walk.

Because we weren’t staying in the main albergue in town, there were only five of us in the whole place. The bunkhouse connected to the kitchen with sliding doors, and in that eating area we had the rare treasure of a TV with YouTube. Someone put on an American classic music playlist, and we all danced and had the most beautiful time.

The boys and I would now begin our journey to Santiago for real.

We each set our alarms for an early start.

She fell in love with long walks in nature during her Appalachian Trail 2022 thruhike where she dove into finding herself through the space that the trail provided. Since completing her flip-flop at Springer Mountain, she has crafted her lifestyle to travel full-time and serve others within her passion of holistic health. This walk is another step into the unknown to embark on "A Saunter of Spirit" as she raises awareness for mental health through raising money for Hike For Mental Health. Hailey Bieber Says She's Been Diagnosed With PMDD: What Are the Signs, Symptoms?

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Hailey Bieber is sharing her experience with premenstrual dysphoric disorder (PMDD), a condition she says has fueled her growing interest in women’s health research.

The Rhode Beauty founder recently announced a $5 million, three-year partnership with the Foundation for Women’s Health in an interview with Women’s Wear Daily (WWD).

“As a woman, I feel frustrated by the lack of research, the lack of awareness, the lack of certain conversation around it,” Bieber told WWD.

Motherhood has also shaped her perspective. Bieber welcomed her son, Jack Blues, with her husband, Justin Bieber, in 2024, describing the experience as a “really intense and special journey.”

She also shared that she “struggled” with PMDD after giving birth, noting that there’s “not a lot of conversation or research around it.”

“When we can identify with each other and have conversations relating to each other and talking about the things we’re dealing with and struggling with, I do think it makes it feel like less of a lonely journey,” Bieber said.

PMDD is a complexcondition that causes severe physical and psychological symptoms that typically occur in the days or weeks leading up to menstruation.

“What distinguishes PMDD from the more common PMS (premenstrual syndrome) is the severity of the symptoms and how significantly they can interfere with daily functioning,” Sari Eitches, MD, dual board certified physician and medical advisor to Terra Mare, told Healthline.

“Another defining feature is the timing: symptoms typically worsen in the week or two before a period, resolve within a few days after menstruation begins, and are minimal or absent during the rest of the cycle,” she said.

Eitches also noted that PMDD is a chronic condition that can affect women at any stage of their reproductive lives, from adolescence through perimenopause, as long as they are having menstrual cycles.

“It is caused by a sensitivity to cyclic hormonal and neurotransmitter fluctuations, so [it] can occur in anyone with a menstrual cycle, though stages of hormonal fluctuation- including postpartum, weaning breastfeeding, and perimenopause- can be a trigger,” she added.

According to research published in 2024, approximately 1.6% of women and girls meet the strict diagnostic criteria for PMDD, equivalent to an estimated 31 million people worldwide.

A higher percentage of 3.2% have a provisional diagnosis, meaning a healthcare professional may believe a person has PMDD, but symptoms have not been measured for a sustained amount of time to meet the criteria for a confirmed diagnosis.

Eitches said that women with a personal or family history of depression or anxiety may be more prone to developing PMDD, as are women with a family history of PMDD itself.

“PMDD is understood to be caused by a combination of heritable biological sensitivities and environmental triggers. These can include a history of trauma, chronic stress, and other chronic medical conditions,” she noted.

Eitches also said that you should consider seeing a doctor if your symptoms are severe enough that they’re interfering with your ability to go to work or school, maintain your relationships, or participate in your life in the same way that you do during the other weeks of the month.

To diagnose PMDD, a healthcare professional will review your medical history and symptoms and may perform a physical exam. They will typically ask you to track your symptoms over at least two menstrual cycles.

A PMDD diagnosis generally requires at least five symptoms, including at least one mood-related symptom, that occur before menstruation, improve shortly after a period begins, and significantly interfere with daily life.

Treatment options for PMDD include prescription medications and over-the-counter treatments that may help relieve certain symptoms, such as:

Eitches noted that cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) can also help manage symptoms.

Lifestyle changes may also help manage PMDD symptoms, including practicing relaxation techniques, eating a balanced diet, limiting sugary and salty foods, and getting regular physical activity.

Eitches noted that getting enough high quality sleep and resting on a consistent schedule is also important.

“I also recommend focusing on a whole-foods-based diet, limiting added sugar and caffeine, and avoiding cigarette smoking,” she said.

Starship flight 15 powers to orbit—setting up a possible catch on Saturday

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

One way or another, the company will get some more data on Saturday.

STARBASE, Texas—Starship lifted off right on schedule Friday from South Texas, making its 15th test flight in more than three years.

The largely successful launch took place in the early evening; as the massive rocket rose into the atmosphere, the Sun sank toward the western horizon. Conditions were ideal for the launch, with the atmospheric winds so calm the rocket’s white plume did not dissipate for many minutes. The weather in South Texas is not always comfortable; summers are long and hot. Friday evening was perfect at Starbase.

The first stage ascended toward space flawlessly, releasing the upper stage above the Gulf of Mexico. From there, the Starship upper stage ignited its Raptor engines and powered its way into a nominal orbit. This was the second time SpaceX has flown Starship into low-Earth orbit.

When it came time for the first stage to return to the ground, it dropped like a rock. However, due to an unspecified issue, the vehicle was commanded to divert into the Gulf rather than return to the Starbase facility to be caught by the launch tower. It created a fantastically powerful explosion, even for observers more than 10 miles away.

The primary aim of Friday’s launch was the deployment of 26 Starlink V3 satellites from a narrow door at the base of the spacecraft. This occurred about 40 minutes into Starship’s flight, and all 26 satellites—which have solar panels that stretch 60 meters end to end when fully deployed—safely made it into orbit, where they will now use onboard propulsion to raise their altitude.

This means that Starship is now an operational, orbital rocket. It is the largest and most powerful machine humans have ever launched into space. The challenge for the company now is to make Starship reusable and dramatically increase its flight rate.

In that sense, the first part of Friday’s mission was not a success. SpaceX had hoped to return the first-stage booster, refurbish it, and fly it again. The company has previously landed and reflown Starship boosters but has yet to do so with the latest iteration of the vehicle, version 3.

However, SpaceX still has a chance to make history on Saturday with the potential return of the Starship upper stage. SpaceX has never attempted to catch the vehicle’s upper stage.

Doing so will require Starship to pass a series of tests in orbit and subsequently during its return to Earth to ensure it is fully healthy. This is critical because Starship will be passing over parts of Mexico and the United States before returning to Starbase, and SpaceX does not want to endanger people on the ground. Additionally, it does not want to risk damage to its launch towers.

If Starship passes these health checks, it would land at about 3:15 pm local time in Texas (21:15 UTC). If it does not, the company has earlier options to de-orbit the vehicle into an ocean.

Successfully returning Starship’s upper stage would be historic, as well as significant for SpaceX’s efforts to reuse the massive rocket. It would also give the company valuable information about the vehicle’s performance during reentry.

One of the biggest hurdles left in Starship’s development is the vehicle’s heat shield, which must protect the upper stage during its return through Earth’s atmosphere. More than that, SpaceX aims to make Starship rapidly reusable, and to do this, Starship’s tiles must be light and resilient.

SpaceX has been experimenting with tiles since the beginning of the Starship program, trying to find the right combination of low mass, heat resistance, and durability so the tiles require minimal refurbishment between launches. For this flight, SpaceX said it is applying learnings from the previous test flight in September, including additional retention mechanisms on tiles in areas deemed to be at the highest risk of falling off during ascent and addressing areas with a flow path for plasma to get behind the tiles.

Another problem on recent flights has been heat incursion into the small gaps between the edges of the tiles. According to SpaceX, this Starship has a large portion of the heat shield equipped with a curved tile design that has shown the ability to reduce heating in the gaps between tiles.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-delays-enforcement-medicaid-work-requirements-six-months" hreflang="en">Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is giving states an optional six-month grace period for enforcing new Medicaid work requirements, which were due to go into effect January 1.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) announced Friday an optional "hold harmless" period through June 30, 2027, to give states a transition period to further modernize systems, improve processes and streamline the applicant and beneficiary experience.

During the "hold harmless" period, states will still assess whether Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries meet the work requirements or qualify for an exclusion, but otherwise eligible people will not lose or be denied coverage solely because they fail to meet the requirement, CMS said.

CMS says the transition is intended to give states time to improve eligibility systems, automate verification, train workers and improve the beneficiary experience.

"States have made real progress, and they’ve been clear with us about what it takes to get this right for beneficiaries—at scale and for the long term," Dan Brillman, Deputy Administrator and Director of the Center for Medicaid and CHIP Services, said in a statement. "If the state can use data to show that someone is working or qualifies for an exclusion, that person should not have to track down paperwork to prove it. And when someone needs support, they should know exactly what to do, when to do it, and where to go. That’s how we build a Medicaid program that works better for people, connects them to work, education, and training opportunities, and supports paths to prosperity."

During the six-month enforcement pause, CMS encourages states to use existing data to automatically verify qualifying activities and exemptions before requesting additional information from beneficiaries.

States are also encouraged to simplify notices and make the process easy to navigate so beneficiaries understand what the state has already verified, what information is still needed and how to respond or get help. States should also test eligibility systems end-to-end, particularly how the systems handle missing, outdated or conflicting information, to resolve operational issues and minimize inappropriate coverage disruptions.

CMS also called on states to coordinate with community partners to connect applicants and beneficiaries who need assistance with opportunities for employment, education, job training and volunteering.

The work requirements were established as part of last summer's massive H.R. 1 legislation, or the One Big Beautiful Bill Act. Under the mandate, certain Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries ages 19 to 64 must meet a statutory work requirement, unless excluded. There are exemptions in place for the medically frail, disabled and others who are unable to meet the mandates.

CMS’ June 2026 interim final rule outlines qualifying activities, including 80 hours per month of work, community service or participation in a work program; enrollment in an educational program at least half-time; a combination of qualifying activities totaling at least 80 hours per month; or meeting the specified monthly income threshold.

State Medicaid administrators, health insurers, physicians and hospitals have pushed back on the Medicaid work requirements, citing concerns about financial and operational challenges, a spike in uncompensated care and the rule's strict "medical frailty" exemptions.

Industry groups argue the requirements impose onerous documentation burdens. The Alliance of Community Health Plans said that the final rule adds documentation requirements and tightens criteria for exemptions to the requirements, which will make it harder for individuals who need coverage to secure exemptions they may qualify for.

The organization also noted that most individuals enrolled in Medicaid are already working or in school.

Jennifer Tolbert, deputy director of the program on Medicaid and the uninsured at KFF, said in a quick take post back in June that "the rule adopts a restrictive definition of medical frailty that differs from states’ early expectations."

"Given the complexity of the provisions in the rule, states will likely face significant challenges in operationalizing the requirements in the next six months," Tolbert wrote. "With the short implementation timeline, states were already making implementation decisions that may not align with the rule."

Studies estimate that between 5 million and 10 million people could lose Medicaid coverage in 2028 following the rollout of new work requirements.

The Trump administration's tight definitions of medical frailty and documentation burdens have triggered multiple lawsuits from states, medical organizations and advocacy groups. A coalition of 26 states sued the Trump administration over its rollout of Medicaid work requirements. arguing that key provisions could cause eligible people to lose coverage and disrupt states’ implementation efforts. The states argue CMS’ new definition of who qualifies as “medically frail” is too restrictive and could make it harder for people with serious health conditions to receive exemptions.

In October, a Massachusetts judge sided with the states and rejected the Trump administration's definition of "medical frailty" for critical exemptions.

A group of physician groups, individual Medicaid enrollees and the city of Columbus also sued the federal government to challenge the work requirements.

State Medicaid agencies have warned that the accelerated IT and administrative build required by January 1, 2027, is putting severe pressure on local welfare offices and safety-net hospital operating margins, as Senior Writer Anastassia Gliadkovskaya reported.

Scientists discover a hidden danger of viral infections that vaccines may prevent

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Vaccines may do more than protect people from the immediate effects of a viral infection. New research suggests they could also help prevent lasting damage to the immune system that remains even after the virus has disappeared.

Scientists at Pompeu Fabra University discovered that mice recovering from an acute viral infection experienced prolonged problems with their immune defenses. Although the animals had successfully fought off the virus, their immune systems remained impaired, making it harder to respond to new threats. Remarkably, mice vaccinated before infection did not develop these problems, suggesting that vaccination may provide an additional layer of protection beyond preventing illness.

Most people think of common viral infections, such as colds or stomach viruses, as temporary illnesses. A virus enters the body, multiplies, causes symptoms, and is eventually brought under control by the immune system. Once symptoms disappear, recovery is generally considered complete. The immune response also creates protection against future encounters with the same virus.

But what if the immune system continues to experience harmful effects long after the infection appears to be over?

Viral Infections Can Leave Lasting Immune Damage

Researchers from Pompeu Fabra University's Infection Biology Laboratory investigated this possibility in a study published in Cell Death & Disease.

Using mice with acute viral infections, the team examined what happened to their immune systems after the animals had recovered. They found that eliminating the virus did not necessarily restore normal immune function. Instead, the infection left behind changes that persisted well beyond the initial illness.

According to Andreas Meyerhans, an ICREA research professor at UPF, who led the research, "there is a general perception that an acute viral infection is a relatively minor and transient event: once the virus is controlled and the symptoms have resolved, the body has fully recovered and is back to normal. But this view may underestimate consequences that are not immediately visible and can persist long after the infection itself has resolved."

The researchers traced these lingering effects to significant changes in the spleen, an organ that plays an important role in coordinating immune defenses. As the immune system fought to eliminate the virus, the spleen developed fibrosis, a condition involving the buildup of scar-like tissue. The arrangement and positioning of immune cells within the organ also became disrupted.

These structural changes interfered with the interactions between immune cells that are necessary to activate T cells. These specialized cells are essential to adaptive immunity because they help recognize and destroy infected cells.

As a result, the mice remained less capable of launching effective immune responses when exposed to new challenges. Even though the original infection had been brought under control, their immune defenses continued to function poorly.

"The infection can be controlled, but that does not necessarily mean that the host has fully recovered," says Dr. Paula Cebollada Rica, first author of the study. "Our findings show that the immune-related consequences of an acute viral infection can persist beyond the period in which the virus is detectable, leaving the animals in an immunocompromised state."

Vaccination Prevented Lasting Immune System Damage

After identifying these long-term effects, the scientists investigated whether they could be avoided. They focused on vaccination, which prepares the immune system to recognize and fight a pathogen before an infection occurs.

The results revealed a striking difference between vaccinated and unvaccinated animals. Mice that received vaccination before infection did not develop the spleen damage or immune dysfunction seen in the other group.

Their spleens maintained their normal structure, and their T cells continued to function at levels comparable to those of mice that had never been infected.

This finding suggests that vaccination can protect the immune system itself from some of the harmful consequences of fighting a viral infection. In the mouse model, vaccination not only protected against the disease caused by the virus but also preserved the animals' ability to respond to future immune challenges.

"Vaccinated animals did not experience T-cell dysfunction or splenic alterations after recovering from an acute viral infection. This means that vaccination not only prevented the disease resulting from the acute infection but also preserved the immune system's ability to cope with new challenges," Cebollada Rica explains.

A Hidden Benefit of Vaccines With Public Health Implications

The findings broaden the potential benefits of vaccination by highlighting a form of protection that may not be immediately apparent. Beyond reducing the immediate effects of an infection, vaccines may also help prevent persistent changes that weaken immune defenses.

Although these results were obtained in mice and do not establish that the same effects occur in humans, they raise important questions about how viral infections influence long-term immune health and how vaccination might help protect it.

The research also comes at a time when vaccination policies and infectious disease prevention efforts are increasingly influenced by political decisions. The researchers emphasize the importance of scientific evidence in understanding the full consequences of infections, particularly when policy choices can undermine coordinated efforts to prevent infectious diseases.

Understanding the broader benefits of vaccination could therefore influence not only medical care but also how public health officials explain the value of vaccines to the public.

"The benefits of vaccination highlighted by this research are important not only for healthcare, but also for how we communicate the value of vaccination to the population at large," Meyerhans states. "It is important that people are aware of the broader protection that vaccination can provide and continue to trust in vaccines as one of the most effective tools to prevent diseases. Put simply, vaccines provide strong health benefits at a relatively low cost to society."

Stopping statins after 70 may be safer than expected, but there's a catch

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A major new study suggests that many adults age 70 and older who have never experienced a heart attack or stroke may be able to stop taking statins without substantially increasing their short-term cardiovascular risks. However, the findings also point to a possible increase in risk over a longer period, making the decision more complicated than it initially appears.

The independent, preliminary research was presented at the American Heart Association's Scientific Sessions 2026, held November 6 through 9 in Chicago. The annual conference brings together cardiovascular researchers and healthcare professionals from around the world to share scientific discoveries, emerging evidence, and advances in clinical care.

An estimated 25% of American adults have elevated levels of low-density lipoprotein-cholesterol (LDL-C), commonly called "bad cholesterol." When too much LDL cholesterol accumulates in the arteries, it can restrict blood flow and contribute to heart attacks and strokes. Statins are widely prescribed to lower cholesterol and reduce these risks, including in people who have never experienced a cardiovascular event. This preventive approach remains a central part of the American Heart Association's 2026 joint guideline on dyslipidemia.

Do Older Adults Need to Keep Taking Statins?

Although statins are an established treatment for reducing cardiovascular risk, questions remain about how much benefit they provide when people reach their 70s and beyond, particularly if they have never had a heart attack or stroke.

"While the Association's recent joint guideline recommends lifestyle and potentially medication for people with high LDL-C at younger ages to reduce the risk of heart attack and stroke, recent studies question the health benefits of continuing statins in the later decades of life," said study author Rita F. Redberg, M.D., M.Sc., FAHA, professor of cardiology and the Araxe Vilensky Endowed Chair in Cardiology at the University of California, San Francisco School of Medicine. "A few previous studies assessed the safety of discontinuing statins for primary prevention at age 70 and later, and they found no difference in mortality. One study suggested quality of life improvements after stopping statins. Whether it is safe to discontinue statins to prevent a first CVD event in the elderly remained unclear and the question we explored in our study."

To investigate, researchers enrolled nearly 1,900 adults aged 70 and older who were taking statins specifically to prevent a first cardiovascular event. Every participant also had at least two chronic medical conditions, such as a history of cancer or lung disease.

Participants were divided into two groups. One group stopped taking statins, while the other continued their prescribed treatment. Researchers tracked their health for approximately two years, with additional follow-up extending as long as four years. They examined rates of heart attack, stroke, and death, along with changes in quality of life, muscle pain, falls, and muscle wasting (known as sarcopenia).

What Happened When Older Adults Stopped Statins?

The initial findings were largely reassuring. After approximately two years, participants who stopped taking statins did not experience a statistically significant increase in heart attacks, strokes, or deaths compared with those who remained on the medications.

Stopping treatment also did not produce measurable improvements in the other outcomes researchers examined. Quality of life, muscle discomfort, falls, and muscle wasting were similar in both groups.

There was, however, a small difference in cardiovascular outcomes. Over the first two years, the results indicated approximately one additional cardiovascular event for every 40 people who discontinued statins compared with those who continued treatment.

The longer follow-up revealed a more concerning pattern. Over a period of up to four years, 12.7% of participants who stopped statins experienced a heart attack, stroke, or death, compared with 9.2% of those who kept taking the medications.

Longer Follow-Up Reveals a Potential Risk

Redberg explained that the higher rate of serious outcomes during the four-year follow-up represents a new finding in research on statin discontinuation among older adults. She suggested that the difference may have emerged because this trial included more participants and tracked them for a longer period than earlier studies.

These results complicate the question of whether older adults should continue cholesterol treatment indefinitely. While the findings suggest that stopping statins may be a reasonable option for many people in this age group, they also indicate that remaining on treatment could offer some protection over time.

"This small, increased risk of cardiovascular events means that one cannot make a general recommendation about stopping statins in this patient population," she said. "There should be shared decision-making between the patient and the healthcare professional to know if it is appropriate for an individual to discontinue the medication. In general, however, discontinuation is safe for most people who are in this age group and without a previous CVD event."

The trial involved 1,880 participants aged 70 and older (average age of 77; 53% men, and 47% women), all of whom had at least two chronic health conditions. Individuals with established cardiovascular disease or a previous cardiovascular event were not eligible to participate.

Researchers conducted the study between November 2021 and April 2026 across 24 medical centers in Switzerland, France, and the Netherlands. Of the participants, 938 were assigned to discontinue statins, while 942 were assigned to continue taking their medications.

Throughout the trial and subsequent follow-up, researchers recorded heart attacks, acute ischemic strokes, and deaths from any cause. Participants also completed surveys assessing their overall quality of life, muscle pain, falls, and sarcopenia.

One limitation was that some participants did not remain in their originally assigned groups. Some people instructed to stop statins eventually resumed treatment, while others assigned to continue taking statins stopped using them. Altogether, approximately 14% of participants switched from their assigned treatment approach during the study.

To determine whether these changes affected the findings, the researchers performed several additional statistical analyses. The results remained unchanged.

Taken together, the findings suggest that discontinuing statins may be an option for many adults over 70 who have not previously experienced cardiovascular disease, especially when considering outcomes over the first two years. However, the higher rate of heart attacks, strokes, and deaths during longer follow-up underscores why stopping treatment should be an individualized decision made with a healthcare professional rather than a general recommendation for all older adults.

Materials provided by American Heart Association. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Want to Turn Back the Clock? These 10 Diets May Help You Age Better

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

You’ve got way more options than just the Mediterranean diet.

When healthy aging is the goal, it makes sense to try everything you can to make it happen. That means doing your best to exercise regularly, keep up with doctor's appointments, and eat a well-balanced diet. You have lots of options when it comes to the last one—but it can be hard to know which of the many popular eating plans is right for you. Thankfully, new research has helped narrow the choices.

The study, which was published in Nature Communications, analyzed several different diets to see how they impacted biological aging. (Biological aging is the age of your cells, while your chronological age is how old you are.) Popular eating plans like the Mediterranean diet and the DASH (Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension) diet made the cut, of course, but several lesser-known diets did too. Here’s what to know.

Meet the experts: Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers; Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet; and Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian.

For the study, researchers analyzed data from 6,470 participants who were 30 and older. Researchers looked at some of the participants' biomarkers that correlated with biological age and compared that with information about their diets.

After analyzing the data, researchers found that healthier eating in general was linked with slower biological aging, no matter which diet people chose. Each specific eating plan offered an edge with certain perks, though. Case in point: The DASH diet (which is specifically meant to stop hypertension) had more cardiovascular benefits for biological aging, while the MIND diet (which combines the Mediterranean and DASH diets for brain health) offered cognitive perks. But researchers discovered that there were a lot of shared biological aging benefits between the different types of diets.

These are the 10 diets flagged as being good for biological aging:

All of these diets are slightly different, but there are a few common threads. "They generally emphasize fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and other nutrient-dense foods," says Keri Gans, RD, author of The Small Change Diet.

The diets also stress the importance of minimally processed foods, while limiting refined carbs, added sugars, and processed meats, according to Sonya Angelone, RD, PhD, a former spokeswoman for the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics and a San Francisco-based dietitian. "The recommended foods provide fiber, antioxidants, polyphenols, vitamins, minerals, and healthy fats that support metabolic health and help protect cells from oxidative stress and chronic inflammation," she says. Oxidative stress and chronic inflammation are both linked to a higher risk of disease, Angelone points out.

These diets also support the heart, brain, and digestive systems, says Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers. "All of these things factor into the aging process and somebody's quality of life as they age," she says. "When you're eating to support these different organ systems, you support your longevity and lower your risk of disease."

There are a few things to take away from the findings, according to dietitians. One is that there isn't a single eating plan that's superior to others when it comes to longevity. "It's the overall quality of what we eat consistently that matters most, rather than following a particular diet or avoiding individual foods," Gans says.

While there are plenty of diet plans out there that claim they can help you live longer, Angelone stresses this: "Healthy aging doesn’t have to include expensive foods, supplements, or restrictive diets."

Finally, the study underscores the power of nutrition in keeping you healthy. "Our genes are not our destiny," Angelone says. "We can change the expression of our genes—genomics—with nutrition and lifestyle habits."

Supporting healthy aging with the way you eat means giving your body the fuel it requires to function well, Angelone says. "Healthy aging isn’t about finding a perfect diet or miracle food or supplement," she says. "It is about consistently providing foods that supply nutrients needed by our bodies to function optimally, repair cellular damage, and protect themselves. This will impact how we age."

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What a Missed Deadline Taught Me About Switching Off

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

I missed a deadline last week. The piece you’re reading was supposed to be filed before I headed out on a long weekend to Italy for my wife’s birthday.

The main problem was I simply ran out of time to write the piece. An urgent stakeholder ask out of the blue, a couple of unanticipated work fire drills, and a long-standing evening social commitment earlier in the week made for a perfect storm, and something had to give.

The other problem and consequence was that I carried a chunk of guilt into the break.

It caught up with me at 6 am on the first morning, when I found myself drafting this column in my head. I should have been dozing peacefully. I continued to ruminate on my lengthy to-do list at work deep into day one as we headed from Venice into the Dolomites. My body was in Italy, but my brain was clearly still at my desk.

Switching off, particularly from work, is hard for most of us, and especially in an age of ubiquitous screens and wall-to-wall information competing for our attention. For me, that always-on reality plays a major role in the difficulties I have disconnecting from the daily grind.

I’m fortunate to love my job, and if I’m candid, I probably take a little too much of my identity from it. I hold on tight, and the phone in my pocket is an ever-present enabler.

A meme that has stuck with me over the years goes something along the lines of being an adult is staring at medium screens all day only to come home and stare at a little screen while a big screen is on in the background. The irony is not lost on me that I came across this rather withering take on modern life while scrolling on said little screen.

Regardless of the role the screen plays, psychologists who study employee well-being describe what I was missing for the early part of the weekend as “psychological detachment.” A state where you are genuinely able to not think about work while away from it. The science suggests it’s an important thing to work on.

A 2025 meta-analysis of 32 studies found that vacationing has a strong effect on well-being, and that the effect lasts longer than earlier research suggested. A second, smaller analysis of eight studies points to detaching from work, along with physical activity, as among the most helpful things to do while you’re off. In another study that tracked teachers during a two-week break, exhaustion, anxiety, and low mood all declined, while those who kept ruminating about work recovered less.

Whatever the science says, I know that for me the act of stepping away from work and being disciplined about not checking back in while I’m away is critical for my mental health and for staving off burnout. Getting out in nature and hiking or cycling or swimming keeps me present. It resets and recharges my batteries, and it tops up my creativity in a way that I find benefits what I am then able to bring back to the office.

Sure enough, as we drove deeper into the mountains on a crisp, blue-sky day, I could feel the golden autumnal hues and majestic rock formations drawing me in, closer to an elemental feeling of calm and happiness. That sense of equilibrium stayed with me all weekend, and while I’d be lying if I said my mind didn’t occasionally drift to my desk, it was fleeting, with the knowledge that all the things would be waiting for me when I got back. Just like this piece was.

With Thanksgiving on the horizon, and the holidays coming thick and fast thereafter, I’ll leave you with this bit of hard-earned advice: if your job is deeply entwined with your life and part of who you are, as mine is, time away can sometimes feel like a small act of disloyalty. It isn’t. In fact, it’s probably the most important thing you can do to remain faithful to your work ethic, and to yourself.

And if you’ll be working over the holidays, or you’re not working right now, or you don’t have the benefit of someone on hand to carry some of the load for you (shout out to all the Grannies out there), or your vacation looks very different from mine, that’s all okay. Find the pockets where and when you can: an evening, a walk, an hour in front of a screen, or better yet a book, and let work wait. Above all else, be kind to yourself. That’s how you really give yourself a break.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

“For The First Time In A While, I Feel Joy” & 59 Other Anonymous Mom Confessions

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Hope, grief, happiness, burnout — this week’s confessions have it all.

Is that... dare we say it... hope in the air?! This week’s anonymous mom confessions came in like a tidal wave. Literally, we’ve never seen so many 🌊 emojis in our inbox. Because, yes, moms wanted to talk about the midterms. Hundreds of them! Hope, relief, crying in the kitchen, feeling like they can breathe for the first time in years... along with a few who weren’t celebrating at all. We couldn’t even print a fraction of it, so please know, if you submitted one, you were in extremely plentiful company.

But what also really struck us was the sentiment underneath: So many moms feel like they’re living in the in-between.

That tracks for the midterm results, obviously. Will we actually see change once the new class gets sworn in this January? Meanwhile, families are still struggling with inflation and high gas prices and just generally being tired of being broke. This sentiment also surfaced in confessions from moms at crossroads in their relationships, whether it’s the precipice of divorce or considering starting a spicy affair. Moms are feeling it across the board, really, from parenting (the tween years are hitting some of you hard) to health (perimenopause purgatory strikes again).

One mom kind of hit the nail on the head: Her personal life and finances are pretty much in shambles, and also, for the first time in a long time, she feels joy. Both. At once. That’s where a lot of you seem to be right now.

So there’s hope, and there’s the other thing, and most weeks they’re just two wings on the same bird. If that sounds familiar, you’ll likely find a lot of validation in this week’s roundup.

In reversal, American Cancer Society urges prostate cancer tests for all men

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Men aged 50–69 and at average risk should get PSA screening every two years.

In a significant update to its guidelines for prostate cancer screening, the American Cancer Society is now recommending that all men get routinely screened for prostate cancer with a PSA test.

Men at average risk should get a PSA test every two years from age 50 to 69, according to the new guidelines released Thursday.

Men considered at high risk due to race and family history should start testing earlier, at 45, and most are recommended to get tested every year. Specifically, yearly testing starting at age 45 is recommended for all Black men, who have higher rates of prostate cancer. Non-Black men should also get yearly testing starting at 45 if a close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer before age 65, or if more than one of their relatives has had prostate cancer. On the other hand, if only one close relative was diagnosed with prostate cancer and it was diagnosed at age 65 or older, non-Black men in this case should start PSA screening at 45, but are recommended to get testing only every two years.

Men ages 70 to 74 should consult their doctor on continuing screening, while those 75 and up are not recommended to get screened.

The new guidance departs from ACS’s previous iteration, published in 2010, which made no universal recommendations for routine screening. Men at age 50 were recommended to use shared-decision making with their doctors on whether or not to get tested. The recommendation was similar to that from the US Preventive Services Task Force, a federal panel that regularly evaluates evidence and makes recommendations on such preventive medical care. (The USPSTF is currently working on an update to its prostate cancer screening recommendation, though it should be noted that the panel has recently been overhauled by anti-vaccine Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and new members are reportedly rife with conflicts of interest.)

In announcing the guidelines today, the ACS says its updates are based on new evidence since 2010 that shows screening saves lives, and that advances in case management can avoid the concerning harms from overdiagnosis and overtreatment. For many men with prostate cancer, their cancer will never grow or cause harm in their lifetime, while treatment can run the risk of erectile dysfunction and urinary incontinence.

In the new guidance, the ACS adds guardrails, firstly advising all men to be informed about the risks and benefits. If a PSA test comes back high—defined as 3 ng/mL or higher for men under 70 and 6.5 ng/mL or higher for those 70 and up—the ACS recommends repeating the test before taking any next steps.

If repeat testing is still high, then men should get either a prostate MRI or a validated biomarker test to assess risk. Only if there is a high suspicion of cancer—such as having a very high PSA test result—should men jump to a prostate biopsy.

For men diagnosed with prostate cancer, active surveillance should be the initial management strategy for men whose cancer appears to be at low risk of growing or spreading.

“By catching aggressive cancers with a simple PSA blood test, you open the door to highly effective treatments that protect your future,” Dr. Shanthi Sivendran, interim chief patient officer for the ACS, said in a statement. “Meanwhile, lower-risk findings can be safely managed through careful, active surveillance.”

Prostate cancer is one of the most common cancers in men and the second leading cause of cancer death in US men, the ACS notes. Nearly 334,00 men are estimated to be diagnosed with prostate cancer in 2026, and it’s estimated that over 36,320 will die from the disease.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Missing Teen Found Alive After 6 Days Alone in Australian Bushland

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A 17-year-old boy was found alive in the Australian wilderness and has been reunited with his mother after going missing for 6 days.

The boy went missing on Friday, November 6 after his mother dropped him and a friend off to go camping. William had reportedly walked a short distance away to retrieve more camping equipment and did not return, and the friend was unable to find him despite searching the area.

An extensive search was launched, and William was ultimately located when an aerial search unit spotted a tackle box in the bushland. Honing in on the area, they soon spotted William lying near a creek nearby and were able to retrieve him. After an emotional reunion between William and his mother, he was brought to the hospital for monitoring.

“Amazingly, William is in good spirits and for the most part, in good health,” Victoria police said in a press release.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-pauses-hospice-home-health-medicare-enrollments-fraud-crackdown" hreflang="en">CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS extends moratorium on new Medicare enrollments for hospice, home health agencies" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is extending its moratorium on new Medicare enrollments from hospice and home health agencies.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said Thursday that it will extend the moratorium by six more months as part of its efforts to combat fraud, waste and abuse in the Medicare program.

CMS said that the initial moratorium window has allowed the agency "to conduct targeted investigations and deploy advanced data analytics" to identify potential cases of fraud. An extension was deemed necessary to continue that effort, as the "circumstances warranting the imposition of the moratoria have not yet abated."

The agency said that hospice and home health have long been considered high risk areas for fraud.

“We’ve made real progress over the past six months, but fraud in hospice and home health persists and our work isn’t finished,” said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. “Medicare beneficiaries deserve protection from these bad actors, and American taxpayers need to know the programs they rely on aren’t being bled dry by fraudsters.”

Like the original moratorium, the extension applies nationwide. However, the extension will allow currently enrolled hospice providers or home health agencies to add new practice locations to their existing enrollment in Medicare. Providers currently enrolled in Medicare will not be impacted and can continue to provide services to beneficiaries.

The Trump administration has issued a six-month moratorium on hospice and home health agencies enrolling in Medicare as part of its efforts to combat fraud.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said in an announcement on Wednesday morning that the "data-driven" decision targets a key source of fraud activity. It follows a similar announcement made earlier this year of a moratorium on durable medical equipment, prosthetics, orthotics and supplies companies.

During the six-month window, CMS said it will focus on identifying and investigating bad actors and deploying advanced analytics that help accelerate the removal of hospice and home health providers that are likely perpetrators of fraud.

CMS added that recent action under the broader White House fraud push has suspended payments to 773 hospice providers and 23 home health agencies in the Los Angeles area, totaling about $70 million in funds.

“We’ve seen systemic and deeply troubling fraud in the hospice and home health space, with bad actors exploiting some of our most vulnerable Medicare patients and stealing money from the American taxpayer,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in the agency's press release. “Today we’re shutting the door on fraud—preventing new bad actors from entering Medicare while we aggressively identify, investigate and remove those already exploiting them."

"This is about protecting patients, restoring integrity and safeguarding taxpayer dollars," Oz said.

CMS said that the moratorium will not affect providers currently enrolled in Medicare, and they can continue to offer key services to members. It will apply, however, to new applicants for participation and "certain changes in majority ownership," which CMS said can be used to "obscure control by bad actors."

The moratoria will also prevent suspected fraudsters from evading punishment by moving across state lines, CMS said.

The announcement, the agency said, builds on a number of steps CMS has taken of late to address fraud in hospice and home healthcare, including increased oversight in states with elevated risk of fraud, such as Arizona, California, Georgia, Ohio, Nevada and Texas.

CMS is also conducting hospice site visits across the country to monitor operations and potentially identify suspicious behavior. In addition, it expanded a demonstration across several states—Florida, Illinois, Oklahoma, Ohio, North Carolina and Texas—that allowed for pre- and post-claim reviews of submissions from HHAs, which may stop improper payments before they're made.

The National Alliance for Care at Home, in a statement responding to the news, said it welcomes the administration's scrutiny and appreciates that "providers will still be able to conduct face-to-face recertification visits via telehealth during the enrollment moratorium, which will help avoid unnecessary care disruptions for patients and families." Still, it said the action raises concerns over access to care in high-demand areas and urged CMS to focus its efforts on "high-fraud markets" or actors without catching the rest of the sector in its net.

“CMS must use data-driven, risk-based program integrity measures and focus resources on boots-on-the-ground surveys and enforcement of existing oversight mechanisms that root out the blatantly bad actors without potentially limiting patient access to care or punishing high-quality providers operating in good faith," Alliance CEO Jennifer Sheets said in the statement.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/no-surprises-act-idr-claims-cost-employer-plans-additional-84b-last-year-study-finds" hreflang="en">No Surprises Act IDR claims cost employer plans an additional $8.4B last year, study finds</a>

📅 2026-11-12  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
No Surprises Act IDR claims cost employer plans an additional $8.4B last year, study finds" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The No Surprises Act's independent dispute resolution process cost employer plans $8.39 billion in 2025, according to a new analysis.

Researchers at the Employee Benefit Research Institute combed federal IDR data and found that these additional costs equal about 63 cents per $100 spent by employer-based insurance plans. The total includes $4.73 billion in costs incurred in the first half of the year as well as $3.66 billion in the second half of 2025.

Even when EBRI analysts used different thresholds to exclude outlier awards, such as costs that were unusually high, they still landed on an estimate of at least $8.02 billion, per the study.

The researchers note that this data doesn't necessarily paint the full picture, as it only encompasses cases that proceeded to IDR. The study doesn't include information on claims that were settled outside of arbitration, administrative costs, any changes in networks or provider rates or the cost associated with patient protections.

The study calculates additional cost by taking the IDR award amounts and subtracting the plan's initial payment and any cost-sharing from the patient.

"These new data provide a clearer picture of what health plans owe for out-of-network services beyond their initial payments, after accounting for patient cost sharing,” said Paul Fronstin, Ph.D., director of Health Benefits Research at EBRI, in the announcement. “Separating those amounts from arbitration awards allows us to measure additional plan obligations more directly."

The study also doesn't project the impact of IDR on premiums, and the researchers said that having greater insight into the administrative expenses and other ancillary costs would be necessary to understand how the No Surprises Act's processes may impact patients' costs directly.

The IDR processes have gained increasing scrutiny from health plans and plan sponsors as the number of disputes, and their associated payouts, rise. When a claim reaches the arbitration phase, the results overwhelming favor providers, as they won in 85% of disputes last year.

An analysis from Georgetown University, released in August, estimated that total costs associated with IDR reached $22 billion in 2025. There were 2.6 million disputes initiated in 2025; by comparison, federal estimates projected about 22,000 cases annually reaching the arbitration phase.

Providers have pushed back on criticism of IDR, arguing that the root cause is payers undercutting them during the opening negotiation phase by setting low qualifying payment amounts for claims.
